\begin{document}
\maketitle

\begin{abstract}
Let $F:\bits^n\to\bits^n$ have a Boolean circuit of size $s$, and put
$A=s+n^3$ and $B=A+\sqrt{n^3 2^nA}$.  Every fixed iterate $F^T$ has a
bounded-fan-in circuit of size $O(B)$, independently of the magnitude of
$T\geq0$, without assuming injectivity.  The construction uses sparse
checkpoints and sorting-based lookup, with every access and arithmetic gate
charged.  The circuit-size bound is nonuniform; constructing its hardwired
data can require work exponential in the state width.

For even $n$, an explicit linear-size permutation has almost all powers of
complexity $\Omega(2^{n/2}/n)$ under a uniform exponent modulo its order.
This matches the upper bound's exponential scale, but identifies no explicit
hard power; hard exponents necessarily have exponentially many binary digits.
The upper-bound construction also gives $O(B)$-gate cycle labels, transient
depth, pair-input reachability, exact first hitting times, and complete visit
descriptors, together with input-time and shared batched versions.  Exact
target-distance summaries have a local correctness equation, yielding
compact safety invariants and termination ranks with coNP verification.
Under SETH, general deterministic uniform analysis cannot save a fixed fraction of the
state-width exponent, even when the requested powered map has a tiny circuit.

Structured transitions admit stronger bounds.  A permutation differing from
a counter at $r$ states has $O(n(r+1))$-gate signed fixed powers and navigation,
and $O(n(r+m+1))$-gate iteration for an $m$-bit input time.  Its orbit chart
can be constructed in polynomial time from an explicit edit list.  An
autonomous $q$-bit controller with a $p$-bit affine payload gives
$O(2^q(q+p^2))$-gate fixed powers.  Lean checks these core circuit bounds;
further extensions state their individual formalization boundaries.
The checkpoint and block
methods have established antecedents, and historical priority of these
circuit formulations remains unresolved.
\end{abstract}

\noindent\textbf{Keywords.} Boolean circuit complexity; function iteration;
functional graphs; nonuniform computation; sorting networks; permutation
inversion; one-way functions.

\section{Introduction}\label{sec:intro}

A simple transition rule can describe a computation of enormous duration.
How complicated can its final-state map be as a Boolean function of the
initial state?  We study this question for a fixed map
$F:\bits^n\to\bits^n$.  The computational resource is the total number of
Boolean gates, rather than the number of evaluations of $F$, the number of
memory probes, or the time of a random-access algorithm.

Let $N=2^n$ and suppose that $F$ has a circuit of size $s$.  Directly
unrolling $T$ applications gives a circuit of size at most $Ts$ for $F^T$.
Alternatively, hardwiring a complete truth table and testing the input
against all $N$ states gives an $O(nN)$-gate circuit, independently of $T$.
The latter bound ignores the possibility that $F$ itself is much simpler
than an arbitrary map on its state space.  Our main result exploits that
possibility.

\begin{theorem}[Fixed-power tradeoff]\label{thm:main}
Let $n\geq 1$, let $F:\bits^n\to\bits^n$ satisfy $\CC(F)\leq s$, and put
$N=2^n$ and $A=s+n^3$.  For every integer $T\geq 0$ and every integer
$1\leq L\leq N$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:tradeoff}
  \CC(F^T)
  =O\!\left(Ls+n^3\left(L+\frac{N}{L}\right)\right).
\end{equation}
In particular,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:optimized}
  \CC(F^T)
  =O\!\left(A+\sqrt{n^3NA}\right).
\end{equation}
The implicit constants are absolute.  The circuit may depend on $F$ and
$T$, but the size bound does not depend on the bit length of $T$.
\end{theorem}

Here and throughout, circuits use the full fan-in-two Boolean basis and
have unrestricted fan-out.  The results also hold over the basis
$\{\mathsf{AND},\mathsf{OR},\mathsf{NOT}\}$ with a constant-factor change.
Circuit size refers to the whole multi-output circuit, not to a separate
size bound for each output bit.

\begin{corollary}\label[corollary]{cor:poly}
If $\CC(F_n)=n^{O(1)}$, then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:half}
  \sup_{T\geq 0}\CC(F_n^T)\leq 2^{n/2+O(\log n)}.
\end{equation}
The constant hidden in $O(\log n)$ may depend on the polynomial bound for
$\CC(F_n)$, but not on $T$.
\end{corollary}

For each fixed $F$ and $T$, the circuit works for every initial state.

\subsection{Proof overview}

The functional graph of $F$ consists of directed cycles with directed
trees feeding into them.  For a parameter $L$, we select $O(N/L)$
checkpoints so that every trajectory either encounters one within $2L$
steps or repeats a state within that many steps.  A repeated state reveals
a period and permits direct recovery of the answer from the initial
trajectory.

Set $K=2L$ and assume $T\geq K$.  At each checkpoint $h$, store
$z_h=F^{T-K}(h)$.  If the initial trajectory encounters $h$ at time $d\leq K$,
then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:overview-identity}
  F^{K-d}(z_h)
  =F^{K-d}\bigl(F^{T-K}(F^d(x))\bigr)
  =F^T(x).
\end{equation}
Thus only a short \emph{forward} correction is needed after the lookup.
There is no inverse operation, so the argument applies to noninjective
maps.

A separate lookup for each of $\Theta(L)$ visited states against
$\Theta(N/L)$ checkpoints would cost $\Theta(N)$ comparisons.  Instead,
we generate the full initial trajectory before looking anything up and
resolve all its queries together.  Sorting the queries and dictionary
records reduces this cost to
$\poly(n)(L+N/L)$ Boolean gates.  Combining the initial and final short
simulations with this batched lookup gives \cref{eq:tradeoff}.

\paragraph{A worked example.}
Consider eight states, encoded by three bits, with transitions
\[
  0\to1\to2\to3\to4\to5\to2,
  \qquad 6\to7\to6.
\]
Take $L=3$, $K=6$, and $H=\{2,5\}$, the endpoints of the disjoint
three-vertex paths $(0,1,2)$ and $(3,4,5)$.  Fix $T=101$.
Starting at $x=1$, the first checkpoint is $h=2$, reached at $d=1$.
Its stored payload is $z_2=F^{95}(2)=5$, so the forward correction gives
\[
  F^{K-d}(z_2)=F^5(5)=2=F^{101}(1).
\]
Starting instead at $x=6$, the prefix $6,7,6$ repeats without meeting a
checkpoint.  The repeated indices $i=0$, $j=2$ give period $p=2$.
The answer is prefix entry $i+((101-i)\bmod p)=1$, namely $7$.
Thus the two-cycle needs no dictionary entry.

\subsection{Further results and sharpness}

The exponent $1/2$ is sharp for arbitrary fixed powers.  In
\cref{sec:lower}, an explicit linear-size permutation has almost all powers
of complexity $\Omega(2^{n/2}/n)$, when the exponent is uniform modulo its
order.  The hard powers are selected by counting, require exponentially
long binary exponents, and are not explicitly identified.  The same
permutation has a polynomial-size iterator for polynomial-width input times.

Write $B=A+\sqrt{n^3NA}$.  The extensions in \cref{sec:variable,sec:batch}
handle input exponents and shared evaluation.  The graph-query circuits
in \cref{sec:structure} compute cycle labels, transient depth, reachability,
first hitting times, visit descriptors, and synchronous coalescence in
$O(B)$ gates.  Pair queries take two $n$-bit states; their exponential
scale remains $2^{n/2}$ for polynomial-size transitions.
Recognizable targets include eventual membership and recurring or permanent
acceptance.  For a predicate of size $a$, put $A_P=s+a+n^3$ and
$B_P=A_P+\sqrt{n^3NA_P}$.

\Cref{sec:structured} gives stronger bounds for autonomous controllers and
sparse counter edits.  For the latter, an explicit edit list permits
polynomial-time chart construction; transport to other coordinates charges
their encoding and decoding circuits.  Choosing $T=\ord(P)-1$ also recovers
the earlier square-root permutation-inversion bound, compatible with
polynomial-size one-wayness; see \cref{sec:one-way}.

The further extensions separate several questions that the size bound
does not settle.  \Cref{sec:exact-constant,sec:explicit-exponents,sec:near-landau}
refine counting, approximation, and the complexity of specifying hard
exponents.  \Cref{sec:aks,sec:depth-size,sec:distinguished} study smaller
polynomial factors, depth, width, and reversible implementations;
the distinguished-point procedure has checked semantics, while its
resource bounds remain prose.  \Cref{sec:min-checkpoints} studies optimal
checkpoint sets.  \Cref{sec:program-analysis,sec:preprocessing} connect
compact summaries to certification and the cost of construction, and
\cref{sec:blackbox} states its oracle-model restrictions and probabilistic
hypotheses explicitly.

\subsection{Related work and status of the formulation}\label{sec:related}

Preprocessing trajectories to exchange storage for later computation is
classical.  Hellman's cryptanalytic time--memory tradeoff is an early
example of this approach~\cite{Hellman80}.  More directly, Munro, Raman,
Raman, and Rao~\cite[Section~3.1]{MRRR12} give a shortcut representation
for permutation inversion: with a parameter $L$, their black-box
construction uses $O(L)$ evaluations and
$O((N/L)(\log N+\log L))$ auxiliary bits.  They also give succinct
representations of general functions supporting positive powers with a
word-size exponent~\cite[Section~5]{MRRR12}.  Those results concern
representation space, RAM time, and black-box access, rather than total
Boolean gate count.

A closer circuit antecedent is Hirahara, Ilango, and
Williams~\cite[Section~3.2]{HIW24}: they obtain square-root-size
permutation-inversion circuits using checkpoints and batched lookup.
Their Section~3.2 treats a single-cycle permutation explicitly and notes
in one sentence that the construction extends to all permutations through
the cycle decomposition.  Their lookup theorem~\cite[Theorem~2.2]{HIW24}
generalizes a lemma of Paul~\cite[Lemma~2]{Paul76} on evaluating a
Boolean function on many inputs by sorting; that lemma is an ancestor of
the sorting-based batched lookups used there and in
\cref{lem:lookup}.  This draft generalizes
that permutation-inversion construction to arbitrary fixed forward powers
of possibly noninjective maps.  It does not subsume their general-function
inversion and compression theorems.

For general functions, Fiat and Naor~\cite{FN99} extend Hellman's method
to rigorous time--space tradeoffs for inverting arbitrary functions, in
the random-access model; Hirahara, Ilango, and Williams implement their
algorithm with circuits~\cite[Section~3.3]{HIW24}.  Lower bounds in this
preprocessing model face a known barrier.  Corrigan-Gibbs and
Kogan~\cite{CGK19} show that improving Yao's bound $ST=\widetilde\Omega(N)$
for inverting a random function with $S$ bits of advice and $T$ queries
would give new lower bounds for depth-two circuits with arbitrary gates,
and that strong lower bounds for nonadaptive inverters would give lower
bounds for linear-size logarithmic-depth circuits.  These barriers concern
inversion, not forward powers, but they show that even black-box lower
bounds in the preprocessing model can be as hard as circuit lower bounds.
The lower bound of \cref{sec:lower} avoids such questions by counting
over exponentially long exponents (\cref{sec:lower-limits}).  \Cref{sec:crypto} compares the
construction with the treatment of preprocessing in the literature on
verifiable delay functions and time-lock puzzles.

Eppstein~\cite{Eppstein23} studies uniform iteration of polynomial-time
bijections, with the iteration count supplied in binary, and characterizes
the resulting class of functional problems as
$\mathsf{FP}^{\mathsf{PSPACE}}$.  Our resource question differs: the map
is fixed, the circuits are nonuniform, and the size bound remains
exponential in the state length.  Our result supplies no polynomial-time
algorithm for that iteration problem.  \Cref{sec:short-exponents} gives a nonuniform
counterpart for short exponents: hard powers at exponents with
polynomially many bits exist exactly when
$\mathsf{PSPACE}\not\subseteq\mathsf{P}/\mathrm{poly}$.

The present construction uses the same checkpoint and batched-lookup
principles.  Batcher's sorting networks~\cite{Batcher68} implement the
lookup; the asymptotically smaller networks of Ajtai, Koml\'os, and
Szemer\'edi~\cite{AKS83} save a factor of $n$ (\cref{sec:aks}), while
the shifted payload in \cref{eq:overview-identity} and the
checkpoint-or-repetition lemma handle general powers and functional graphs.
No RAM memory probe is treated as a unit-cost circuit operation.
Checkpointing, sorting-based lookup, and square-root-size permutation
inversion are not claimed as new.  Historical priority for the full
fixed-power theorem and the shared bounds in this draft remains unresolved.

\section{Model and circuit primitives}\label{sec:model}

\subsection{Conventions}

All logarithms are to base two.  The notation $F^t$ denotes $t$-fold
composition, with $F^0$ equal to the identity.  The quantity $\CC(G)$ is the
minimum number of internal gates in an acyclic Boolean circuit computing
all output bits of $G$.  Each internal gate computes a Boolean function
of at most two bits.  Inputs, wires, fan-out, and output designations are
not charged as gates.  The two constant bits are available; charging a
constant number of gates for them would not change any bound.

The circuit for an iterate is allowed to depend on the entire function
$F$ and on its fixed exponent.  Preprocessing time and the time required
to find the circuit are not bounded.  Hardwired data do not provide free
addressing: every comparison, multiplexer, and routing operation used to
access them is realized with Boolean gates and included in our estimates.

All state words have $n$ bits.  Because $L\leq 2^n$, indices up to $2L$ fit
in $n+2$ bits.  In the batched construction $r\leq 2^n$, so query identifiers
and record positions also have $O(n)$ bits.  We retain explicit polynomial
factors in $n$ for a concrete implementation; these factors are not
claimed to be optimal.

\subsection{Arithmetic and selection}

Comparison, addition, subtraction, and conditional selection of $w$-bit
words have $O(w)$-gate circuits.  Binary long division gives an
$O(w^2)$-gate circuit for the remainder of a $w$-bit integer modulo a
positive $w$-bit integer: process the dividend bits from most to least
significant, and at each stage conditionally subtract the modulus from
the current shifted remainder.  There are $O(w)$ stages and $O(w)$ gates
per stage.

A circuit can select one of $q$ words of width $w$ using $O(qw)$ gates by
a binary tree of word multiplexers, padding to a power of two when
necessary.  It can also select one flagged record from an ordered list of
$q$ records in $O(qw)$ gates.  For example, a left-to-right scan maintains
a bit indicating whether a flagged record has already been found and
retains the first one.  This scan is simply an acyclic chain of gates;
no depth bound is needed here.

\begin{lemma}[Batched fixed-dictionary lookup]\label[lemma]{lem:lookup}
Suppose a fixed dictionary consists of $M$ distinct $w$-bit keys, each
with a $w$-bit payload.  Given $Q\geq 1$ query keys, a Boolean circuit can
output a match bit and the corresponding payload for every query, in the
original query order, using
\begin{equation}\label{eq:lookup-general}
  O\!\left((M+Q)\bigl(w+\log(M+Q+2)\bigr)
                    \log^2(M+Q+2)\right)
\end{equation}
gates.  The payload of an unmatched query can be set to zero.  In
particular, if $w=O(n)$ and $\log(M+Q+2)=O(n)$, the cost is
$O(n^3(M+Q))$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Let $R=M+Q$.  Form one record for each dictionary entry and one for each
query.  A record contains a key, a dictionary/query tag, a payload field,
and an original-position field.  The record width is
$O(w+\log(R+2))$.

Sort the records lexicographically by key, then by the tag with dictionary
records first, and then by original position.  A sorting network with
$O(R\log^2(R+2))$ comparators exists~\cite{Batcher68}.  A comparator
and the resulting conditional swap of the entire records cost
$O(w+\log(R+2))$ Boolean gates.  Padding uses a separate dummy-record bit
so that no actual key is reserved as a sentinel.

In the sorted list, a dictionary record precedes every query with its key.
Scan the list, resetting the match bit when the key changes and propagating
the payload from a dictionary record through the equal-key run that
follows it.  If an equal-key run has no dictionary record, every query in
that run receives match bit zero.  Dictionary keys are distinct, so no
run contains two competing payloads.  This scan uses
$O(R(w+\log(R+2)))$ gates.

Finally, sort records by a tag that places queries first and then by the
query's original position.  The first $Q$ records give the requested
outputs in order.  A second sorting network has the same asymptotic cost
as the first.  Every operation described is a fixed acyclic circuit.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Detecting a repeated state]\label[lemma]{lem:repeat}
Given $Q$ words of $w$ bits, a circuit can return a flag indicating whether
two words are equal and, when the flag is one, indices $i<j$ of two equal
words.  Its size is
\[
  O\!\left(Q\bigl(w+\log(Q+2)\bigr)\log^2(Q+2)\right).
\]
In particular, for $Q\leq 2^{n+O(1)}$ and $w=O(n)$, the size is $O(n^3Q)$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Attach each word's original index and sort by word and then by index.
A duplicate exists exactly when two adjacent sorted records have the
same word.  Compare adjacent words and select one flagged pair.  Sorting
by index within an equal-word run guarantees that the returned indices
satisfy $i<j$.  The remaining comparisons and selection use at most
$O(Q(w+\log(Q+2)))$ gates.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Sparse checkpoints in functional graphs}\label{sec:checkpoints}

The functional graph of a map $F$ has one vertex for each state and the
edge $v\to F(v)$ at every vertex.  Each weakly connected component has a
unique directed cycle, with directed trees feeding into it.  For a
noncycle vertex, its \emph{depth} is the number of steps needed to reach
the cycle.

\begin{lemma}[Checkpoint or repetition]\label[lemma]{lem:checkpoints}
Let $F$ be a function on a set of $N$ states, and let $1\leq L\leq N$.
There is a set $H$ of at most $\floor{N/L}$ states such that, for every $x$,
either two of $x,F(x),\ldots,F^{L-1}(x)$ are equal or
$F^d(x)\in H$ for some $d<2L$.
More generally, the same conclusion holds for starting states in any
finite forward-invariant set $S$, with $|H|L\leq |S|$ and $H\subseteq S$.
The ambient state type need not be finite.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Choose a maximal family of pairwise vertex-disjoint simple directed paths,
each consisting of $L$ vertices in $S$.  Put the last vertex of each path
into $H$.  Disjointness gives $|H|L\leq |S|$.
If the first $L$ states of an orbit are distinct, they form such a path
in $S$.  By maximality this path meets a chosen path: say
$F^i(x)=F^j(a)$, where $i,j<L$ and the chosen path starts at $a$.
Its marked endpoint is reached from $x$ after
$i+(L-1-j)<2L$ steps.  If the prefix is not simple, it already contains a
repetition.  Taking $S$ to be the entire state space proves the first
claim.  The Lean proof implements the same packing argument by induction
on $|S|$, removing one simple path at a time.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}
It is essential that the lemma allows a short repetition instead of a
checkpoint.  A function can have $N$ fixed points, in which case a set
meeting every eventual cycle must contain all $N$ states.  Such cycles
need no stored shortcut: their behavior is already visible locally.
\Cref{sec:min-checkpoints} determines how small such sets can be and how
hard a minimum one is to find.
\end{remark}

\section{Circuits for fixed powers}\label{sec:fixed}

We now prove \cref{thm:main}.  The construction is uniform as a description
of a circuit from the chosen constants, but no efficient procedure for
computing those constants is asserted.

\subsection{Short trajectories and shifted payloads}

Fix $L$ and let $K=2L$.  If $T<K$, use $T$ copies of a circuit for $F$;
this costs at most $2Ls$ gates.  Hence assume $T\geq K$.
Choose $H$ as in \cref{lem:checkpoints}.  For every $h\in H$, hardwire the
$n$-bit payload
\begin{equation}\label{eq:payload}
  z_h=F^{T-K}(h).
\end{equation}
The dictionary is fixed independently of the input $x$ and has at most
$N/L$ entries.

On input $x$, first compute
\begin{equation}\label{eq:prefix}
  x_0=x,\quad x_1=F(x),\quad\ldots,\quad x_K=F^K(x).
\end{equation}
This requires $Ks$ gates.  Apply \cref{lem:lookup} to all $K+1$ states in
\cref{eq:prefix}, using the dictionary $h\mapsto z_h$.  Select one
matching record, if any, and retain its original trajectory index $d$.
This gives a match flag and a pair $(d,z_h)$ with $x_d=h$ whenever the
flag is one.

For the matched case, compute a second short trajectory
\[
  z_h,F(z_h),\ldots,F^K(z_h)
\]
and select its entry at position $K-d$.  Its value is
\begin{align}
  F^{K-d}(z_h)
    &=F^{K-d}\bigl(F^{T-K}(h)\bigr)\notag\\
    &=F^{T-d}(h)\notag\\
    &=F^{T-d}\bigl(F^d(x)\bigr)
     =F^T(x).\label{eq:identity}
\end{align}
All exponents used here are nonnegative.  This second trajectory costs
another $Ks$ gates; selection costs $O(nK)$ gates.

\subsection{The repeated-state case and very large exponents}

Use \cref{lem:repeat} on \cref{eq:prefix}.  If no checkpoint was found,
\cref{lem:checkpoints} guarantees indices
\[
  0\leq i<j\leq K,\qquad x_i=x_j.
\]
Put $p=j-i$.  Determinism implies $x_{u+p}=x_u$ for all $u\geq i$.
The number $p$ need not be the least period.  Since $T\geq K\geq i$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:period}
  F^T(x)=x_{\,i+((T-i)\bmod p)}.
\end{equation}
The selected index is at most $i+p-1=j-1$, so the required state already
occurs in the initial trajectory.

To implement \cref{eq:period} without paying for the bit length of $T$,
hardwire the table
\begin{equation}\label{eq:residue-table}
  R_T(p)=T\bmod p,\qquad 1\leq p\leq K.
\end{equation}
There are $K$ entries, each of $O(n)$ bits.  A multiplexer tree retrieves
$R_T(p)$ in $O(nK)$ gates.  Compute $i\bmod p$ by binary long division
and then compute
\[
  (T-i)\bmod p
    =\bigl(R_T(p)-(i\bmod p)\bigr)\bmod p
\]
using a comparison, subtraction, and a conditional addition of $p$.
These operations take $O(n^2)$ gates.  A further $O(nK)$ gates select the
state prescribed in \cref{eq:period}.

A circuit evaluates both branches, so all intermediate operations must
also be defined when their output will be discarded.  If a repeated pair
is absent, supply the dummy values $i=0$ and $p=1$ to the arithmetic
branch.  If a checkpoint is absent, supply a dummy state and $d=0$ to
the second trajectory.  The final output selects the checkpoint branch
when its match flag is one, and otherwise selects the repetition branch.
The checkpoint lemma ensures that the selected branch is always valid.
In particular, no division by zero occurs on any input.

\subsection{Size bound}

The two simulations together use at most $2Ks=4Ls$ gates.  The dictionary
and query list contain
\[
  |H|+(K+1)=O\!\left(\frac{N}{L}+L\right)
\]
records of $O(n)$ bits, and the logarithm of this number is $O(n)$.
Thus \cref{lem:lookup} costs
$O(n^3(N/L+L))$ gates.  Repeated-state detection costs $O(n^3L)$ gates,
and all remaining lookup, arithmetic, and selection operations cost
$O(nL+n^2)$ gates.  Since $n,L\geq 1$, the latter cost is absorbed by
$O(n^3L)$.  This proves \cref{eq:tradeoff}.

Write the tradeoff as $O(AL+n^3N/L)$, where $A=s+n^3$, and choose
\begin{equation}\label{eq:L-choice}
  L=\max\left\{1,\ceil{\sqrt{n^3N/A}}\right\}.
\end{equation}
Since $A\geq n^3$, this choice satisfies $L\leq\ceil{\sqrt N}\leq N$.
If $A\leq n^3N$, then
$\sqrt{n^3N/A}\leq L\leq 2\sqrt{n^3N/A}$.  Hence
\[
  AL=O(\sqrt{n^3NA}),\qquad
  n^3N/L=O(\sqrt{n^3NA}).
\]
If $A>n^3N$, the choice is $L=1$ and the cost is $O(A)$.
This establishes
\cref{eq:optimized} and completes the proof of \cref{thm:main}.
\hfill$\square$

\begin{remark}[Where the exponent is stored]\label[remark]{rem:nonuniform}
For $T\geq K$, the information about $T$ used by the circuit is confined
to the checkpoint payloads in \cref{eq:payload} and the residue table in
\cref{eq:residue-table}.  Their total length is
$O(n(N/L+L))$ bits.  The construction does not place the binary expansion
of $T$ on a long hidden input tape, and it does not perform uncharged
arithmetic on an arbitrarily large integer.  Computing the hardwired
information is a preprocessing task outside the asserted circuit bound.
\end{remark}

\begin{remark}[Succinct checkpoint sets]\label[remark]{rem:succinct-checkpoints}
A checkpoint set with a small membership circuit, such as the zeros of a
hash function, does not reduce the size in general.  It shortens the
description of $H$, but the dependence on $T$ sits in the payloads.  For
the permutation $P_n$ of \cref{sec:lower}, a circuit for $P_n^T$
determines $T\bmod a$ for every $1\leq a\leq B$ by
\cref{thm:remainders}, hence $T\bmod D_B$, which takes at least
$B-\log(B+1)$ bits by \cref{lem:lcm}.  \Cref{thm:lower} bounds every
circuit for some power, however its checkpoints are chosen.  Only
compressible payloads could help, and for $P_n$ they are structured,
being residues of $T$, yet some power still needs $\Omega(2^{n/2}/n)$
gates.
\end{remark}

\subsection{Charging only long trajectories}

Let $\delta(v)$ be the distance from $v$ to its eventual cycle, and let
$\ell(v)$ be that cycle's length.  For $1\leq L\leq N$, put
\[
 Q_L=|\{v:\delta(v)\geq L\}|+
     |\{v:\delta(v)=0,\ \ell(v)\geq L\}|.
\]
The two sets are disjoint.  Their union contains the deep transient
vertices and all vertices of cycles with length at least $L$.

\begin{theorem}[Long-trajectory tradeoff]\label{thm:active-mass}
For every fixed $T\geq0$ and $1\leq L\leq N$,
\[
 \CC(F^T)=O\!\left(Ls+n^3\left(L+\frac{Q_L}{L}\right)\right).
\]
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Apply the path-packing argument of \cref{lem:checkpoints} only to paths
whose $L$ vertices all lie in the union above.  Mark their endpoints.
The resulting set $H$ satisfies $|H|L\leq Q_L$.  Every $L$-state prefix
inside this union either repeats or meets a packed path, and hence reaches
its marked endpoint within $2L$ steps.  This packing argument does not
require the union to be forward invariant.

If $\delta(x)\geq2L$, the first $L$ states are all deep, so that argument
applies immediately.  Otherwise the trajectory reaches its cycle before
$2L$ steps.  A short cycle then repeats before $3L$ steps.  On a long
cycle all vertices lie in the union, so the packing argument gives a
checkpoint or repetition within another $2L$ steps.  Thus every trajectory
hits $H$ or repeats within a prefix of $4L$ steps.

Use this prefix in the fixed-power construction, with payload
$F^{T-4L}(h)$ when $T\geq4L$ and direct unrolling otherwise.  The
dictionary has at most $Q_L/L$ entries and the prefix has $O(L)$ states,
giving the stated cost.
\end{proof}

Short transient paths leading to short cycles incur no dictionary cost.
The bound includes $Q_L=0$.  Since $Q_L$ itself depends on $L$, one must
retain that dependence when choosing the tradeoff parameter.

\subsection{Invariant sets and image-sensitive bounds}

\begin{theorem}[Invariant-set tradeoff]\label{thm:invariant}
Let $S\subseteq\bits^n$ be forward invariant under $F$, with $|S|=M$.
For every fixed $T\geq0$ and $1\leq L\leq N$, there is a circuit agreeing
with $F^T$ on $S$ of size
\[
 O\!\left(Ls+n^3\left(L+\frac{M}{L}\right)\right).
\]
The circuit is total on $\bits^n$; correctness outside $S$ is not asserted.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Use the invariant-set version of \cref{lem:checkpoints}, and otherwise
apply the fixed-power construction unchanged.  A state still occupies
$n$ bits, but the dictionary contains only $O(M/L)$ records.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Image sensitivity]\label[corollary]{cor:image}
Put $M_d=|\operatorname{im}(F^d)|$.  For $T\geq d$ and $1\leq L\leq M_d$,
\[
 \CC(F^T)\leq \CC(F^d)+
 O\!\left(Ls+n^3\left(L+\frac{M_d}{L}\right)\right).
\]
Consequently this is at most
$\CC(F^d)+O(A+\sqrt{n^3M_dA})$.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
The image of $F^d$ is forward invariant, since
$F(F^d(x))=F^d(F(x))$.  Compute $F^d(x)$ and apply
\cref{thm:invariant} on its image for $T-d$ more steps.
Optimize $L$ as in \cref{eq:L-choice}, replacing $N$ by $M_d$.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Permutation inversion and one-way functions}\label{sec:one-way}

\begin{corollary}[Exact permutation inversion]\label[corollary]{cor:inverse}
Let $P:\bits^n\to\bits^n$ be a permutation with $\CC(P)\leq s$, and put
$N=2^n$ and $A=s+n^3$.  For every $1\leq L\leq N$,
\[
  \CC(P^{-1})
  =O\!\left(Ls+n^3\left(L+\frac NL\right)\right).
\]
In particular,
\[
  \CC(P^{-1})=O\!\left(A+\sqrt{n^3NA}\right).
\]
If $s=n^{O(1)}$, an inverter of size $2^{n/2+O(\log n)}$ is correct on
every input.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
Let $D=\ord(P)$, the least common multiple of its cycle lengths.
Then $P^D$ is the identity, so $P^{-1}=P^{D-1}$.
Apply \cref{thm:main}.  The exponent may have a long binary expansion,
but the theorem charges only the resulting tables and their access gates.
\end{proof}

This recovers the permutation-inversion circuit bound already discussed
by Hirahara, Ilango, and Williams~\cite[Section~3.2]{HIW24}.
It identifies an earlier special case of the fixed-power theorem.

One-wayness against polynomial-size circuits requires every such inverter
to have negligible success on the image of a uniform input
\cite[Section~7.1.1]{Goldreich08}.  The exponential-size exact inverter above
is compatible with that requirement.  It does rule out security against
all circuits of size $2^{(1/2+\varepsilon)n}$, for any fixed
$\varepsilon>0$ and sufficiently large $n$; it establishes no security
at smaller sizes.

For noninjective maps, a forward power need not find a preimage.  For
example, the map with values $(0,0,1,0)$ on $\{0,1,2,3\}$ has unique
preimage $2$ of $1$, while the forward orbit of $1$ stays in $\{0,1\}$.
Hirahara, Ilango, and Williams give a separate general-function inversion
bound of $2^{4n/5}\poly(s+n)$ gates~\cite[Theorem~3.5]{HIW24}.
Encoding exhaustive preimage search as iteration retains both an $n$-bit
target and an $n$-bit candidate, so the present theorem gives only
$2^{n+O(\log n)}$ gates for that construction.

Nor does the counting example in \cref{sec:lower} establish one-way
functions.  Its update permutation has a linear-size inverse, obtained
by decrementing the active cycle coordinate.  The selected hard powers
have exponential worst-case forward complexity, so cannot themselves
form a polynomial-time one-way function.  The counting argument gives
no average-case inversion hardness.

\subsection{Cryptographic consequences}\label{sec:crypto}

Iterated round functions serve as delay functions.  Iterated hashing,
which Boneh, Bonneau, B\"unz, and Fisch~\cite{BBBF18} discuss as the basic
sequential function behind verifiable delay functions, the slow hash
sloth~\cite{LW15}, and the candidate MinRoot~\cite{KMT22} all evaluate
$g^T$ for a public round function $g$ on an $n$-bit state.  The security
property, \emph{sequentiality}, asks
that $g^T$ cannot be computed much faster than by $T$ sequential
applications of $g$.  \Cref{thm:main} bounds the nonuniform complexity of
$g^T$, so it limits what a nonuniform sequentiality claim can assert.
This subsection records the resulting limits and checks them against
concrete parameters.  Lean checks only the exponent reduction for
sequential squaring and the arithmetic of the table entries
(\cref{sec:lean}); the remaining statements are prose.  None of them
affects a deployed parameter set that we examined.

\paragraph{Depth.}
Sequentiality concerns depth, while \cref{thm:main} counts gates.
For a round circuit $g$ of size $s$ and depth $d$, the construction in
\cref{thm:depth-size} has size $O(Ls+n^3(L+N/L))$ and depth
$4Ld+O(n^2\log n)$: the two trajectories contribute at most $4Ld$,
and parallel prefix operations and sorting bound the remaining depth.

\paragraph{Speed-up and size.}
Unrolling $g^T$ has depth $Td$.  For $T\leq\sqrt N$, choose $L$ of order
$T/\sigma$ for a speed-up factor $\sigma$ with $4\leq\sigma\leq T$.  The
depth becomes about $Td\cdot4/\sigma$, and when the dictionary term
dominates the size is of order
\[
  \sigma\cdot\frac{n^3N}{T}.
\]
Equivalently, depth times size is of order $n^3Nd$, independently of $T$.
In particular, even a constant-factor speed-up costs about $n^3N/T$
gates in this construction.  For $T\geq\sqrt N$ the choice
\cref{eq:L-choice} gives size $2^{n/2}\poly(n,s)$ and depth at most
$4\ceil{\sqrt N}d+O(n^2\log n)$, an unbounded speed-up as $T$ grows.  All of this
is an upper bound for one construction; we make no claim that smaller
fast circuits do not exist.

The same tradeoff appears in cryptanalysis as an average-case statement
about random-access machines: with $S$ stored iterates spaced $L$ apart,
a random challenge meets one within $L$ steps with probability about
$SL/N$.  \Cref{thm:main} is the every-input, gate-counted form, with a
size independent of $T$.

\paragraph{A design rule.}
Say that $g^T$ is sequential at level $k$ against circuits if no circuit
of size below $2^k$ computes $g^T$ correctly on every input with depth at
most a constant fraction of $Td$.  The construction above then shows the
following.  If $T\leq\sqrt N$ and $Ts<2^k$, sequentiality at level $k$ requires
roughly $n+3\log_2n-\log_2T\geq k$.  If sequentiality is required for every number
of rounds, then, taking $T$ near $\sqrt N$ and beyond, it requires
$2^{n/2}\poly(n,s)\geq2^k$, that is,
\[
  n\geq 2k-O(\log k)
\]
for polynomial $s$.  The rule is consistent with existing practice: sloth
takes a prime $p\geq2^{2k}$ at security level $k$~\cite[Section~3.3]{LW15},
so its state has about $2k$ bits; MinRoot claims 128-bit security with a
512-bit state~\cite[Claim~1]{KMT22}; and time--memory--data tradeoffs for
state recovery~\cite{BS00} are the source of the rule that a stream cipher's
state should have at least twice as many bits as its key.  We do not claim
the rule as new.  We found no derivation of it from the circuit size of
iterates.  For noninjective round functions, \cref{cor:image} gives more.
If $|\operatorname{im}(g^d)|=O(N/d)$ for $d$ near $N^{1/3}$, as is expected
heuristically for random-like maps, then every $g^T$ has a circuit of size
$N^{1/3}\poly(n,s)$, and the corresponding rule for unboundedly many rounds
would be $n\geq3k-O(\log k)$.  We do not know whether any concrete hash
function has this image profile.

\paragraph{Concrete parameters.}
\Cref{tab:crypto} lists $\log_2$ of the two sizes for some state lengths,
with constant factors and polynomial factors other than $n^3$ omitted.
We use $T=2^{40}$ honest rounds, generously; the MinRoot analysis report
names $t\approx2^{30}$ as the practical range~\cite[Section~4.2.4]{MinRoot23}.
MinRoot adds the round index to the state in every
round~\cite[Section~4.1.2]{MinRoot23}, so its round function is a fixed map
only on a state extended by a counter; this makes $n$ larger than $512$ and
the sizes larger.

\begin{table}[htbp]
\centering
\small
\begin{tabular}{@{}lrrrr@{}}
\toprule
Round function & $n$ & $k$ & $\log_2\sqrt N$ & $\log_2(n^3N/T)$, $T=2^{40}$ \\
\midrule
MinRoot over a 256-bit field & $\geq512$ & 128 & $\geq256$ & $\geq499$ \\
sloth with $p\approx2^{256}$ & 256 & 128 & 128 & 240 \\
SHA-256 chain & 256 & 128 & 128 & 240 \\
any 128-bit state & 128 & 128 & 64 & 109 \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\caption{Sizes, as base-2 logarithms, of the circuits of \cref{thm:main}:
for $g^T$ at every $T$ (column $\sqrt N$), and for a constant-factor
speed-up at $T=2^{40}$ (last column).  Constant factors and the factors
$\poly(n,s)$ in the $\sqrt N$ column are omitted; $k$ is the claimed or
intended security level.}
\label{tab:crypto}
\end{table}

For MinRoot, sloth, and an iterated 256-bit hash, the speed-up size exceeds
$2^{k}$ by more than $2^{100}$.  The every-$T$ size exceeds $2^k$ by
$2^{128}$ for MinRoot.  For sloth and the hash it equals $2^k$ up to the
omitted polynomial factors, which is the boundary $n\approx2k$ of the
design rule.  Moreover, the obvious way to compute the
payloads evaluates $g$ on essentially all $N$ states, whereas MinRoot
allows the adversary $2^{128}$ precomputation calls~\cite{KMT22}.  So no
deployed parameter that we checked is affected.  The last row only
illustrates the design rule: a 128-bit state would admit, in principle,
a circuit of about $2^{109}$ gates that evaluates $2^{40}$ rounds a constant
factor faster than unrolling, below a nominal 128-bit nonuniform level.

\paragraph{Where the cryptographic literature stands.}
Sequentiality definitions bound the preprocessing.  In the definition of
Boneh, Bonneau, B\"unz, and Fisch~\cite[Definition~4]{BBBF18}, a
preprocessing algorithm $A_0$ runs in time polynomial in $t$ and the
security parameter, and an online algorithm $A_1$ has parallel time below
$t$ on polynomially many processors.  Their iterated sequential functions
must be sequential for $2^{o(\lambda)}$ rounds with $\lambda=O(\log|X|)$
\cite[Definition~7]{BBBF18}, far fewer than $\sqrt{|X|}$, so
\cref{thm:main} does not contradict these definitions.  The MinRoot
analysis report~\cite[Chapter~4]{MinRoot23} models the cube root as a
random permutation and bounds the oracle queries of $A_0$ and $A_1$.  Its
Theorem~2 is a precomputation attack of the table-lookup type: $A_0$
stores $d$-round outputs of many starting points, and a challenge in the
stored set saves $d$ rounds; its advantage is stated as an average over
the challenge.  The report leaves a tight lower bound in the
bounded-advice model open.  Mahmoody, Moran, and Vadhan~\cite{MMV11}
analyse time-lock puzzles in the random oracle model against
query-bounded adversaries without auxiliary input.  We found no prior
statement of the every-input, gate-counted tradeoff for forward iterates
in these works, and no sequentiality bound for an iterated random oracle
against adversaries with auxiliary input.  Whether the checkpoint tradeoff
is optimal in that model is open.

\paragraph{Sequential squaring.}
For a fixed squarefree modulus $M$ of $n$ bits, the map $x\mapsto x^2\bmod M$
has polynomial-size circuits, so \cref{thm:main} gives $2^{n/2}\poly(n)$
gates for $x\mapsto x^{2^T}\bmod M$.  Nonuniformly much more is true.
With the Carmichael exponent $\lambda(M)$ hardwired, $x^a\equiv x^b\pmod M$
for all $x$ whenever $a,b\geq1$ and $a\equiv b\pmod{\lambda(M)}$, so
$x^{2^T}\bmod M=x^e\bmod M$ with $e=1+((2^T-1)\bmod\lambda(M))$, which
square-and-multiply computes in polynomial size.  The congruence holds
for non-units because each prime factor $p$ of $M$ divides $x$ or has
$x^{\lambda(M)}\equiv1\pmod p$, and the shift by one keeps $e\geq1$: when
$\lambda(M)$ divides $2^T$, the exponent $2^T\bmod\lambda(M)=0$ would
give $x^0=1$ at $x=0$.  Lean checks this reduction for every $T$ and
every residue, with $1\leq e\leq\lambda(M)$; the square-and-multiply
circuit is not mechanized.  The sequentiality of
the time-lock puzzle of Rivest, Shamir, and Wagner~\cite{RSW96} and of
squaring-based delay functions is therefore a statement about adversaries
that cannot factor $M$, not about circuits that may depend on $M$.  In the
definitions above this is enforced by the polynomial time bound on $A_0$.
For a fixed public round function such as sloth's or MinRoot's, by
contrast, the model of \cref{thm:main}, in which the circuit may depend on
$g$, is the preprocessing model with an exponential rather than a
polynomial budget.

\paragraph{Stream ciphers.}
The state update of Trivium~\cite{Trivium06} is a fixed map on $288$ bits,
and that of Grain~\cite{Grain07} on $160$ bits; the key enters only
through the initial state.  For every $T$, \cref{thm:main} gives circuits
of size $2^{144}\poly(n)$ and $2^{80}\poly(n)$ that jump ahead $T$ steps.
This is no attack.  Jumping ahead helps only for $T$ beyond what can be
run directly, and these sizes are at least $2^k$ for the $80$-bit keys,
in accordance with the state-size rule above.  The theorem does show that
a generator on $n$ bits hides no forward sequentiality beyond
$2^{n/2}\poly(n)$ nonuniform gates, however many steps are taken.

\section{Minimum checkpoint sets}\label{sec:min-checkpoints}

The constructions above only need the existence of a sparse checkpoint
set.  This section asks how small such a set can be for a given map, and
how hard it is to find one.  For a map given by a circuit, deciding
whether a checkpoint set of a given size exists is
$\mathsf{coNP}$-complete; for a map given as a table, a minimum set is
computable in polynomial time.  The proof rests on a min--max duality for
directed paths in in-forests.  That duality, \cref{lem:forest-duality},
is checked in Lean as \texttt{ForestDuality.forest\_duality}, for an
arbitrary vertex set of a finite in-forest; the component formula, the
complexity results and the table algorithm are not mechanized.  None of
the results in this section is used elsewhere in the paper.

\begin{definition}\label[definition]{def:ckpt}
Let $F$ be a map on a finite set $V$ of $N$ states, and let $K\geq1$.  A
\emph{$K$-trajectory} is a list $x,F(x),\ldots,F^{K-1}(x)$ of $K$
pairwise distinct states.  A set $H\subseteq V$ is a
\emph{$K$-checkpoint set} if it meets every $K$-trajectory, and
$\mathrm{opt}_K(F)$ is the least size of a $K$-checkpoint set.
\end{definition}

If $x,\ldots,F^{2L-1}(x)$ are distinct, then so are
$x,\ldots,F^{L-1}(x)$, and the set $H$ of \cref{lem:checkpoints} contains
some $F^d(x)$ with $d<2L$.  So that set is a $2L$-checkpoint set and
$\mathrm{opt}_{2L}(F)\leq\floor{N/L}$.  This is tight up to a factor of
two: on a single cycle of length $N\geq K$, each state lies in exactly
$K$ of the $N$ $K$-trajectories, so $\mathrm{opt}_K\geq N/K$.  For other
maps $\mathrm{opt}_K$ can be far smaller; it is $0$ for a map with only
fixed points.

\begin{lemma}[Long trajectories]\label[lemma]{lem:ckpt-long}
If $\mathrm{opt}_K(F)\leq m$, then every list $x,F(x),\ldots,F^{R-1}(x)$
of $R$ pairwise distinct states has $R<(m+1)K$.  In particular every
cycle of $F$ has length less than $(m+1)K$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
The list contains $\floor{R/K}$ disjoint consecutive blocks of $K$
states, each a $K$-trajectory, and each needs its own point of a
$K$-checkpoint set.  So $\floor{R/K}\leq m$.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Duality in in-forests}

An \emph{in-forest} is a finite set $V$ with a partial successor map
$\sigma:V\to V\cup\{\bot\}$ that has no cycles.  A \emph{path} is a list
$v_1,\ldots,v_j$ with $\sigma(v_i)=v_{i+1}$ for $i<j$; it \emph{ends} at
$v_j$ and has length $j$.  A vertex $u\neq v$ is an \emph{ancestor} of $v$
if $\sigma^i(u)=v$ for some $i\geq1$.  Fix a requirement
$r:V\to\{1,2,\ldots\}$.  A path is \emph{required} if its length is at
least $r$ of its end.  Let $\tau_r$ be the least size of a set meeting
every required path, and $\nu_r$ the largest number of pairwise
vertex-disjoint required paths.  Write $\tau_K,\nu_K$ when $r\equiv K$.

\begin{lemma}[Duality]\label[lemma]{lem:forest-duality}
In every in-forest, $\tau_r=\nu_r$.  A minimum cover is obtained as
follows.  Process the vertices in an order in which every vertex follows
all of its ancestors, maintaining a set $G$.  At $v$, let
\[
  d(v)=1+\max\{d(u):\sigma(u)=v,\ u\notin G\},
\]
with the maximum of the empty set equal to $0$, and add $v$ to $G$ if
$d(v)\geq r(v)$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
By induction in the processing order, $d(v)$ is the largest length of a
path ending at $v$ whose vertices other than $v$ lie outside $G$; the
status of those vertices is final when $v$ is processed, because they
are ancestors of $v$.

$G$ is a cover.  Let $Q$ be a required path ending at $v$ and avoiding
$G$.  Then $d(v)\geq|Q|\geq r(v)$, so $v\in G$, a contradiction.

For each $v\in G$ choose a \emph{witness} $W_v$: the last $r(v)$ vertices
of a path of length $d(v)$ realizing the definition.  It is a required
path ending at $v$, and $W_v\cap G=\{v\}$, since its other vertices are
ancestors of $v$ and lie outside $G$.  Witnesses are pairwise disjoint.
Suppose $u\in W_v\cap W_w$ with $v\neq w$.  Both $v$ and $w$ lie on the
unique forward path from $u$, say $v$ strictly before $w$.  Then $v$ lies
on $W_w$ between $u$ and $w$, and $v\in G$, contradicting
$W_w\cap G=\{w\}$.

Hence $|G|\leq\nu_r$.  Disjoint required paths need distinct points of a
cover, so $\nu_r\leq\tau_r\leq|G|$.
\end{proof}

\subsection{The component formula}

A $K$-trajectory follows $F$, so it lies in one weakly connected
component of the functional graph.  Hence $\mathrm{opt}_K(F)$ is the sum
of $\mathrm{opt}_K$ over the components.  Fix a component $Q$ with cycle
$Z$ of length $\ell$.  For a cycle vertex $c$, let $Q-c$ be the in-forest
on $Q\setminus\{c\}$ with $\sigma(v)=F(v)$ unless $F(v)=c$, in which case
$\sigma(v)=\bot$.  Let $Q-Z$ be the in-forest on $Q\setminus Z$ with
$\sigma(v)=F(v)$ unless $F(v)\in Z$, in which case $\sigma(v)=\bot$, and
give it the requirement $r_Z(v)=K-\ell$ if $F(v)\in Z$ and $r_Z(v)=K$
otherwise.

\begin{lemma}[Component formula]\label[lemma]{lem:component-formula}
Let $\mathrm{opt}(Q)$ be the least size of a set meeting every
$K$-trajectory in $Q$.
\begin{enumerate}
\item If $\ell\geq K$, let $W$ consist of any $K$ consecutive vertices of
  $Z$.  Then $\mathrm{opt}(Q)=\min_{c\in W}\bigl(1+\nu_K(Q-c)\bigr)$.
\item If $\ell<K$, then
  $\mathrm{opt}(Q)=\min\bigl(\nu_{r_Z}(Q-Z),\
  \min_{c\in Z}(1+\nu_K(Q-c))\bigr)$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
First fix a cycle vertex $c$.  Because $Q-c$ has no cycle, its paths of
length $K$ are lists of $K$ distinct consecutive states avoiding $c$,
that is, the $K$-trajectories of $Q$ avoiding $c$; conversely every such
trajectory is a path of $Q-c$.  So the least size of a set containing $c$
and meeting every $K$-trajectory of $Q$ is $1+\tau_K(Q-c)$, which equals
$1+\nu_K(Q-c)$ by \cref{lem:forest-duality}.

(1) The $K$ vertices of $W$ are distinct consecutive states, so $W$ is a
$K$-trajectory and every feasible set contains some $c\in W$.  The
formula is the minimum of the previous paragraph over $c\in W$.

(2) A feasible set either contains a cycle vertex, which is covered by
the first paragraph, or avoids $Z$.  Let $x$ have depth $a$, so that
$x,\ldots,F^{a-1}(x)$ are tree vertices and $F^a(x)\in Z$.  The first
$a+\ell$ states of the orbit are distinct and the next one repeats, so
$x$ starts a $K$-trajectory exactly when $a+\ell\geq K$.  A set avoiding
$Z$ meets this trajectory exactly when it meets its tree part
$x,\ldots,F^{\min(a,K)-1}(x)$.

These tree parts are required paths of $Q-Z$ under $r_Z$.  If $a\geq K$
the part has $K$ vertices.  If $K-\ell\leq a<K$, it has $a\geq K-\ell$
vertices and ends at a vertex $u$ with $F(u)\in Z$.

Conversely, every required path of $Q-Z$ contains such a tree part.  A
required path ending at a vertex $v$ with $F(v)\notin Z$ contains a path
of exactly $K$ vertices ending at $v$, which is the tree part of its
first vertex, whose depth is at least $K$.  A required path ending at
$u$ with $F(u)\in Z$ contains the path of its last $K-\ell$ vertices,
which is the tree part of a state of depth exactly $K-\ell$.

So a set avoiding $Z$ is feasible exactly when it covers the required
paths of $Q-Z$, and the least such size is
$\tau_{r_Z}(Q-Z)=\nu_{r_Z}(Q-Z)$ by \cref{lem:forest-duality}.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Maps given by circuits}

Let $\mathrm{CKPT}$ be the problem: given a circuit for
$F:\bits^n\to\bits^n$ and integers $K$ and $m$ in unary, decide whether
$\mathrm{opt}_K(F)\leq m$.  The defining formula, that some list $H$ of at
most $m$ states meets the first $K$ states of every orbit with no
repetition among them, places $\mathrm{CKPT}$ in $\Sigma_2^p$.  Duality
brings it down one level.

\begin{theorem}\label{thm:ckpt-conp}
$\mathrm{CKPT}$ is $\mathsf{coNP}$-complete.  For every fixed $m\geq0$ it
remains $\mathsf{coNP}$-complete with $m$ fixed and $K=2$.  Consequently
$\mathrm{CKPT}$ is not $\Sigma_2^p$-hard under polynomial-time reductions
unless $\mathsf{NP}=\mathsf{coNP}$, in which case the polynomial
hierarchy collapses to $\mathsf{NP}$~\cite{Stockmeyer76}.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
\emph{Membership.}  We give polynomially checkable certificates for
$\mathrm{opt}_K(F)>m$.  Put $M=(m+1)K$.  A certificate is either
\begin{enumerate}
\item[(i)] a list $x,F(x),\ldots,F^{M-1}(x)$ of $M$ distinct states, or
\item[(ii)] at most $m+1$ components, each given by a state $z$, an
  integer $\ell<M$ and an integer $b\geq1$, together with packings.
\end{enumerate}
In (ii), with $W=\{z,\ldots,F^{K-1}(z)\}$ when $\ell\geq K$, the
verifier checks that $F^\ell(z)=z$ and $F^j(z)\neq z$ for
$0<j<\ell$, so that $Z=\{z,\ldots,F^{\ell-1}(z)\}$ is a cycle of length
$\ell$.  It checks that the cycles of different listed components are
disjoint, and that the listed $b$ sum to at least $m+1$.  For each option
of \cref{lem:component-formula}, the certificate lists pairwise disjoint
required paths in the corresponding in-forest: $b-1$ of them for an
option $c$, and $b$ of them for the option $Q-Z$.  Each path is an
explicit list of at most $K$ states.  The verifier checks the $F$-steps,
the avoidance of $c$ or of $Z$, the length condition, and that the
path lies in the component: from each vertex, at most $M+\ell$
applications of $F$ reach $Z$.  There are at most $K$ options per
component, so everything is polynomial in the size of $F$, $K$ and $m$.

Soundness.  A certificate of type (i) works by \cref{lem:ckpt-long}.
For type (ii), the listed components are distinct, since their cycles are
disjoint.  In each, a packing of $b-1$ paths gives
$1+\tau_K(Q-c)\geq b$, and a packing of $b$ paths gives
$\tau_{r_Z}(Q-Z)\geq b$.  Every feasible set falls under one of the
options, as in the proof of \cref{lem:component-formula}, so
$\mathrm{opt}(Q)\geq b$.  Summing over components gives
$\mathrm{opt}_K(F)\geq m+1$.

Completeness.  Suppose $\mathrm{opt}_K(F)>m$ and no certificate of type
(i) exists.  Then every orbit has fewer than $M$ distinct states, so
every cycle has length less than $M$ and every depth is less than $M$.
Choose components with $\mathrm{opt}(Q)\geq1$ until their optima sum to
at least $m+1$; at most $m+1$ are needed.  Set $b=\mathrm{opt}(Q)$,
lowering the last value so that the sum is exactly $m+1$.  Each option of
\cref{lem:component-formula} has value at least $\mathrm{opt}(Q)\geq b$,
and the maximum packings of \cref{lem:forest-duality} supply the paths.
Shortening every path to exactly $r$ of its end keeps them disjoint and
required, with at most $K$ states each.

\emph{Hardness.}  Reduce from the tautology problem for Boolean circuits
$\varphi$ on $n'$ variables, with $K=2$.  Use states $(y,j)$ with
$y\in\bits^{n'}$ and $j\in\{0,1,2\}$, encoded in $n'+2$ bits; the unused
encodings are fixed points.  Put $F(y,j)=(y,j)$ if $\varphi(y)=1$ or
$j=2$, and $F(y,j)=(y,j+1)$ otherwise.  If $\varphi(y)=0$, then
$(y,0),(y,1)$ is a $2$-trajectory.  If $\varphi$ is a tautology, every
state is fixed, so there is no $2$-trajectory.  Hence
$\mathrm{opt}_2(F)=0$ exactly when $\varphi$ is a tautology.  For fixed
$m\geq1$, add a flag bit and, on the flagged states, $m$ disjoint cycles
of length $2$, all other flagged states being fixed.  Each new cycle is a
$2$-trajectory and one point per cycle meets all of its trajectories, so
$\mathrm{opt}_2$ increases by exactly $m$.  The circuit for $F$ has size
polynomial in that of $\varphi$.

If $\mathrm{CKPT}$ were $\Sigma_2^p$-hard, then
$\Sigma_2^p\subseteq\mathsf{coNP}$, hence $\mathsf{NP}\subseteq
\mathsf{coNP}$ and $\mathsf{NP}=\mathsf{coNP}$.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}\label[corollary]{cor:ckpt-approx}
For every function $\alpha\geq1$, computing a number in
$[\mathrm{opt}_K(F),\alpha\cdot\mathrm{opt}_K(F)]$ from a circuit for $F$
and $K$ in unary is $\mathsf{coNP}$-hard.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
Such a number is $0$ exactly when $\mathrm{opt}_K(F)=0$, which is the
$\mathsf{coNP}$-hard case $m=0$ of \cref{thm:ckpt-conp}.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[Search]\label[proposition]{prop:ckpt-search}
Given a circuit for $F$ and $K,m$ in unary with $\mathrm{opt}_K(F)\leq m$,
the value $\mathrm{opt}_K(F)$ and a minimum $K$-checkpoint set are
computable in polynomial time with an $\mathsf{NP}$ oracle.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
The value takes $m+1$ queries to the $\mathsf{coNP}$ language of
\cref{thm:ckpt-conp}.  For the set, put $M=(m+1)K$; by
\cref{lem:ckpt-long}, every orbit has fewer than $M$ distinct states.
Membership of a state in the component of a known cycle is therefore
decidable in polynomial time, and each search below is an $\mathsf{NP}$
question whose witness is found bit by bit.

Find the components that contain a $K$-trajectory one at a time: ask for
a $K$-trajectory whose orbit reaches none of the cycles found so far, and
iterate it to find its cycle.  Each such component has
$\mathrm{opt}(Q)\geq1$, so there are at most $m$ of them, and the other
components need no point.

For each such component and each option of \cref{lem:component-formula},
compute a minimum cover of the in-forest $(Y,\sigma,r)$ of that option as
follows.  Call a vertex \emph{violated} if some required path ending at
it avoids the current set $G$.  Repeatedly find a violated vertex, then
move to a violated proper ancestor while one exists, and add the final
vertex to $G$.  Each move strictly increases the depth, which stays
below $M$, so each round makes fewer than $M$ moves.  Stop when no vertex is violated,
or as soon as $|G|$ exceeds $m$, in which case the option is not optimal.
Output the best option over all components.

This rule produces a minimum cover.  Each added vertex $v$ has a
witness: a required path $W_v$ ending at $v$ that avoided $G$ when $v$ was
added.  Suppose two witnesses share a vertex $u$.  Their ends lie on the
forward path of $u$; call the earlier one $a$ and the later one $b$, so
$a$ is a proper ancestor of $b$ and $a\in W_b$.  If $a$ was added before
$b$, then $W_b$ did not avoid $G$ when $b$ was added.  If $a$ was added
after $b$, then $W_a$ avoided $G$ already when $b$ was added, since $G$
only grows; so $a$ was a violated proper ancestor of $b$, contradicting
the choice of $b$.  So the witnesses are disjoint, and
$|G|\leq\nu_r=\tau_r$.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Maps given as tables}

\begin{theorem}\label{thm:ckpt-table}
Given $F$ as a table of $N$ entries and $K\geq1$, a minimum
$K$-checkpoint set can be computed in time
\[
  O\Bigl(N+\sum_Z \ell_Z\min(K,\ell_Z)\Bigr)\subseteq O(NK)
\]
on a word RAM, where $Z$ ranges over the cycles and $\ell_Z$ is the length
of $Z$.  On an in-forest with an arbitrary requirement, time $O(N)$
suffices.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
For an in-forest, run the greedy rule of \cref{lem:forest-duality},
processing the vertices by decreasing distance to their root, which is
computed by one traversal.  Ancestors lie strictly farther from the root.

For a general $F$, find the cycles and the depths of all tree vertices
in time $O(N)$.  Let $T$ be the in-forest of tree vertices, with
$\sigma(v)=F(v)$ unless $F(v)$ is a cycle vertex, and requirement $K$.

\emph{Step 1.}  Run the greedy rule on $T$, in decreasing order of depth,
obtaining $G$.  Put $\bar d(u)=0$ for $u\in G$ and $\bar d(u)=d(u)$
otherwise.  So $\bar d(u)$ is the largest length of a path of $T$ ending
at $u$ that avoids $G$, and $\bar d(u)\leq K-1$.

\emph{Step 2.}  For each cycle $Z=(v_0,\ldots,v_{\ell-1})$ and each $i$,
let $a_i$ be the maximum of $\bar d(u)$ over tree vertices $u$ with
$F(u)=v_i$, or $0$ if there is none.  If $w_i=K-a_i\leq\ell$, record the
arc $I_i=\{v_i,v_{i+1},\ldots,v_{i+w_i-1}\}$, indices mod $\ell$.

\emph{Step 3.}  On each cycle, compute a minimum set $P_Z$ of cycle
vertices meeting all recorded arcs.  Some arc $I_s$ of least width must
contain a point of every feasible $P_Z$.  For each $p\in I_s$, discard
the arcs containing $p$.  Cut the circle at $p$, which turns the
remaining arcs into intervals on a line, and run the classical line
greedy: scan intervals by right endpoint, choosing the right endpoint of
each interval not yet met.  Take the best $1+(\text{greedy size})$ over
$p\in I_s$.  With bucket sorting each cut costs $O(\ell)$, and
$|I_s|\leq\min(K,\ell)$.

Output $G\cup\bigcup_ZP_Z$.  We show that this is optimal.

\emph{Feasibility.}  Every $K$-trajectory contained in $T$ is a required
path of $T$, so it meets $G$ (\cref{lem:forest-duality}).  Any other
$K$-trajectory avoiding $G$ starts at some $x$, runs through a tail
$x,\ldots,F^{a-1}(x)$ of $a\leq K-1$ tree vertices avoiding $G$, enters
the cycle at $v_i$, and continues through the $K-a\leq\ell$ distinct
vertices $v_i,\ldots,v_{i+K-a-1}$.  If $a\geq1$ the tail ends at a tree
vertex $u$ with $F(u)=v_i$, so $a\leq\bar d(u)\leq a_i$.  Hence the arc
$I_i$ is recorded, lies inside the trajectory, and is met by $P_Z$.

\emph{Every recorded arc forces a point.}  The arc $I_i$ is the cycle
part of a $K$-trajectory avoiding $G$.  If $a_i\geq1$, prefix $I_i$ with
a path of $a_i$ tree vertices that ends at a preimage of $v_i$ and avoids
$G$.  If $a_i=0$, the trajectory starting at $v_i$ is $I_i$ itself.

\emph{Some minimum set contains $G$.}  Let the picks of Step~1 be
$g_1,g_2,\ldots$ in processing order, and suppose a minimum set $S$
contains $g_1,\ldots,g_{j-1}$.  The witness $W$ of $g_j$ is a
$K$-trajectory with $W\cap G=\{g_j\}$, so $S$ contains some $u\in W$.
Replace $u$ by $g_j$.  A $K$-trajectory met by $u$ but not by $g_j$
follows $W$ from $u$ and stops before $g_j$.  So it is a path of $T$ of
length $K$ ending at an ancestor $e$ of $g_j$, and it contains a pick
$g$.  Its vertices are ancestors of $e$ or $e$ itself, so $g$ was
processed before $g_j$ and lies in $S$, and $g\neq u$ because $u\notin
G$.  The new set is therefore still feasible.

\emph{Optimality.}  Let $S\supseteq G$ be minimum.  Replace each tree
vertex $u\in S\setminus G$ by the cycle vertex at which the orbit of $u$
enters its cycle.  A $K$-trajectory met by $u$ either lies in $T$, and is
met by $G$, or reaches the cycle, which it enters at that vertex.  So the
new set is feasible, no larger, and of the form $G\cup P$ with $P$ on the
cycles.  By the previous paragraph $P\cap Z$ meets every recorded arc of
$Z$.  Hence $|S|\geq|G|+\sum_Z|P_Z|$.

The cost is $O(N)$ for Steps~1 and~2 and $O(\ell_Z\min(K,\ell_Z))$ for
Step~3 on each cycle.
\end{proof}

For a map given by a small circuit the components can be exponentially
large.  In \cref{thm:ckpt-conp}, duality replaces the existential
quantifier over $H$ by packings, which certify lower bounds.  The
constructions of this paper need no such optimization:
\cref{lem:checkpoints} is within a factor of two of the worst case, and
that factor only changes constants.
The undirected relative, the $k$-path vertex cover problem, is
$\mathsf{NP}$-hard in general graphs and linear on
trees~\cite{BKKS11}.  The out-degree-one structure is what makes
duality and the polynomial table algorithm available here.
The script \texttt{verify\_checkpoint\_sets.py} compares
\cref{lem:forest-duality,lem:component-formula,thm:ckpt-table} with brute
force on small random maps.

\section{A matching exponent for arbitrary fixed powers}\label{sec:lower}

This section gives an explicit linear-size permutation whose collection
of powers is large enough that some member has circuit complexity at
least $2^{n/2}/\poly(n)$.  The counting argument is elementary and does
not require an asymptotic estimate for the maximal order of a permutation.

\subsection{A permutation with cycles of all lengths up to \texorpdfstring{$2^{n/2}-1$}{2 to the power n/2, minus 1}}

Let $n=2k$ and $B=2^k-1$.  Write a state as a pair of $k$-bit integers
$(a,b)$.  Define
\begin{equation}\label{eq:permutation}
 P_n(a,b)=
 \begin{cases}
   (a,b+1\bmod a),&1\leq a\leq B\ \text{and}\ 0\leq b<a,\\
   (a,b),&\text{otherwise}.
 \end{cases}
\end{equation}
For each $1\leq a\leq B$, the states
$(a,0),(a,1),\ldots,(a,a-1)$ form one cycle of length $a$; every other
state is fixed.  Hence $P_n$ is a permutation and
\begin{equation}\label{eq:order}
  \ord(P_n)=D_B:=\lcm(1,2,\ldots,B).
\end{equation}

Despite the notation in \cref{eq:permutation}, the update does not require
a general division circuit.  Compare $b$ with $a$, increment $b$, and,
when $b<a$, replace $b$ by zero exactly when the increment equals $a$.
Otherwise retain the appropriate increment or original value.
Comparison, increment, and selection each have $O(k)$-gate circuits.
The first coordinate is passed through unchanged.  Thus
\begin{equation}\label{eq:linear-update}
  \CC(P_n)=O(n),
\end{equation}
and the family has a direct polynomial-time circuit construction.

\begin{theorem}[Powers and fixed-integer remainders]\label{thm:remainders}
For a fixed $T\geq0$, define $r_T(a)=T\bmod a$ when $a>0$ and
$r_T(0)=0$, using $k$ bits for both input and output.  Then
\[
  \CC(r_T)\leq\CC(P_n^T)\leq\CC(r_T)+O(n).
\]
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Restrict the power circuit to inputs $(a,0)$ and retain its second
coordinate.  This computes $r_T$, including the zero-modulus branch.
Conversely, on an active state compute $b+r_T(a)$ using $k+1$ bits.
Both summands are below $a$, so a comparison and at most one subtraction
of $a$ give the result modulo $a$.  The spare bit retains the carry.
Select the original $b$ on inactive states and copy $a$ to the output.
All additional operations have linear-size Boolean circuits.  The first
inequality uses the paper's free constant-wire convention; the native
Lean model charges one shared zero gate for the restriction.
\end{proof}

Thus the arithmetic object behind the lower bound is the remainder of a
single fixed integer as a function of its input modulus.  The theorem
does not identify a particular hard integer.

\begin{lemma}\label[lemma]{lem:lcm}
For every integer $B\geq 1$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:lcm-lower}
  \lcm(1,2,\ldots,B)\geq \frac{2^B}{B+1}.
\end{equation}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
We show that every binomial coefficient $\binom Bj$ divides $D_B$.
For a prime $p$, factorial valuations give
\[
 \nu_p\!\binom Bj
 =\sum_{a\geq 1}
   \left(\floor{\frac{B}{p^a}}-
         \floor{\frac{j}{p^a}}-
         \floor{\frac{B-j}{p^a}}\right).
\]
Each summand is zero or one, and it is zero when $p^a>B$.
Therefore
\[
  \nu_p\!\binom Bj\leq \floor{\log_p B}=\nu_p(D_B),
\]
where both sides are zero for $p>B$.  This proves divisibility.
Since $\sum_{j=0}^B\binom Bj=2^B$, some binomial coefficient is at least
$2^B/(B+1)$, and $D_B$ is at least that large.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Counting multi-output circuits]\label[lemma]{lem:count}
The number of functions $\bits^n\to\bits^n$ computable with at most $q$
fan-in-two Boolean gates is at most
\begin{equation}\label{eq:count}
  (q+1)16^q(n+q+2)^{2q+n}
  =2^{O((q+n)\log(n+q+2))}.
\end{equation}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Topologically order the gates.  At each gate choose one of at most $16$
Boolean operations and two predecessors among the inputs, the two
constants, and gates.  Allowing all $n+q+2$ possible predecessors at each
position only increases the count.  Choose the $n$ output wires from
these same possibilities.  Sum over gate counts from zero through $q$;
each summand is bounded by the expression without its factor $q+1$.
Different encodings may compute the same function, which only helps the
upper bound.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Hard powers of a simple permutation]\label{thm:lower}
For all sufficiently large even $n$, the permutation in
\cref{eq:permutation} satisfies
\begin{equation}\label{eq:hard-power}
  \max_{T\geq 0}\CC(P_n^T)
    =\Omega\!\left(\frac{2^{n/2}}{n}\right).
\end{equation}
Together with \cref{thm:main}, this gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:tight-exponent}
  \log_2\!\left(\max_{T\geq 0}\CC(P_n^T)\right)
    =\frac n2+O(\log n).
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
There are exactly $D_B$ distinct powers of $P_n$, with representatives
$0\leq T<D_B$.  By \cref{lem:lcm},
\[
  \log_2 D_B\geq B-\log_2(B+1).
\]
Let $q=\floor{cB/n}$ for a sufficiently small positive absolute constant
$c$.  Since $B=2^{n/2}-1$, \cref{lem:count} bounds the logarithm of the
number of circuits of size at most $q$ by
\[
  O((q+n)\log(n+q+2))=O(cB+n^2).
\]
Choose $c$ so that the coefficient of $B$ is less than $1/2$.
For all sufficiently large even $n$, this quantity is smaller than
$B-\log_2(B+1)$.  Thus there are fewer size-$q$ circuits than distinct
powers of $P_n$.  At least one power has complexity greater than $q$,
proving \cref{eq:hard-power}.

For the upper bound, substitute $\CC(P_n)=O(n)$ into
\cref{eq:optimized}.  This gives
$\max_T\CC(P_n^T)=O(n^3 2^{n/2})$.  Combining the upper and lower
bounds and taking logarithms proves \cref{eq:tight-exponent}.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Almost every power is hard]\label{thm:typical-powers}
There is an absolute $c>0$ such that, for all sufficiently large even $n$,
\[
 \Pr_{T\sim\mathrm{Unif}\{0,\ldots,D_B-1\}}
 \left[\CC(P_n^T)\geq c\frac{2^{n/2}}n\right]
 \geq 1-2^{-\Omega(2^{n/2})}.
\]
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Set $U=2^{n/2}$.  The preceding counting argument bounds the number of
powers with circuits of size at most $\floor{cU/n}$ by $2^{\alpha U}$,
for some fixed $\alpha<1$ after decreasing $c$.  On the other hand,
$D_B\geq2^{U-1}/U$.  Divide the two counts to bound the exceptional
fraction by $U2^{1-(1-\alpha)U}=2^{-\Omega(U)}$.
\end{proof}

The probability concerns exponent residues modulo the whole group order.
It does not concern short exponent descriptions or average-case hardness
over inputs to any one power.  In particular, easy powers remain among
the exceptional residues.

\begin{corollary}\label[corollary]{cor:one-output}
For all sufficiently large even $n$, some power of $P_n$ has an output
coordinate of circuit complexity $\Omega(2^{n/2}/n^2)$.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
For the power supplied by \cref{thm:lower}, join optimal circuits for all
$n$ output coordinates.  Their total size is at most $n$ times the
largest coordinate complexity and is an upper bound on the complexity
of the whole map.
\end{proof}

\subsection{The exact constant and average-case behaviour}\label{sec:exact-constant}

\Cref{thm:lower,thm:typical-powers} determine $\max_T\CC(P_n^T)$ up to a
polynomial factor.  This subsection determines its leading constant and
describes how well a fixed power can be approximated on most states.
The results refine the mechanized statements above but are proved here
in prose only; they are not covered by the Lean formalization.  Throughout,
$n=2k$, $B=2^k-1$, and $\pi(x)$ is the number of primes at most $x$.

Two classical estimates replace \cref{lem:lcm,lem:count}.  First,
$\ln D_B=\psi(B)=\sum_{p^j\leq B}\ln p$ is Chebyshev's function, so the
prime number theorem with the de la Vall\'ee Poussin error
term~\cite{MV07} gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:lcm-pnt}
  \log_2 D_B=\frac{B}{\ln 2}\bigl(1+O(e^{-c\sqrt k})\bigr)
\end{equation}
for an absolute $c>0$; similarly $\pi(x)=(1+O(1/\ln x))\,x/\ln x$ and,
by Mertens' second theorem,
$\sum_{p\leq x}1/p=\ln\ln x+M+O(1/\ln x)$ for a constant $M$.
Second, the circuit count of \cref{lem:count} can be sharpened by the
classical symmetry argument~\cite{Shannon49,Jukna12}.

\begin{lemma}[Sharp circuit count]\label[lemma]{lem:sharp-count}
For $q\geq1$, the number of functions $\bits^\ell\to\bits^m$ with circuit
complexity at most $q$ is at most
\[
  (q+1)\left(\frac{16e(\ell+q+2)^2}{q}\right)^{q}(\ell+q+2)^m.
\]
In particular, for $q\geq\ell$ its base-two logarithm is
$q\log_2q+O(q)+O(m\log_2 q)$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Let $\CC(f)=s$ and fix a circuit for $f$ with exactly $s$ gates.  Its gates
compute pairwise distinct functions, none equal to an input variable or a
constant.  Indeed, if a gate $g$ computes the same function as an input or
constant wire, redirect every use of $g$, including output designations,
to that wire and delete $g$.  If two gates $g,h$ compute the same
function and $h$ is not an ancestor of $g$, redirect every use of $h$ to
$g$ and delete $h$; since $g$ does not depend on $h$, the result is still
acyclic.  Either operation contradicts minimality.

A \emph{description} with $s$ labelled gates lists, for each label, one of
$16$ operations and two predecessors among the $\ell$ inputs, two
constants, and $s$ labels, and then lists $m$ output wires from the same
$\ell+s+2$ possibilities.  There are at most
$(16(\ell+s+2)^2)^s(\ell+s+2)^m$ descriptions.  Each of the $s!$ ways to
label a minimum circuit for $f$ gives a description of a circuit computing
$f$.  These descriptions are distinct: the description determines the
function computed at each label, and because gate functions are pairwise
distinct this determines the labelling.  Descriptions of circuits for
different functions are different.  Hence the number of $f$ with
$\CC(f)=s$ is at most $(16(\ell+s+2)^2)^s(\ell+s+2)^m/s!$.  Use
$s!\geq(s/e)^s$, bound $\ell+s+2\leq\ell+q+2$, and note that
$s\mapsto(c/s)^s$ increases on $1\leq s\leq c/e$, where here
$c=16e(\ell+q+2)^2>eq$.  Summing over $0\leq s\leq q$ gives the bound.
For $q\geq\ell$, $(\ell+q+2)^2/q\leq 16q$, which gives the logarithmic
form.
\end{proof}

\Cref{lem:count} omits the factor $1/s!$ and therefore charges about
$2q\log_2q$ bits to a size-$q$ circuit instead of $q\log_2 q$, and
\cref{lem:lcm} gives $\log_2D_B\geq B-O(k)$ instead of $(1+o(1))B/\ln2$.
Each loss costs a constant factor in \cref{thm:lower}.  With both
estimates sharpened, the counting bound meets a matching construction.

\begin{theorem}[Exact constant for powers of $P_n$]\label{thm:exact-constant}
For every $T\geq0$,
\[
  \CC(r_T)\leq\Bigl(1+O\bigl(\tfrac{\log k}{k}\bigr)\Bigr)\frac{2^k}{k\ln2}.
\]
For all but a $2^{-\Omega(2^k/k)}$ fraction of $T\in\{0,\ldots,D_B-1\}$,
\[
  \CC(r_T)\geq\Bigl(1-O\bigl(\tfrac1k\bigr)\Bigr)\frac{2^k}{k\ln2}.
\]
Consequently, by \cref{thm:remainders},
\[
  \max_{T\geq0}\CC(P_n^T)
   =\Bigl(\frac{2}{\ln2}+O\bigl(\tfrac{\log n}{n}\bigr)\Bigr)\frac{2^{n/2}}{n},
\]
and the lower bound holds for almost every $T$ in the same sense.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
\emph{Lower bound.}  The maps $r_T$ for $0\leq T<D_B$ are pairwise
distinct, since $r_T$ determines $T\bmod a$ for every $a\leq B$ and hence
$T\bmod D_B$.  Put $\Lambda=\log_2D_B$ and
$q=\floor{(1-C/k)\Lambda/k}$ for an absolute constant $C$.  By
\cref{eq:lcm-pnt}, $\log_2 q\leq k+1$ for large $k$, so by
\cref{lem:sharp-count} with $\ell=m=k$ the number of maps of complexity at
most $q$ is at most
\[
  2^{q(k+1)+c_1q+c_2k^2}
  \leq 2^{\Lambda(1-C/k)(1+(1+c_1)/k)+c_2k^2}
  \leq 2^{\Lambda-\Lambda/k}
\]
once $C\geq c_1+3$ and $k$ is large, because $\Lambda/k$ grows like
$2^k/k$.  Thus at most a $2^{-\Lambda/k}$ fraction of the $D_B$ residues
$T$ have $\CC(r_T)\leq q$.  Finally
$q=(1-O(1/k))2^k/(k\ln2)$ by \cref{eq:lcm-pnt}.

\emph{Upper bound.}  Let $s_0=\floor{\sqrt B}$ and call a prime
\emph{large} if it exceeds $s_0$.  Every $1\leq a\leq B$ factors uniquely as
$a=a_sp$, where $a_s$ has no large prime factor and $p$ is either $1$ or a
large prime: two large prime factors would give $a\geq(s_0+1)^2>B$.
Then $\gcd(a_s,p)=1$.  The circuit has four stages.

\emph{(i) Smooth part.}  Start with $(A,r)=(1,0)$.  For each prime
$d\leq s_0$ in turn, compute $e=\nu_d(a)$ by testing $d^j\mid a$ for
$j\leq\floor{\log_dB}$, select the hardwired constants $d^e$ and
$T\bmod d^e$ with a multiplexer over at most $k+1$ entries, and replace
$(A,r)$ by $(Ad^e,\rho)$, where $\rho<Ad^e$ satisfies $\rho\equiv r\pmod A$
and $\rho\equiv T\pmod{d^e}$.  The Chinese remainder step uses an inverse
of $A$ modulo $d^e$, computed by an extended-Euclid circuit.  All numbers
are below $2^k$, so each prime costs $\poly(k)$ gates, and there are
$\pi(s_0)\leq2^{k/2}$ primes.  At the end $A=a_s$ and $r=T\bmod a_s$.
One division gives $p=a/A$.

\emph{(ii) Index of the large prime.}  Let $L=2^{\ceil{k/2}}$ and
$h=\floor{p/L}$, the top $\floor{k/2}$ bits of $p$.  Read
$\pi(hL-1)$ from a hardwired table with $\floor{k/2}$ input bits, using
$O(k2^{k/2})$ gates.  Add the number of primes in $[hL,p)$ using $L$
primality circuits of size $\poly(k)$ and a running counter.  Such
circuits exist by the AKS algorithm~\cite{AKS04} and the Pippenger--Fischer
simulation~\cite{PF79}, or nonuniformly by Miller--Rabin with
$O(k)$ hardwired bases chosen by Adleman's argument~\cite{Adleman78}.
Subtracting $\pi(s_0)$ yields the index $\iota(p)\in[0,M)$ of $p$ among
the large primes, where $M=\pi(B)-\pi(s_0)$.  This stage costs
$2^{k/2}\poly(k)$ gates.

\emph{(iii) Residue table.}  Let $G(i)=T\bmod p_i$, where $p_i$ is the
large prime of index $i$.  Write $M=\sum_{t\in S}2^t$ in binary; this
partitions $[0,M)$ into aligned dyadic blocks $I_t$ of length $2^t$, one
for each $t\in S$, and membership of $i$ in $I_t$ is an equality test on
the bits of $i$ above position $t$.  On $I_t$, each of the $k$ output bits
of $G$ is a total function of the low $t$ bits of $i$.  Compute it with
Lupanov's bound $\CC\leq(1+\alpha_t)2^t/t$, where
$\alpha_t=O(\log t/t)$~\cite{Lupanov58,Jukna12}, and with an $O(2^t/t)$
bound for small $t$.  An AND--OR selection combines the blocks with
$O(k^2)$ gates.  Let $j=\floor{\log_2M}$ and $t_0=j-\ceil{\log_2 j}$.
Blocks with $t\geq t_0$ cost at most
$k(1+\sup_{t\geq t_0}\alpha_t)M/t_0$, and blocks with $t<t_0$ cost
$O(k2^{t_0}/t_0)=O(kM/j^2)$.  By the prime number theorem,
$M=(1+O(1/k))2^k/(k\ln2)$, so $j=k-\log_2k+O(1)$,
$t_0=k-O(\log k)$, and this stage costs $(1+O(\log k/k))M$ gates.

\emph{(iv) Combination.}  If $a=0$, output $0$.  If $p=1$, output $r$.
Otherwise output the $\rho<a$ with $\rho\equiv r\pmod{a_s}$ and
$\rho\equiv G(\iota(p))\pmod p$, again by a $\poly(k)$-size
Chinese remainder circuit.  On $a=1$ stage (i) gives $a_s=1$, $p=1$ and
$r=0$, as required.  Values computed on discarded branches are ignored.

The total is $(1+O(\log k/k))2^k/(k\ln2)+2^{k/2}\poly(k)$, which proves
the upper bound.  Substituting $k=n/2$ and using \cref{thm:remainders},
whose additive $O(n)$ is negligible, gives the statement for $P_n^T$.
\end{proof}

The primes in $(\sqrt B,B]$ carry the leading-order independent
information about $T$.  The residues modulo the prime powers $d^e\leq B$
with $d\leq\sqrt B$ are further independent data, stored separately in
stage~(i), but they occupy only $2^{k/2}\poly(k)$ bits; every other
residue is reconstructed from these two kinds by the Chinese remainder
theorem.  Storing $T\bmod p$ in $k$ bits instead of $\log_2p$ bits wastes
only a $1+O(1/k)$ factor, because most large primes lie near $B$.  This is
why the two bounds agree in their leading constant
$2/\ln2\approx2.885$.  The construction improves the general bound of
\cref{thm:main}, which gives $O(n^32^{n/2})$ for this family, by a
factor of order $n^4$.

\paragraph{Approximation on most states.}
For a map $g$ on $\bits^n$, let $\operatorname{err}_T(g)$ be the fraction of
states $x\in\bits^n$ with $g(x)\neq P_n^T(x)$.  Hardness of $P_n^T$
does not survive this relaxation by much: every power can be approximated
by circuits a polynomial factor below the worst case, with error
$\Theta(\log n/n)$, and this is sharp for random powers.

\begin{proposition}[Every power is approximable]\label[proposition]{prop:approx-all}
Fix $c>1$.  For every $T$ there is a circuit of size
$(1+o(1))2^k/k^c$ with
\[
  \operatorname{err}_T\leq(1+o(1))\frac{(c-1)\log_2k}{2k}.
\]
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Let $y$ be the least integer with $\pi(y)-\pi(s_0)\geq2^k/k^c$; by the
prime number theorem, $\log_2y=k-(c-1)\log_2k+O(1)$, and $y>\sqrt B$ for
large $k$.  Run the circuit of \cref{thm:exact-constant}, but place in
stage (iii) only the large primes at most $y$.  Its cost is
$(1+O(\log k/k))2^k/k^c+2^{k/2}\poly(k)$.  Wrap it as in
\cref{thm:remainders}, and output the input unchanged whenever $p>y$.
The result is correct on every inactive state and on every active state
$(a,b)$ whose $a$ has no prime factor in $(y,B]$.  Since $y\geq\sqrt B$,
such an $a$ has exactly one such factor $p$, and $a=pu$ with
$u\leq\floor{B/p}$.  The number of wrong states is therefore at most
\[
  \sum_{y<p\leq B}\ \sum_{u\leq\floor{B/p}}pu
  \leq\frac{B^2}2\sum_{y<p\leq B}\frac1p+\frac{B\pi(B)}2.
\]
By Mertens' theorem the sum of reciprocals is
$\ln(\ln B/\ln y)+O(1/k)=(1+o(1))(c-1)\log_2k/k$, and
$B\pi(B)=O(4^k/k)$.  Dividing by $2^n=4^k$ gives the claim.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Random powers are hard on average]\label{thm:random-average}
Fix $c>1$ and let $q=\floor{2^k/k^c}$.  For $T$ uniform in
$\{0,\ldots,D_B-1\}$, with probability $1-2^{-\Omega(qk)}$ every map $g$
with $\CC(g)\leq q$ satisfies
\[
  \operatorname{err}_T(g)\geq(1-o(1))\frac{(c-1)\log_2k}{2k}.
\]
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Put $K=k^{c-1}$, $y=B/K$, and let $\mathcal P$ be the set of primes in
$(y,B]$.  For $p\in\mathcal P$ let $C_p$ be the set of active states
$(a,b)$ with $p\mid a$, and $W_p=|C_p|$.  Since $y\geq\sqrt B$ for large
$k$, the sets $C_p$ are disjoint, and $W_p\leq B(K+1)/2$ because
$\floor{B/p}<K$.

Fix $g$.  At an active state $(a,b)$, $P_n^T(a,b)=(a,(b+T)\bmod a)$.  If
$g(a,b)=(a,v)$ with $v<a$, correctness at $(a,b)$ forces
$T\equiv v-b\pmod a$, and in particular fixes $T\bmod p$ for $p\mid a$;
otherwise $g$ is wrong at $(a,b)$.  So for each $p\in\mathcal P$ and each
residue $\rho$ modulo $p$, let $Y_p(\rho)$ be the number of states in $C_p$
whose forced residue modulo $p$ is $\rho$.  The number of correct states
in $C_p$ is at most $Y_p(T\bmod p)$, and $\sum_\rho Y_p(\rho)\leq W_p$.
Call $p$ \emph{good} if $Y_p(T\bmod p)>\varepsilon W_p$.  Fewer than
$1/\varepsilon$ residues are good, and the residues $T\bmod p$ for
$p\in\mathcal P$ are independent and uniform, since each such $p$ divides
$D_B$ exactly once.  Hence the events ``$p$ good'' are independent, each
of probability at most $1/(\varepsilon y)=K/(\varepsilon B)$.  If fewer
than $2q$ primes are good, then $g$ errs on at least
$(1-\varepsilon)(W(\mathcal P)-2q\max_pW_p)$ states, where
$W(\mathcal P)=\sum_{p\in\mathcal P}W_p$.

Using $|\mathcal P|\leq\pi(B)\leq 2B/k$ for large $k$, the probability that
at least $2q$ primes are good is at most
\[
  \binom{|\mathcal P|}{2q}\Bigl(\frac{K}{\varepsilon B}\Bigr)^{2q}
  \leq\Bigl(\frac{eK}{\varepsilon kq}\Bigr)^{2q}
  =2^{-2q(k-O(\log k)-\log_2(1/\varepsilon))}.
\]
By \cref{lem:sharp-count} with $\ell=m=n$, at most
$2^{q(k-c\log_2k)+O(q)}$ maps have $\CC(g)\leq q$.  Take
$\varepsilon=1/\log_2k$.  A union bound shows that, with probability
$1-2^{-\Omega(qk)}$, fewer than $2q$ primes are good for every such $g$.

On that event every such $g$ errs on at least
$(1-\varepsilon)(W(\mathcal P)-qB(K+1))$ states.  Since
$\floor{B/p}\geq B/p-1$, we have $W_p\geq(B^2/p-B)/2$, so by Mertens'
theorem
\[
  W(\mathcal P)\geq\frac{B^2}2\Bigl(\ln\frac{\ln B}{\ln y}-O\bigl(\tfrac1k\bigr)\Bigr)
     -\frac{B\pi(B)}2
   =(1-o(1))\frac{B^2}2\cdot\frac{(c-1)\log_2k}{k}.
\]
Moreover $qB(K+1)=O(B^2/k)$, which is of smaller order.  Dividing by
$4^k$ completes the proof.
\end{proof}

Together, \cref{prop:approx-all,thm:random-average} show that the optimal
error at size $2^k/k^c$ is $(1+o(1))(c-1)\log_2k/(2k)$ for almost every
power: constant average-case hardness, i.e.\ a constant lower bound on
the approximation error, is impossible at size $2^{n/2}/\poly(n)$.  The
same arguments give constant average-case hardness at somewhat smaller
sizes.

\begin{corollary}[Constant average-case hardness at size \texorpdfstring{$2^{(1-\delta)k}$}{2 to the (1-delta)k}]\label[corollary]{cor:constant-error}
Fix $0<\delta\leq1/3$ and let $q=2^{(1-\delta)k}$.  Every power has a
circuit of size $(1+o(1))q$ with error at most
$\frac12\ln\frac1{1-\delta}+o(1)$, and for almost every $T$ every circuit
of size at most $q$ has error at least $\frac12\ln\frac1{1-\delta}-o(1)$,
