with probability $1-o(1)$ over uniform $T\in\{0,\ldots,D_B-1\}$.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
For the upper bound, repeat the proof of \cref{prop:approx-all} with $y$
the least integer such that $\pi(y)-\pi(s_0)\geq(1-\delta)q$.  Stage (iii)
then has $t_0=(1-\delta)k-O(\log k)$, so its $k$ output bits cost
$(1+o(1))k(1-\delta)q/t_0=(1+o(1))q$ gates; stages (i) and (ii) cost
$2^{k/2}\poly(k)=o(q)$ because $\delta<1/2$.  Moreover
$\ln y=(1-\delta)k\ln2+O(\log k)$ and $y\geq\sqrt B$.  For the lower bound, repeat the proof of
\cref{thm:random-average} with $\log_2K=\delta k-\sqrt k$ and fixed
small $\varepsilon$.  The union bound requires
$2(\log_2q-\log_2K)>(1+o(1))\log_2q$, that is,
$\log_2K<(1-\delta)k/2-\omega(\log k)$, which holds because
$\delta\leq(1-\delta)/2$.  The subtracted term $qBK$ is
$o(B^2)$ because $\log_2K<\delta k-\omega(1)$, and $y=B/K\geq\sqrt B$.
In both bounds the error is $\frac12\ln(\ln B/\ln y)+o(1)$ by Mertens'
theorem; letting $\varepsilon\to0$ slowly gives the statement.
\end{proof}

For $\delta>1/3$ the two arguments no longer meet, and for $\delta>1/2$
the moduli may have two prime factors above $y$; we do not pursue these
ranges.  The hardness of a typical power of $P_n$ is spread thinly over
the cycles whose length has a prime factor near $B$.  A permutation whose
cycles all have comparable prime lengths would avoid this dilution; we
leave such constructions to future work.

\subsection{Why the lower bound does not apply to short input times}
\label{sec:lower-limits}

The lower bound selects $T$ from $\{0,\ldots,D_B-1\}$; it places no
polynomial bound on the bit length of that exponent.  Indeed,
\cref{lem:lcm} shows that $\log D_B\geq 2^{n/2}-O(n)$.
The choice of a power therefore has exponentially many available
bits.  The theorem does not provide a polynomial-time procedure that,
given $n$, identifies a hard power.

For the very same family, the iterator with an $m$-bit time input has
size $O(n(m+1))$.  On an active cycle,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:easy-iterator}
  P_n^t(a,b)=(a,(b+(t\bmod a))\bmod a).
\end{equation}
An $m$-stage remainder computation with a $k$-bit running remainder costs
$O(mk)$ gates, and the remaining comparison, addition, and selection
cost $O(n)$ gates as in \cref{thm:remainders}.  On inactive states the answer is the
input itself; use a dummy positive modulus when $a=0$.
Consequently, for $m=\poly(n)$ this iterator has polynomial size.

\begin{corollary}[Bit length of hard exponents]\label[corollary]{cor:exponent-length}
Let $T_n$ be exponents satisfying
$\CC(P_n^{T_n})=\Omega(2^{n/2}/n)$ for sufficiently large even $n$.
Their binary lengths
$m_n=\max\{1,\ceil{\log_2(T_n+1)}\}$ satisfy
\[
  m_n=\Omega(2^{n/2}/n^2).
\]
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
Hardwire the $m_n$-bit exponent into the iterator just described.
This gives $\CC(P_n^{T_n})=O(n(m_n+1))$.
Comparing with the assumed lower bound proves the claim.
\end{proof}

Thus the hard powers necessarily require exponentially long binary
exponents; long descriptions are not merely allowed by the counting proof.
Theorem~\ref{thm:lower} establishes sharpness for arbitrary fixed powers.
It gives no hardness result for the bounded-bit-time problem.

\subsection{Explicit hard exponents}\label{sec:explicit-exponents}

By \cref{cor:exponent-length}, a hard power of $P_n$ has an exponent
with $2^{n/2}/\poly(n)$ binary digits.  The natural notion of an explicit
hard power therefore allows time polynomial in that length.  Call a
sequence of exponents $(T_n)_{n\ \mathrm{even}}$ \emph{explicit} if a
deterministic algorithm outputs the binary expansion of $T_n$ from $1^n$
in time $2^{O(n)}$.  Write
$\mathsf{E}=\mathsf{DTIME}(2^{O(m)})$ for languages of strings of
length $m$, and $L_m$ for the length-$m$ slice of a language $L$.

We measure the hardness of a power of $P_n$ on the scale $2^k$, where
$k=n/2$.  This is the natural scale: by \cref{thm:main} every power has
$\CC(P_n^T)\leq 2^{k+O(\log k)}$, just as every $m$-input Boolean
function has circuits of size $O(2^m/m)$.  On these scales, explicit
hard powers and circuit lower bounds for $\mathsf{E}$ are equivalent,
with matching exponents.

\begin{theorem}[Explicit hard powers and lower bounds for
\texorpdfstring{$\mathsf{E}$}{E}]\label{thm:explicit-E}
Let $0<\varepsilon,\delta\leq1$.  Read ``for infinitely many'' in both
hypothesis and conclusion of each part, or ``for all sufficiently
large'' in both.
\begin{enumerate}
\item If explicit exponents $(T_n)$ satisfy
  $\CC(P_n^{T_n})\geq 2^{\varepsilon k}$, $k=n/2$, for infinitely many
  (for all sufficiently large) even $n$, then for every
  $\delta<\varepsilon$ some $L\in\mathsf{E}$ satisfies
  $\CC(L_m)\geq 2^{\delta m}$ for infinitely many (for all sufficiently
  large) $m$.
\item If some $L\in\mathsf{E}$ satisfies $\CC(L_m)\geq 2^{\delta m}$ for
  infinitely many (for all sufficiently large) $m$, then for every
  $\varepsilon<\delta$ there are explicit exponents $(T_n)$ with
  $\CC(P_n^{T_n})\geq 2^{\varepsilon k}$, $k=n/2$, for infinitely many
  (for all sufficiently large) even $n$.
\end{enumerate}
Consequently, the supremum of the $\varepsilon$ attained by explicit
powers of $P_n$ equals the supremum of the $\delta$ for which
$\mathsf{E}$ has a language of circuit complexity $2^{\delta m}$, under
either reading.
\end{theorem}

The second part uses a moment bound for sums of $t$-wise independent
variables.

\begin{lemma}[Bellare and Rompel~{\cite[Lemma~2.3]{BR94}}]
\label[lemma]{lem:br-tail}
Let $t\geq4$ be even, and let $X=\sum_iX_i$ be a finite sum of $t$-wise
independent random variables with values in $[0,1]$ and mean
$\mu=\mathbb{E}X$.  For every $A>0$,
\[
  \Pr\bigl[|X-\mu|\geq A\bigr]
  \leq 8\left(\frac{t\mu+t^2}{A^2}\right)^{t/2}.
\]
In particular, if $\mu\geq t$ then
$\Pr[X<\mu/2]\leq 8(8t/\mu)^{t/2}$.
\end{lemma}

The particular case takes $A=\mu/2$ and uses $4t^2/\mu^2\leq4t/\mu$.

\begin{proof}[Proof of \cref{thm:explicit-E}, part~1]
For $m\geq2$ let $k(m)$ be the largest $k\geq1$ with
$k+\ceil{\log_2k}\leq m$.  Since $k\mapsto k+\ceil{\log_2k}$ is strictly
increasing, every $k\geq1$ equals $k(m)$ for $m=k+\ceil{\log_2k}$, and
$m<(k(m)+1)+\ceil{\log_2(k(m)+1)}$, so $m-k(m)\leq\log_2m+2$.

A string of length $m$ is read as $(a,i,w)$, with $a\in\bits^{k}$ for
$k=k(m)$, $i\in\bits^{\ceil{\log_2 k}}$, and padding $w$.  Let $L$
contain $(a,i,w)$ exactly when $a\geq1$, $i<k$, and bit $i$ of
$T_{2k}\bmod a$ is one.  To decide $L$, compute $k(m)$, compute $T_{2k}$
in time $2^{O(k)}$, and reduce it modulo $a$ by long division in time
$2^{O(k)}$.  Hence $L\in\mathsf{E}$.

Hardwiring $i=0,\ldots,k-1$ and constant padding into $k$ copies of a
circuit for $L_m$ computes $r_{T_{2k}}$ on all $k$-bit inputs, including
$r_{T_{2k}}(0)=0$.  By \cref{thm:remainders},
\[
  k\,\CC(L_m)\geq\CC(r_{T_{2k}})\geq\CC(P_{2k}^{T_{2k}})-O(k).
\]
If $\CC(P_{2k}^{T_{2k}})\geq2^{\varepsilon k}$, then
$\CC(L_m)\geq(2^{\varepsilon k}-O(k))/k$, which is at least $2^{\delta m}$
once $k$ is large, because $m\leq k+\log_2k+3$ and $\delta<\varepsilon$.
For the ``infinitely many'' reading apply this at
$m=k+\ceil{\log_2k}$ for each hard $k$.  For the ``all sufficiently
large'' reading every large $m$ has a large $k(m)$, and the hypothesis
applies at $n=2k(m)$.
\end{proof}

\begin{proof}[Proof of \cref{thm:explicit-E}, part~2]
Fix an even $t\geq4$ with $\delta t/(t+2)>\varepsilon$.

\emph{Parameters.}  For $k\geq2$ let $m(k)$ be the largest integer
$m\geq0$ with
\[
  \varphi_k(m):=m+\ceil{2(m+4)/t+\log_2(16tk)}\leq k-1,
\]
when such an $m$ exists, and put $g=g(k)=k-1-m(k)$.  Then
$g\geq 2(m+4)/t+\log_2(16tk)$; in particular $g\geq1$.  The function
$\varphi_k$ is strictly increasing in $m$, and
$\varphi_k(m)\leq\varphi_{k+1}(m)\leq\varphi_k(m)+1$.  Hence
$\varphi_{k+1}(m(k))\leq k$, so $m(k+1)\geq m(k)$; and
$\varphi_{k+1}(m(k)+2)\geq\varphi_k(m(k)+1)+1\geq k+1$, so
$m(k+1)\leq m(k)+1$.  Thus, once defined, $m(k)$ is nondecreasing,
increases by at most one per step, and takes every sufficiently large
integer value.  Finally
$\varphi_k(m)\leq(1+2/t)m+8/t+\log_2(16tk)+1$ gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:mk-lower}
  m(k)\geq\frac{t}{t+2}\,k-O(\log k).
\end{equation}

\emph{A hashed partition of the $k$-bit integers.}  Every
$p\in[2^{k-1},2^k)$ has a unique representation
$p=2^{k-1}+y2^g+z$ with $0\leq y<2^m$ and $0\leq z<2^g$, because
$m+g=k-1$.  Since $g\geq1$, $p$ is even when $z$ is even; so every
prime in this range has $z$ in the set $Z$ of odd $z<2^g$, and
$|Z|=2^{g-1}$.  Let $w=\max(m,g)$, and identify $\bits^w$ with the field
$\mathrm{GF}(2^w)$ through the lexicographically first irreducible
polynomial of degree $w$, found by exhaustive search in time $2^{O(w)}$.
Embed $z<2^g$ and $y<2^m$ into $\bits^w$ by zero-padding.  For a
polynomial $f$ over $\mathrm{GF}(2^w)$ of degree less than $t$, let
$G_f(z)\in\bits^m$ be the first $m$ bits of $f(z)$, and define the
\emph{class} of $p=2^{k-1}+y2^g+z$ as $r_f(p)=y\oplus G_f(z)\in\bits^m$.
The class $r$ is
\[
  \bigl\{2^{k-1}+(r\oplus G_f(z))2^g+z:0\leq z<2^g\bigr\},
\]
with exactly one element for each $z$, and the $2^m$ classes partition
$[2^{k-1},2^k)$.

Write $\pi(x)$ for the number of primes at most $x$, and put
\[
  \mu=2^{-m}\bigl(\pi(2^k)-\pi(2^{k-1})\bigr),
\]
the average number of primes per class.  By the prime number theorem
(Chebyshev's estimates already suffice), $\pi(2^k)-\pi(2^{k-1})\geq
2^{k-1}/(2k)$ for all large $k$, so $\mu\geq 2^g/(2k)\geq 8t$ by the
choice of $g$.  Call $f$ \emph{good} if every class contains at least
$\mu/2$ primes.

\emph{A good $f$ exists.}  Choose the $t$ coefficients of $f$ uniformly
and independently.  For any $t$ distinct field elements the values of
$f$ are uniform and independent (the Vandermonde matrix is invertible),
so the values $G_f(z)$ for distinct $z<2^g$ are $t$-wise independent
and uniform in $\bits^m$.  Fix a class $r$ and let
$X_z=[2^{k-1}+(r\oplus G_f(z))2^g+z\text{ is prime}]$ for $z<2^g$.
These indicators are $t$-wise independent.  For each fixed $z$ the
string $r\oplus G_f(z)$ is uniform, so
$\mathbb{E}X_z=2^{-m}\#\{y<2^m:2^{k-1}+y2^g+z\text{ is prime}\}$, and
summing over $z$ gives $\mathbb{E}\sum_zX_z=\mu$ exactly.  No
equidistribution of primes among residues is needed.  Since
$\mu\geq t$, \cref{lem:br-tail} bounds the probability that class $r$
contains fewer than $\mu/2$ primes by
\[
  8\left(\frac{8t}{\mu}\right)^{t/2}
  \leq8\left(16tk\,2^{-g}\right)^{t/2}
  \leq 8\cdot2^{-(m+4)}=2^{-m-1},
\]
where the last step uses
$g-\log_2(16tk)\geq2(m+4)/t$.  A union bound over the $2^m$ classes
shows that $f$ is good with probability at least $1/2$.

\emph{The explicit exponent.}  For large even $n=2k$ for which $m(k)$ is
defined and $L_{m(k)}$ is to be encoded, do the following.  Compute the
truth table of $L_m$, $m=m(k)$, in time $2^{O(m)}$, and sieve the
integers below $2^k$.  Try all at most $2^{tw}\leq2^{tk}$ polynomials $f$
in lexicographic order, and stop at the first good one; checking a
candidate means computing the class of each prime in $[2^{k-1},2^k)$ and
counting, in time $2^{O(k)}$.  Let $T_n$ be the least nonnegative integer
with
\begin{align*}
  T_n&\equiv L_m\bigl(r_f(p)\bigr)\pmod p
  &&\text{for every prime }p\in[2^{k-1},2^k),\\
  T_n&\equiv0\pmod p&&\text{for every prime }p<2^{k-1}.
\end{align*}
The moduli are distinct primes and each prescribed residue is $0$ or
$1$, so the Chinese remainder theorem gives $T_n$, smaller than the
product of the primes below $2^k$, hence below $4^{2^k}$.  The
reconstruction is polynomial in $2^k$.  For all other even $n$, and if
no good $f$ exists, put $T_n=0$.  Since $t$ is constant, the whole
computation takes time $2^{O(k)}=2^{O(n)}$, so $(T_n)$ is explicit.

\emph{The reduction.}  Primality of $k$-bit integers is decidable in
deterministic polynomial time~\cite{AKS04}, and a multitape machine
running in time $\tau$ has circuits of size
$O(\tau\log\tau)$~\cite{PF79}.  Hence some circuit $\mathrm{PRIME}_k$
of size $\poly(k)$ decides primality of $k$-bit integers.  Evaluating
$G_f$ by Horner's rule costs $t$ multiplications in $\mathrm{GF}(2^w)$,
that is $O(tk^2)$ gates.

Let $C$ be a circuit for $r_{T_n}$.  Consider the randomized circuit
that, on input $r\in\bits^m$, draws $u=4km$ independent uniform
$z_1,\ldots,z_u\in Z$; forms
$p_j=2^{k-1}+(r\oplus G_f(z_j))2^g+z_j$, a $k$-bit integer; tests each
$p_j$ with $\mathrm{PRIME}_k$; selects the first prime $p_j$, or $p_1$
if there is none; and outputs the low bit of $C(p_j)$.  Every $p_j$ lies
in class $r$, because $r_f(p_j)=(r\oplus G_f(z_j))\oplus G_f(z_j)=r$.
So whenever some $p_j$ is prime, the output is the low bit of
$T_n\bmod p_j=L_m(r)$, which is $L_m(r)$.  Since $f$ is good, class $r$
contains at least $\mu/2$ primes, all with $z\in Z$; so each draw yields a
prime with probability at least $(\mu/2)/2^{g-1}=\mu/2^g\geq1/(2k)$.
All $u$ draws fail with probability at most
$(1-1/(2k))^{4km}\leq e^{-2m}<2^{-2m}$.  A union bound over the $2^m$
inputs $r$ shows that some fixed choice of $z_1,\ldots,z_u$ is correct on
every input~\cite{Adleman78}.  Hardwiring it gives
\[
  \CC(L_m)\leq\CC(r_{T_n})+u\,\poly(k)
  \leq\CC(P_n^{T_n})+\poly(k),
\]
by \cref{thm:remainders}.

\emph{Conclusion.}  If $\CC(L_m)\geq2^{\delta m}$ at $m=m(k)$, then by
\cref{eq:mk-lower}
\[
  \CC(P_n^{T_n})\geq2^{\delta m(k)}-\poly(k)
  \geq2^{\delta\frac{t}{t+2}k-O(\log k)}-\poly(k)\geq2^{\varepsilon k}
\]
for all large $k$, since $\delta t/(t+2)>\varepsilon$.  Because $m(k)$
eventually takes every large value and increases by at most one per
step, infinitely many hard lengths $m$ yield infinitely many hard even
$n$, and hardness at all large $m$ yields hardness at all large even
$n$.
\end{proof}

The ``all sufficiently large'' reading of the second part's hypothesis,
for some $\delta>0$, is the hardness assumption of Impagliazzo and
Wigderson~\cite{IW97}.  By \cref{thm:explicit-E}, that assumption holds
exactly when some explicit family of powers of the linear-size
permutation $P_n$ has circuit complexity $2^{\Omega(n)}$ at all large even
$n$.  In particular, unconditionally exhibiting an explicit hard power of
$P_n$ is as hard as proving an exponential circuit lower bound for
$\mathsf{E}$.

The proof avoids primes in arithmetic progressions altogether.  It needs
no Linnik-type bound and no Bombieri--Vinogradov theorem, whose constant
is ineffective; an encoding by progressions modulo $q\leq2^{k/2}$ would
also lose half of the exponent.  The number theory used is Chebyshev's
estimate, polynomial-time primality testing, and the Chinese remainder
theorem.  The hashing replaces a deterministic map from indices to
primes, which is the obstacle in a direct encoding: a randomized circuit
only has to find \emph{some} prime in a prescribed class, and a
$2^m$-entry table of primes would cost about as much as the hardness to
be transferred.

Together with \cref{thm:typical-powers,cor:exponent-length}, this
describes the powers of $P_n$ by the exponent's description.
Polynomially long exponents give polynomial-size powers.  Explicit
exponents of exponential length give powers that are hard exactly as far
as $\mathsf{E}$ is hard.  Arbitrary exponents of exponential length give
powers that are hard unconditionally, by counting.

The amount of hardwired information is also forced.  Distinct residues of
$T$ modulo $D_B$ give distinct powers, and \cref{thm:remainders} recovers
the remainder table $r_T$, hence $T\bmod D_B$, from $P_n^T$.  Any
description of $P_n^T$ from which the map can be evaluated, whether a
circuit, a table, or a checkpoint dictionary, therefore determines
$T\bmod D_B$.  By \cref{lem:lcm}, for some $T$ such a description has at
least $\log_2 D_B\geq 2^{n/2}-O(n)$ bits.  The dictionary of
\cref{sec:fixed}, with $O(n2^{n/2})$ bits at the balanced parameter,
is within a polynomial factor of this minimum.

The Lean development does not formalize uniform complexity classes, so
the results of this subsection are outside its checked boundary.

\subsection{Short descriptions of hard exponents}\label{sec:exponent-descriptions}

Long binary expansions do not mean that hard exponents lack short
descriptions.  This subsection separates the length of an exponent from
the complexity of describing it, and identifies the resource that
matters.  Its results are prose results; they are outside the Lean
formalization of \cref{sec:lean}.

Fix a universal Turing machine $U$.  For integers $T,n\geq0$, the
conditional Kolmogorov complexity $K(T\mid n)$ is the length of a
shortest program $p$ such that $U$, given $p$ and the binary expansion
of $n$, halts with the binary expansion of $T$ on its output
tape~\cite{LV19}.  A different universal machine changes $K$ by an
additive constant only.

\begin{proposition}[Hard exponents of constant description length]
\label[proposition]{prop:kolmogorov}
There is a constant $c_U$ such that, for all sufficiently large even $n$,
some exponent $T_n^*<D_B$ satisfies
\[
  K(T_n^*\mid n)\leq c_U
  \qquad\text{and}\qquad
  \CC(P_n^{T_n^*})\geq c\,\frac{2^{n/2}}{n},
\]
where $c>0$ is the constant of \cref{thm:typical-powers}.  Consequently
no bound of the form
$\CC(F^T)\leq\beta(n,\CC(F),K(T\mid n))$ with $\beta$ a polynomial
holds, and any valid bound of this form satisfies
$\beta(n,O(n),O(1))=\Omega(2^{n/2}/n)$.  The same holds for the
unconditional complexity, since $K(T_n^*)\leq 2\log_2n+O(1)$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Decreasing $c$ if necessary, take $c$ rational.  Let
$\theta_n=\ceil{c2^{n/2}/n}$, and let $T_n^*$ be the least $T\geq0$ with
$\CC(P_n^T)\geq\theta_n$.  By \cref{thm:typical-powers} such a $T$
exists below $D_B$ for all sufficiently large even $n$.  The following
fixed program computes $T_n^*$ from $n$.  For $T=0,1,2,\ldots$ in turn,
compute the truth table of $P_n^T$ by iterating \cref{eq:permutation}
on each of the $2^n$ states.  Then enumerate every circuit with fewer
than $\theta_n$ gates and evaluate it on all $2^n$ inputs.  Output the
first $T$ for which no such circuit agrees with the truth table.  Each
stage is a finite computation, and the search stops at $T_n^*$.  Let
$c_U$ be the length of this program.  For the unconditional bound,
prepend a self-delimiting encoding of $n$.  Since $\CC(P_n)=O(n)$,
these exponents defeat every polynomial $\beta$.
\end{proof}

By \cref{cor:exponent-length}, the exponent $T_n^*$ still has
$\Omega(2^{n/2}/n^2)$ binary digits.  So hard exponents are long but can
be simple.  The upper bound of \cref{thm:main} does not depend on $T$ at
all, so in terms of description complexity the two results match up to
$2^{O(\log n)}$ factors.

The program in the proof is expensive.  It tries up to $D_B$ exponents,
each against $2^{O(\theta_n\log\theta_n)}$ circuits, so it runs in time
$2^{2^{O(n)}}$.  It uses only space $2^{O(n)}$: at any moment it stores
one exponent below $D_B$, one candidate circuit, and one truth table.
We do not claim that a substantially faster short program for a hard
exponent cannot exist.  The next statements show what a cheaper program
would imply.

For a family of exponents $T_n$, a \emph{description in time $t(n)$}
(respectively \emph{in space $S(n)$}) is a family of programs $p_n$ of
length $\poly(n)$ such that $U$, given $p_n$ and $n$, outputs $T_n$
within $t(n)$ steps (respectively, halts using at most $S(n)$ cells of
work tape; the write-only output tape is not charged).  For a family of
maps, membership in $\mathsf{PSPACE}/\poly$ or $\mathsf{EXP}/\poly$
refers to the language of pairs $(x,i)$ such that bit $i$ of the image
of $x$ is one, with polynomial-length advice for each $n$.

\begin{definition}[Residue profile]
For $T\geq0$ and $n\geq1$, the \emph{residue profile}
$\rho_{T,n}:\{0,1,\ldots,2^n\}\to\{0,1,\ldots,2^n\}$ is given by
$\rho_{T,n}(p)=T\bmod p$ for $1\leq p\leq2^n$, and
$\rho_{T,n}(0)=\min(T,2^n)$.
\end{definition}

\begin{proposition}[Residue profiles control powers]
\label[proposition]{prop:residue-profile}
Let $F_n:\bits^n\to\bits^n$ have $\CC(F_n)\leq\poly(n)$, and let
$T_n\geq0$.
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
\item If the family $\rho_{T_n,n}$ is in $\mathsf{PSPACE}/\poly$, then
  so is the family $F_n^{T_n}$.  The same holds with $\mathsf{EXP}/\poly$
  in place of $\mathsf{PSPACE}/\poly$.
\item For the permutations of \cref{eq:permutation}, the family
  $P_n^{T_n}$ is in $\mathsf{PSPACE}/\poly$ if and only if the family of
  remainder maps $r_{T_n}$ of \cref{thm:remainders} is.
\end{enumerate}
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
(i) Write $N=2^n$, $T=T_n$, $F=F_n$, and take as advice the circuit
of $F$ together with the advice for $\rho_{T,n}$.  Given $x$, compute
$y=F^N(x)$ by $N$ evaluations of $F$ with an $(n+1)$-bit counter.  The
state $y$ lies on the cycle reached from $x$.  Its cycle length
$\lambda$ is the least $j\geq1$ with $F^j(y)=y$.  The tail length
$\mu<N$ is the least $i$ with $F^i(x)=F^{i+\lambda}(x)$.  Each of these
searches stores $O(n)$ bits besides one evaluation of $F$.  Read
$m=\rho_{T,n}(0)$.  If $m<\mu$, then $T=m$, and the output is $F^m(x)$.
Otherwise $T\geq\mu$, because $\mu<N$, and
\[
  F^T(x)=F^{\mu+(((T\bmod\lambda)-\mu)\bmod\lambda)}(x),
\]
which uses the value $T\bmod\lambda=\rho_{T,n}(\lambda)$ and at most
$\mu+\lambda\leq2N$ further evaluations.  Each query to $\rho_{T,n}$
is answered by recomputing the requested bits in polynomial space, so
the whole computation runs in polynomial space with polynomial advice.
The computation also runs in time $2^{O(n)}\poly(n)$ plus the cost of
the queries, which gives the $\mathsf{EXP}/\poly$ statement.

(ii) The reductions in the proof of \cref{thm:remainders} are explicit
polynomial-time computations in both directions: $r_T(a)$ is the second
coordinate of $P_n^T(a,0)$, and $P_n^T(a,b)$ is obtained from $a$, $b$,
and $r_T(a)$ by one addition, one comparison, and one selection.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Cheap descriptions give membership in a uniform class]
\label[corollary]{cor:description-resources}
Let $F_n$ and $T_n$ be as in \cref{prop:residue-profile}.
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
\item If $T_n$ has a description in space $\poly(n)$, then $F_n^{T_n}$
  is in $\mathsf{PSPACE}/\poly$.  Hence, if
  $\mathsf{PSPACE}\subseteq\mathsf{P}/\poly$, then
  $\CC(F_n^{T_n})\leq\poly(n)$.
\item If $T_n$ has a description in time $2^{\poly(n)}$, then
  $F_n^{T_n}$ is in $\mathsf{EXP}/\poly$.  Hence, if
  $\mathsf{EXP}\subseteq\mathsf{P}/\poly$, then
  $\CC(F_n^{T_n})\leq\poly(n)$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
(i) A description in space $S=\poly(n)$ halts within $2^{O(S)}$ steps,
so it emits at most $2^{O(S)}$ output bits.  To compute $T\bmod p$ for
$p\leq 2^n$, rerun the program and fold each output bit into a running
remainder as it appears.  For most-significant-first output, replace
$r$ by $2r+\text{bit}\bmod p$.  For least-significant-first output, also
maintain $2^j\bmod p$.  A running value capped at $2^n$ gives
$\min(T,2^n)$ in the same way: for least-significant-first output, it
suffices to record whether a one appears at position $n$ or later.  This uses $S+O(n)$ cells, so $\rho_{T_n,n}$ is in
$\mathsf{PSPACE}/\poly$ with the program as advice, and
\cref{prop:residue-profile} applies.
(ii) The same streaming computation runs in time $2^{\poly(n)}$.
In both parts, the final implication follows by folding the polynomial
advice into the input: the resulting language lies in $\mathsf{PSPACE}$
(respectively $\mathsf{EXP}$), so it has polynomial-size circuits under
the stated inclusion, and fixing the advice gives circuits for
$F_n^{T_n}$.
\end{proof}

So among short descriptions, space rather than length or time is the
relevant resource.  Constant-length descriptions in space $2^{O(n)}$
reach hard exponents by \cref{prop:kolmogorov}.  Polynomial-space
descriptions reach a hard power only if
$\mathsf{PSPACE}\not\subseteq\mathsf{P}/\poly$.  Time-$2^{\poly(n)}$
descriptions reach one only if
$\mathsf{EXP}\not\subseteq\mathsf{P}/\poly$.

\paragraph{Long but easy exponents.}
Several exponents with exponentially long expansions have residue
profiles in $\mathsf{PSPACE}$, so by \cref{prop:residue-profile} their
powers of any polynomial-size map are in $\mathsf{PSPACE}/\poly$:
\begin{itemize}
\item $T=2^{2^n}$: compute $2^{2^n}\bmod p$ by $2^n$ modular squarings
  with an $n$-bit counter.
\item Power towers $T=2\uparrow\uparrow h$ of any height
  $h<2^{\poly(n)}$, with $h$ given as advice: reduce exponents modulo iterated totients, using
  $a^b\equiv a^{(b\bmod\varphi(p))+\varphi(p)}\pmod p$ for
  $b\geq\log_2p$.  Whenever an intermediate exponent
  $2\uparrow\uparrow j$ is below $n$, it is computed exactly and used
  directly, so the condition is needed only where it holds.
  The iterated totient of $p\leq2^n$ reaches $1$ after
  $O(n)$ steps, so only $O(n)$ levels need explicit work, and each
  totient is found by trial division in polynomial space.
\item $T=D_B-1$: $D_B\bmod p$ is a product of the largest prime powers
  not exceeding $B$, enumerated in polynomial space.
\end{itemize}
The last example is the inversion exponent of $P_n$, so
$P_n^{D_B-1}=P_n^{-1}$.  As noted in \cref{sec:one-way}, this power even
has a linear-size circuit.  Membership in $\mathsf{PSPACE}/\poly$ gives
only an upper bound.  These examples show that neither the length of an
exponent nor its numerical size forces hardness.

\subsection{The counting barrier}\label{sec:counting-barrier}

The lower bound of \cref{thm:lower} counts the distinct powers of one
map.  This subsection shows how far such counting can go for an
arbitrary map, which maps admit it at all, and why the exponent $1/2$
is also the limit of oracle-based constructions.  Lean checks
\cref{lem:num-powers} for arbitrary maps on finite types
(\texttt{iterate\_eq\_iterate\_iff}, \texttt{card\_range\_iterate}) and
the counting step behind \cref{prop:count-general} in its native basis:
if the ordered description budget for $q$ gates, or the explicit bound
$2^{3q+h(2q+n+1)}$ with $n+q+1\leq2^h$, is below $\mu(F)+\lambda(F)$, then
some $F^T$ with $T<\mu(F)+\lambda(F)$ has no circuit with at most $q$ gates
(\texttt{exists\_hard\_power\_of\_budget\_lt},
\texttt{exists\_hard\_power}).  The final arithmetic of
\cref{prop:count-general} in the paper's basis and the remaining results
of this subsection are not mechanized.

For $F:\bits^n\to\bits^n$ write $\mu(F)$ for the largest pre-period
(transient depth) of a state, $\lambda(F)$ for the least common
multiple of the cycle lengths of its functional graph, and $D(F)$ for
the number of distinct maps among $F^0,F^1,F^2,\ldots$.

\begin{lemma}[Number of powers]\label[lemma]{lem:num-powers}
For integers $0\leq a<b$, $F^a=F^b$ holds if and only if
$a\geq\mu(F)$ and $\lambda(F)\mid b-a$.  Consequently
$D(F)=\mu(F)+\lambda(F)$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
If $a\geq\mu$, then $F^a(x)$ lies on a cycle for every $x$, and the
length of that cycle divides $\lambda$; so $\lambda\mid b-a$ gives
$F^b(x)=F^a(x)$.  Conversely, suppose $a<\mu$ and let $x$ have
pre-period $\mu$.  Then $F^a(x)$ has pre-period $\mu-a>0$, while
$F^b(x)$ has pre-period $\max(\mu-b,0)<\mu-a$, so the two states
differ.  If $a\geq\mu$ but some cycle length $\ell$ does not divide
$b-a$, then any $x$ on that cycle has $F^a(x)\neq F^b(x)$.  Since
$\lambda$ divides $b-a$ exactly when every cycle length does, this
proves the equivalence.  The powers $F^0,\ldots,F^{\mu+\lambda-1}$ are
therefore pairwise distinct, because two of them with $a<b$ would need
$a\geq\mu$ and $0<b-a<\lambda$; and every $F^T$ with $T\geq\mu+\lambda$
equals $F^{T-\lambda}$.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[Counting over the powers of one map]
\label[proposition]{prop:count-general}
For every $n\geq4$ and every $F:\bits^n\to\bits^n$,
\[
  \max_{T\geq0}\CC(F^T)\;\geq\;
  \frac{\log_2D(F)-n^2-n-1}{3n}-1.
\]
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Let $q=\floor{(\log_2D-n^2-n-1)/(3n)}$; if $q<0$ there is nothing to
prove.  Every cycle length is at most $N$, so $\lambda\leq N!$ and
$\log_2D\leq\log_2(N+N!)\leq nN+1$.  Hence $q\leq N/3+1$, and
$n+q+2\leq N$ for $n\geq4$.  By \cref{lem:count}, at most
\[
  (q+1)16^q(n+q+2)^{2q+n}
  \leq2^{\log_2(q+1)+4q+(2q+n)n}
  \leq2^{3nq+n^2+n}
\]
maps have complexity at most $q$, using $4q\leq nq$ and
$\log_2(q+1)\leq n$.  By the choice of $q$ this is less than $D$, so
by \cref{lem:num-powers} some power has complexity greater than $q$.
\end{proof}

For $F=P_n$, where $\mu=0$ and $\log_2D=\log_2D_B\geq2^{n/2}-O(n)$, this
recovers \cref{thm:lower} up to the constant factor.

\begin{corollary}[A ceiling on the number of powers]
\label[corollary]{cor:landau}
If $\CC(F)\leq s$ and $A=s+n^3$, then
\[
  \log_2\lambda(F)\leq\log_2D(F)
  =O\!\left(nA+n^{5/2}\sqrt{NA}\right).
\]
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
Combine \cref{prop:count-general} with the upper bound
\cref{eq:optimized}, which holds for every power.
\end{proof}

For comparison, let $g(N)$ be Landau's function, the largest order of
a permutation of $N$ points.  The cycle lengths of any map on $N$
states sum to at most $N$, so $\lambda(F)\leq g(N)$ for every $F$, and
Landau's theorem~\cite{Landau03}, $\ln g(N)\sim\sqrt{N\ln N}$, gives
\[
  \log_2g(N)=(1+o(1))\frac{\sqrt{N\ln N}}{\ln2}
  =\Theta\bigl(2^{n/2}\sqrt n\bigr).
\]
Thus \cref{cor:landau} says that a map with a polynomial-size circuit
can have at most as many powers as an arbitrary map, up to $\poly(n)$
factors in the exponent, and \cref{eq:lcm-lower} shows that $P_n$,
with $\log_2D_B\geq2^{n/2}-O(n)$, is extremal up to such factors.
Conversely, a counting argument through \cref{lem:count} can certify
hardness $q$ only when $3nq+n^2+n<\log_2D(F)\leq\log_2(N+g(N))$, so it
never certifies more than $O(2^{n/2}/\sqrt n)$ for any map on $n$ bits.
Counting over the powers of a single map therefore cannot go beyond the
exponent $1/2$ of \cref{thm:main}; the matching exponent of
\cref{thm:lower} is a counting barrier, not only a limit of the
construction.

The ceiling also says which maps have hard powers by counting.  Let
$c(F)$ be the number of distinct cycle lengths.  Distinct lengths sum to
at most $N$, so $c(F)(c(F)+1)/2\leq N$, that is,
$c(F)\leq\sqrt{2N}$, and by the arithmetic--geometric mean inequality
$\log_2\lambda(F)\leq c(F)\log_2(N/c(F))$.  A bound of
$2^{n/2}/n$ from \cref{prop:count-general} needs
$\log_2D(F)\geq3\cdot2^{n/2}$, hence $c(F)=\Omega(2^{n/2}/n)$, within
a factor $O(n)$ of the maximum; $P_n$ has $c(P_n)=2^{n/2}-1$.  Typical
maps are far from this.  For a uniformly random permutation of $N$
points, $\ln\ord$ is asymptotically normal with mean
$\tfrac12(\ln N)^2$~\cite{ET67}, so
$\log_2\ord\approx\tfrac{\ln2}{2}n^2\approx0.35\,n^2$ with high
probability; for a uniformly random map, $\ln\lambda$ has mean
asymptotic to $\tfrac18(\ln N)^2$~\cite{Harris73}, giving about
$\tfrac{\ln2}{8}n^2\approx0.09\,n^2$ bits.  Both have $O(n)$ cycles in
expectation.  For such maps $\log_2D(F)=O(n^2)$, every power equals
$F^{T'}$ with an exponent $T'$ of $O(n^2)$ bits, and counting gives
nothing.  Hard powers obtained by counting are therefore a non-generic
phenomenon; for typical maps the question is that of
\cref{sec:lower-limits}, about exponents of polynomial length.

\paragraph{The image profile is the right parameter.}
\Cref{cor:image} charges $|\operatorname{im}(F^d)|$ in place of $N$, and
this is sharp.  For $n=a+b$ with $a$ even, let
$F(u,v)=(P_a(u),0^b)$ on $\bits^a\times\bits^b$.  Then $\CC(F)=O(n)$
and $|\operatorname{im}(F)|=2^a$, so \cref{cor:image} with $d=1$ gives
$\max_T\CC(F^T)=O(n^32^{a/2})$, however large $b$ is.  On the other
hand $F^T(u,0^b)=(P_a^T(u),0^b)$ for $T\geq1$, so a circuit for $F^T$
yields one for $P_a^T$ by fixing $v=0^b$ and projecting, and
\cref{thm:lower} supplies $T\geq1$ with
$\CC(F^T)=\Omega(2^{a/2}/a)$.  Permutations, for which no image shrinks,
are the hardest case, which is why the sharpness example of
\cref{sec:lower} is a permutation.

For random-like maps the image shrinks substantially.  If
$|\operatorname{im}(F^d)|\leq cN/d$ at $d=\ceil{N^{1/3}}$, then
\cref{cor:image}, with $F^d$ unrolled in $ds$ gates and $T<d$ handled by
unrolling, gives
\[
  \CC(F^T)=O\!\left(N^{1/3}\bigl(s+n^{3/2}\sqrt{cA}\bigr)\right)
  \qquad\text{for every }T.
\]
For a uniformly random map and each fixed $d$,
$\mathbb E|\operatorname{im}(F^d)|/N\to\tau_d$ as $N\to\infty$, where
$\tau_0=1$, $\tau_{d+1}=1-e^{-\tau_d}$ and $\tau_d\sim2/d$~\cite{FO90};
so random maps heuristically have the profile $2N/d$, with $c\approx2$.
This is a statement about expectations for fixed $d$, not about any
explicit family, and extending it to $d=\ceil{N^{1/3}}$ is a heuristic.
Whether a natural explicit family of polynomial-size maps satisfies such
an image profile is open.  The implication itself is unconditional.

\paragraph{Black-box tightness.}
The construction uses $F$ only through evaluations.  In the
preprocessing model, where an unbounded preprocessing stage produces an
advice string of $S$ bits about $F$ and an online stage makes $t$ oracle
queries to $F$, the construction of \cref{sec:fixed} has
$S=O(n(N/L+L))$ bits (payloads and residue table) and $t=O(L)$ queries
for every $T$; the batched lookup makes no queries.  So $S\,t=O(nN)$ for
$L\leq\sqrt N$.

\begin{proposition}[Black-box tightness]\label[proposition]{prop:blackbox}
Let $P$ be a uniformly random permutation of $\bits^n$.  Suppose that an
algorithm with $S$ bits of advice about $P$ and $t$ oracle queries
outputs $P^T(x)$ for every $T\geq0$, or merely for $T=\ord(P)-1$, with
probability at least $\varepsilon$ over $P$ and a uniform $x$.  Then
$S\,t\geq\widetilde\Omega(\varepsilon N)$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
$P^{\ord(P)-1}=P^{-1}$, and the preprocessing may compute $\ord(P)$.
The algorithm therefore inverts $P$ at a uniform point with probability
$\varepsilon$, and the time--space lower bound for inverting a random
permutation with advice~\cite{Yao90,DTT10} gives the claim.
\end{proof}

Hence the tradeoff $S\,t=O(N\log N)$ of the construction, and its
balancing point $L\approx\sqrt N$, are optimal up to polylogarithmic
factors among all schemes that use the transition only as an oracle.
Mazor and Pass~\cite[Theorem~5.8]{MP24} give a related statement for
circuits with oracle gates for the permutation.  Improving the
exponent of \cref{cor:inverse} for a specific permutation therefore
requires using its circuit, not only its values.  Whether the same
holds for a fixed cycle type, such as a random relabelling
$\sigma\circ P_n\circ\sigma^{-1}$ of $P_n$, does not follow directly from
these bounds, which concern uniformly random permutations, and is left
open.  For noninjective maps the black-box question is also open: the
image bound above gives advice times queries squared of
$\widetilde O(N)$ for random-like maps, but verifying $y=F^T(x)$ costs
about $\sqrt N$ queries, and the standard presampling and compression
arguments do not apply directly.

\subsection{A permutation near the counting ceiling}\label{sec:near-landau}

The counting argument of \cref{thm:lower} is limited by the number of
distinct powers.  For $P_n$ this number is $D_B=\lcm(1,\ldots,B)$, and
$\log_2D_B=O(B)$ by Chebyshev's bound on $\pi(B)$, so counting cannot
certify more than $O(2^{n/2}/n)$ for $P_n$.  This subsection gives a
polynomial-size permutation with $2^{\Theta(2^{n/2}\sqrt n)}$ distinct
powers whose hardest power has complexity $\Theta(2^{n/2}/\sqrt n)$,
within a constant factor of what any counting argument over powers can
give.  The family is nonuniform only through $O(n)$ hardwired bits.
Lean checks only the combinatorial core; \cref{rem:near-landau-lean}
lists what is mechanized.

\paragraph{Construction.}
Fix a constant $C\geq1$.  For a large integer $K$ put
\[
  g=\ceil{\log_2(CK)},\qquad m=K-1-g,\qquad n_K=m+K=2m+1+g.
\]
States are pairs $(r,b)$ with $r\in\bits^m$ and $b\in\bits^K$, read as
integers.  Fix an irreducible polynomial of degree $m$ over
$\mathrm{GF}(2)$ to represent $\mathrm{GF}(2^m)$, and for
$\alpha,\beta\in\mathrm{GF}(2^m)$ let $f(z)=\alpha z+\beta$, where
$z<2^g\leq2^m$ is embedded in $\mathrm{GF}(2^m)$ by its binary digits.
Every integer $p\in[2^{K-1},2^K)$ is written uniquely as
\[
  p=2^{K-1}+y\,2^g+z,\qquad 0\leq y<2^m,\ 0\leq z<2^g,
\]
because $2^{K-1}=2^m2^g$.  Assign $p$ to the class $y\oplus f(z)$.  Class
$r$ is therefore
\[
  S_r=\{\,p_r(z)=2^{K-1}+(r\oplus f(z))2^g+z:\ 0\leq z<2^g\,\},
\]
a set of exactly $2^g$ integers, and the classes partition
$[2^{K-1},2^K)$.  Let $\ell_r$ be the prime $p_r(z)$ with least $z$, or
$\ell_r=1$ if $S_r$ contains no prime.  Define
\[
  F(r,b)=
  \begin{cases}
    (r,\,b+1\bmod\ell_r),&\ell_r>1\text{ and }b<\ell_r,\\
    (r,b),&\text{otherwise}.
  \end{cases}
\]

\begin{lemma}[Structure]\label[lemma]{lem:nl-structure}
$F$ is a permutation of $\bits^{n_K}$ with $\CC(F)=\poly(K)$.  It has one
cycle of length $\ell_r$ for each $r$ with $\ell_r>1$, and every other
state is fixed.  The lengths $\ell_r>1$ are distinct primes in
$[2^{K-1},2^K)$, so
\[
  \ord(F)=\lambda:=\prod_{r:\,\ell_r>1}\ell_r,
\]
and the powers $F^T$, $0\leq T<\lambda$, are pairwise distinct.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
For fixed $r$ with $\ell_r>1$ the second coordinate moves around the
cycle $0,1,\ldots,\ell_r-1$; the states with $b\geq\ell_r$, and all
states with $\ell_r=1$, are fixed.  So $F$ is a permutation with the
stated cycles.  Different classes are disjoint, so the primes $\ell_r$
are distinct, and the order of $F$ is their product.  Distinct residues
of $T$ modulo $\lambda$ give distinct powers, since some cycle length
$\ell_r$ does not divide their difference.

For the circuit, compute the $2^g\leq2CK$ candidates $p_r(z)$; each needs
one multiplication in $\mathrm{GF}(2^m)$, $O(m^2)$ gates.  Test each for
primality with a $\poly(K)$-size circuit, obtained from the
Agrawal--Kayal--Saxena algorithm~\cite{AKS04} by the
Pippenger--Fischer simulation~\cite{PF79}.  Select the first prime by a
prefix-OR scan, compare $b$ with $\ell_r$, and update $b$ with an
incrementer and an equality test.  The total is $\poly(K)$.
\end{proof}

Write $\nu$ for the fraction of classes $r$ with $\ell_r>1$, and
\[
  \mu=2^{-m}\bigl(\pi(2^K)-\pi(2^{K-1})\bigr)
\]
for the average number of primes per class.  By the prime number theorem,
$\pi(2^K)-\pi(2^{K-1})=(1+o(1))2^{K-1}/(K\ln2)$, so
\[
  \mu=(1+o(1))\frac{2^g}{K\ln2}\geq(1-o(1))\frac{C}{\ln2}.
\]
The factor $1-o(1)$ is needed: only the prime number theorem, not
Chebyshev's bound, gives the constant $1/\ln2$.

\begin{lemma}[Most classes contain a prime]\label[lemma]{lem:nl-classes}
For some $\alpha,\beta$, the fraction of classes containing a prime is at
least $\mu/(1+\mu)$.  Hence $\nu\geq\mu/(1+\mu)\geq c_C-o(1)$ for this
choice, where $c_C=C/(C+\ln2)$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Choose $\alpha,\beta$ uniformly and fix a class $r$.  Let
$X_z=[p_r(z)\text{ is prime}]$ and $X=\sum_{z<2^g}X_z$.  For each $z$ the
value $f(z)$ is uniform, so $p_r(z)$ is uniform among the $2^m$ integers
of $[2^{K-1},2^K)$ with low part $z$; summing over $z$ gives
$\mathbb EX=\mu$.  For $z_1\neq z_2$ the pair $(f(z_1),f(z_2))$ is
uniform, so the $X_z$ are pairwise independent and
$\operatorname{Var}X\leq\sum_z\mathbb EX_z=\mu$.  By the Cauchy--Schwarz
inequality,
\[
  \Pr[X>0]\geq\frac{(\mathbb EX)^2}{\mathbb E X^2}
  \geq\frac{\mu^2}{\mu+\mu^2}=\frac{\mu}{1+\mu}.
\]
The expected number of nonempty classes is therefore at least
$\mu2^m/(1+\mu)$, and some $(\alpha,\beta)$ attains the expectation.
The function $x\mapsto x/(1+x)$ is increasing, which gives the bound on
$\nu$.
\end{proof}

The lower bound uses the sharp circuit count of \cref{lem:sharp-count},
which discounts relabellings of minimum circuits.

\Cref{lem:count} counts every encoding of a circuit and so carries an
extra factor of about $2$ in the exponent; that would halve the constant
in the next theorem.

\begin{theorem}[Hard powers near the counting ceiling]\label{thm:near-landau}
With $\alpha,\beta$ as in \cref{lem:nl-classes},
\[
  \nu(1-o(1))\,2^m\leq\max_{T\geq0}\CC(F^T)\leq(1+o(1))\,2^m,
  \qquad 2^m=\Theta\!\left(\frac{2^{n_K/2}}{\sqrt{n_K}}\right).
\]
Consequently, for every constant $C\geq1$ and every large $n$ there is a
permutation $F_n$ of $\bits^n$ with $\CC(F_n)=\poly(n)$ and
\[
  \max_{T\geq0}\CC(F_n^T)=\Theta\!\left(\frac{2^{n/2}}{\sqrt n}\right).
\]
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
\emph{Upper bound.}  For every $T$,
\[
  F^T(r,b)=
  \begin{cases}
    \bigl(r,\,(b+(T\bmod\ell_r))\bmod\ell_r\bigr),&\ell_r>1\text{ and }b<\ell_r,\\
    (r,b),&\text{otherwise}.
  \end{cases}
\]
The table $r\mapsto T\bmod\ell_r$, with value $0$ when $\ell_r=1$, has $m$
input bits and $K$ output bits.  By Lupanov's bound~\cite{Lupanov58},
each output bit has a circuit of size $(1+o(1))2^m/m$.  Since
$K=m+O(\log m)$, the $K$ bits together cost $(1+o(1))2^m$.  The circuit
for $\ell_r$ from \cref{lem:nl-structure}, the comparison, the addition
and the reduction modulo $\ell_r$ add $\poly(K)=o(2^m)$ gates.

\emph{Lower bound.}  Let $q=\max(\max_T\CC(F^T),n_K)$.  If $q>2^{m+1}$
there is nothing to prove.  Otherwise $\log_2q\leq m+1$, and by
\cref{lem:nl-structure,lem:sharp-count} with both input and output width $n_K$,
\[
  \nu2^m(K-1)\leq\log_2\lambda\leq q\log_2q+O(q)+O(n_K\log q)
  \leq q(m+O(1))+O(m^2),
\]
using $\ell_r\geq2^{K-1}$ for each of the $\nu2^m$ nonempty classes.
Since $K-1=m+g$ and $\nu$ is bounded below by \cref{lem:nl-classes},
$q\geq\nu(1-O(1/m))2^m-O(m)=\nu(1-o(1))2^m$.  This exceeds $n_K$ for
large $K$, so $q=\max_T\CC(F^T)$.

\emph{Every length.}  We have $2^m=2^{(n_K-1-g)/2}$ and
$CK\leq2^g<2CK$ with $K=n_K/2+O(\log n_K)$, which gives
$2^m=\Theta(2^{n_K/2}/\sqrt{n_K})$, with constants depending on $C$.  As
$K$ increases by one, $n_K$ increases by one or two, so every large $n$
is $n_K$ or $n_K+1$ for some $K$.  In the second case let
$F_n(x,e)=F(x)$ on the first $n_K$ bits and leave the last bit $e$ fixed.
Each power of $F_n$ restricts to the same power of $F$ when $e=0$, and is
computed by a circuit for that power of $F$ with $e$ passed through, so
$\max_T\CC(F_n^T)=\max_T\CC(F^T)$.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Mechanized part]\label[remark]{rem:near-landau-lean}
The module \texttt{PrimeCycles.lean} checks the following for every
function $f$ with values below $2^m$, not only for the affine maps.  The
lengths $\ell_r>1$ are distinct primes in $[2^{K-1},2^K)$
(\texttt{classPrime\_coprime}).  For arbitrary lengths below $2^K$, $F^T$
is given by the formula in the proof of \cref{thm:near-landau} and depends
on $T$ only through the table $r\mapsto T\bmod\ell_r$
(\texttt{power\_apply\_mod}).  For pairwise coprime lengths,
$F^S=F^T$ exactly when $S\equiv T\pmod\lambda$, so $\lambda$ is the
order of $F$ and its number of distinct powers
(\texttt{iterate\_eq\_iterate\_iff}, \texttt{card\_range\_iterate}).
Finally, \texttt{near\_landau\_lower\_bound} proves that $F$ is a
bijection with $\lambda\geq2^{(K-1)c}$, where $c=\nu2^m$ is the number
of classes containing a prime, and that if $3q+h(2q+n_K+1)<(K-1)c$ and
$n_K+q+1\leq2^h$, then some $F^T$ with $T<\lambda$ has no native circuit
with at most $q$ gates.  This step counts ordered circuit descriptions
as in \cref{lem:count}, not minimum circuits as in
\cref{lem:sharp-count}.  With $h=m+O(1)$ it therefore gives the lower
bound of \cref{thm:near-landau} with $\nu/2$ in place of $\nu$.  The
circuit for $F$, the prime number theorem estimate,
\cref{lem:nl-classes}, \cref{lem:sharp-count}, the upper bound, and
\cref{cor:near-landau-average} remain prose results.
\end{remark}

\begin{remark}[The counting ceiling]\label[remark]{rem:landau-ceiling}
The lower bound above, like that of \cref{thm:lower}, uses only the number
$D(F)$ of distinct powers, and it gives $q$ with
$q\log_2q\approx\log_2D(F)$.  For every map $F$ on $N=2^n$ points,
$D(F)\leq N+g(N)$, where $g(N)$ is the largest order of a permutation of
$N$ points: a map has $\mu+\lambda$ distinct powers, $\mu\leq N$, and
$\lambda$ is the lcm of cycle lengths summing to at most $N$.  Landau's
theorem~\cite{Landau03} gives $\ln g(N)=(1+o(1))\sqrt{N\ln N}$, so
$\log_2\log_2D(F)=(1/2+o(1))n$ and this argument can never certify more
than
\[
  (1+o(1))\frac{\log_2D(F)}{\log_2\log_2D(F)}
  \leq\left(\frac{2}{\sqrt{\ln2}}+o(1)\right)\frac{2^{n/2}}{\sqrt n}
  \approx2.40\cdot\frac{2^{n/2}}{\sqrt n}.
\]
\Cref{thm:near-landau} reaches this ceiling up to a constant factor that
depends on $C$ and on the rounding of $g$.  The general upper bound of
\cref{thm:main} is $2^{n/2}\poly(n)$; whether some polynomial-size map
has $\max_T\CC(F^T)$ much larger than $2^{n/2}/\sqrt n$ is beyond
counting.
\end{remark}

Unlike $P_n$, the cycles of $F$ all have lengths of the same order and
carry a constant fraction of the states.  This yields constant
average-case hardness for random powers.

\begin{corollary}[Constant average-case hardness]\label[corollary]{cor:near-landau-average}
Let $0<\delta<\nu/4$ be constant and let $T$ be uniform modulo $\lambda$.
With probability $1-o(1)$, every circuit of size at most $\delta2^m$
disagrees with $F^T$ on at least a $\nu/2-2\delta-o(1)$ fraction of the
$2^{n_K}$ states.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
Put $q=\floor{\delta2^m}$ and $\varepsilon=1/\log_2K$.  Fix a map
$G:\bits^{n_K}\to\bits^{n_K}$ and a class $r$ with $\ell=\ell_r>1$.  On
the cycle states $(r,b)$, $b<\ell$, $G$ is correct exactly when
$G(r,b)=(r,v)$ with $v<\ell$ and $v\equiv b+T\pmod\ell$.  So each such state
names at most one residue of $T$ modulo $\ell$, and at most $1/\varepsilon$
residues are named by more than $\varepsilon\ell$ states.  Call $r$
\emph{good} for $(G,T)$ if $T\bmod\ell_r$ is one of them.  Since $T$ is
uniform modulo $\lambda=\prod\ell_r$, the residues $T\bmod\ell_r$ are
independent and uniform, so the classes are good independently, each
with probability at most $1/(\varepsilon\ell_r)\leq2/(\varepsilon2^K)$.
With at most $2^m$ nonempty classes,
\[
  \Pr[\text{at least $2q$ good classes}]
  \leq\binom{2^m}{2q}\left(\frac{2}{\varepsilon2^K}\right)^{2q}
  \leq\left(\frac{e\,2^{m-K}}{\varepsilon q}\right)^{2q}
  \leq\left(\frac{e}{2CK\varepsilon q}\right)^{2q},
\]
because $2^{m-K}=2^{-1-g}\leq1/(2CK)$.  By \cref{lem:sharp-count}, at
most $2^{q\log_2q+O(q)+O(m^2)}$ maps have circuits of size at most $q$.  A
union bound over them gives total probability at most
$2^{-q(\log_2q+2\log_2(K\varepsilon)-O(1))+O(m^2)}=o(1)$.

So with probability $1-o(1)$, every circuit of size at most $q$ has fewer
than $2q$ good classes, and is wrong on at least a $1-\varepsilon$
fraction of the cycle states of each other nonempty class.  The cycle
states of the nonempty classes number at least $\nu2^m2^{K-1}=(\nu/2)2^{n_K}$,
and the good classes contain at most $2q\,2^K\leq2\delta2^{n_K}$ of them.
The error fraction is therefore at least
$(1-\varepsilon)(\nu/2-2\delta)=\nu/2-2\delta-o(1)$.
\end{proof}

For $P_n$, \cref{prop:approx-all,thm:random-average} instead show that
at size $2^{n/2}/\poly(n)$ the optimal approximation error tends to zero.
Thus the near-ceiling construction also changes the average-case behavior,
not only the polynomial factor in worst-case complexity.

\begin{remark}[Uniformity]\label[remark]{rem:near-landau-uniform}
Apart from the irreducible polynomial, $F$ is determined by the $2m$ bits
of $(\alpha,\beta)$.  The polynomial and a good pair can be found
deterministically in time $2^{O(n)}$ by exhaustive search, after sieving
$[2^{K-1},2^K)$ once.  A uniformly
random pair has at most $2\cdot2^m/(1+\mu)$ empty classes with
probability at least $1/2$, by Markov's inequality, so randomness also
works when $\mu>1$.  A deterministic polynomial-time construction with
the same parameters would need an efficiently computable injective map
from about $2^K/K$ indices to $K$-bit primes that hits a constant
fraction of all of them.  This is related to the construction of primes:
Chen, Lu, Oliveira, Ren and Santhanam give a polynomial-time
pseudodeterministic construction of primes for infinitely many
lengths~\cite{CLORS23}, which is not enough here.  Replacing the hash
classes by the intervals $[2^{K-1}+rCK,\,2^{K-1}+(r+1)CK)$ would give an
explicit family.  Under the Hardy--Littlewood prime $k$-tuples
conjecture, Gallagher~\cite{Gallagher76} shows that the number of primes in
such intervals is asymptotically Poisson distributed, which yields a
constant fraction of nonempty intervals and the same
$\Theta(2^{n/2}/\sqrt n)$ bound.  This route is conditional.
\end{remark}

\section{Input times and shared evaluation}

\subsection{An iteration count supplied as input}\label{sec:variable}

\begin{definition}
For $m\geq 1$, define
\[
  \Iter_{F,m}:\bits^n\times\bits^m\longrightarrow\bits^n,
  \qquad \Iter_{F,m}(x,t)=F^{\operatorname{val}(t)}(x),
\]
where $\operatorname{val}(t)$ is the nonnegative integer encoded by $t$.
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}[Variable-time iteration]\label{thm:variable}
Under the assumptions of \cref{thm:main}, for every $m\geq 1$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:variable}
  \CC(\Iter_{F,m})
  =O\!\left(m\left(A+\sqrt{n^3NA}\right)\right).
\end{equation}
In particular,
\[
  \CC(\Iter_{F,n})
  =O\!\left(n\left(A+\sqrt{n^3NA}\right)\right).
\]
If $s$ and $m$ are polynomial in $n$, then
$\CC(\Iter_{F,m})\leq 2^{n/2+O(\log n)}$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Write $t=\sum_{j=0}^{m-1}t_j2^j$.  For each $j$, apply
\cref{thm:main} directly to the original map $F$ and the fixed exponent
$2^j$, obtaining a circuit for $G_j=F^{2^j}$.  Starting with $v_0=x$, use
\[
  v_{j+1}=
  \begin{cases}
    G_j(v_j),&t_j=1,\\
    v_j,&t_j=0.
  \end{cases}
\]
Inductively,
$v_j=F^{\sum_{a<j}t_a2^a}(x)$, so $v_m=F^t(x)$.
There are $m$ circuits of the size in \cref{eq:optimized} and $m$
$n$-bit multiplexers.  The extra $O(mn)$ gates are absorbed by the claimed
bound.  Each $G_j$ is constructed from $F$ using the theorem; it is not
obtained by duplicating the circuit for $G_{j-1}$.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Long input times]\label{thm:long-time}
Write $B=A+\sqrt{n^3NA}$.  For every $m\geq1$,
\[
 \CC(\Iter_{F,m})=O\!\left(\min\{mB,\ nB+nm\}\right).
\]
Thus the cost of processing additional time bits is additive once the
state width is fixed.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
The first bound is \cref{thm:variable}.  For the second, compute the exact
eventual cycle length $\ell=\ell(x)$ in $O(B)$ gates using
\cref{thm:labels}.  Replace the input time by
\[
 u=\begin{cases}
 t,&t<N,\\
 N+(t-N)\bmod\ell,&t\geq N.
 \end{cases}
\]
Then $u<2N$ and $F^u(x)=F^t(x)$: in the second case both times are past
cycle entry and have the same residue modulo $\ell$.

Compute $t\bmod\ell$ by streaming its bits from most significant to least
significant, retaining an $O(n)$-bit remainder.  Each step doubles the
remainder, adds one bit, and conditionally subtracts $\ell$; a spare bit
prevents overflow.  Shared-carry addition and subtraction give $O(n)$
gates per input bit, including comparison and selection.  The bounded
modular correction by $N$ and the final selection cost $O(n^3+m)$ with
the arithmetic circuits already used in the proof.  Hence forming $u$
costs $O(nm+n^3)$, and its $n+1$ bits feed \cref{thm:variable} at cost
$O(nB)$.  Since $n^3\leq B$, the second bound follows.  If $m\leq n$,
one may simply use the original $m$-stage construction.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Sharing a dictionary among independent trajectories}
\label{sec:batch}

Let
\[
  B_{F,T,r}(x_1,\ldots,x_r)
   =\bigl(F^T(x_1),\ldots,F^T(x_r)\bigr).
\]
The inputs $x_1,\ldots,x_r$ are independent $n$-bit words.  The following
construction uses one checkpoint dictionary for all of them.

\begin{theorem}[Batched fixed powers]\label{thm:batch}
Under the assumptions of \cref{thm:main}, for every $1\leq r\leq N$,
$T\geq 0$, and $1\leq L\leq N$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:batch-tradeoff}
  \CC(B_{F,T,r})
   =O\!\left(rLs+n^3\left(rL+\frac{N}{L}\right)\right).
\end{equation}
Consequently,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:batch-opt}
  \CC(B_{F,T,r})
   =O\!\left(rA+\sqrt{rn^3NA}\right).
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Set $K=2L$.  If $T<K$, unroll $T$ copies of $F$ independently for each
input.  Otherwise use the checkpoint set and dictionary from
\cref{sec:fixed}.  Generate the first $K+1$ states of all $r$ trajectories.
Together with one copy of the dictionary, these give
\[
  R=|H|+r(K+1)=O(N/L+rL)
\]
records.  Attach the trajectory number and within-trajectory position to
each query.  Because $r\leq N$ and $L\leq N$, the record width and
$\log(R+2)$ are $O(n)$.  One application of \cref{lem:lookup} therefore
resolves all queries in $O(n^3(N/L+rL))$ gates and returns them in their
original order.

Select a matching checkpoint separately within each trajectory.  Run
repeated-state detection separately on each trajectory as well; this
costs $O(rn^3L)$ gates and does not confuse a collision between two
different trajectories with a repetition along one trajectory.
The correction trajectory and output selection are also performed
separately for each input.  The fixed residue table may be implemented
with a separate $O(nL)$-gate lookup for each trajectory, whose total
cost is absorbed by $O(rn^3L)$.  The two simulation phases together cost
$O(rLs)$ gates.  This proves \cref{eq:batch-tradeoff}.

\begin{samepage}
Choose
\[
  L=\max\left\{1,\ceil{\sqrt{n^3N/(rA)}}\right\}.
\]
\end{samepage}
As before $1\leq L\leq N$.  If $rA\leq n^3N$, then
$rAL=O(\sqrt{rn^3NA})$ and $n^3N/L=O(\sqrt{rn^3NA})$.
If $rA>n^3N$, the choice is $L=1$ and the cost is $O(rA)$.
This gives \cref{eq:batch-opt}.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Independent input times]\label[corollary]{cor:batch-variable}
For $1\leq r\leq N$ and $m\geq 1$, the map
\[
  \bigl((x_1,t_1),\ldots,(x_r,t_r)\bigr)
   \longmapsto
  \bigl(F^{t_1}(x_1),\ldots,F^{t_r}(x_r)\bigr),
  \qquad t_i\in\bits^m,
\]
has a circuit of size
\begin{equation}\label{eq:batch-variable}
  O\!\left(m\left(rA+\sqrt{rn^3NA}\right)\right).
\end{equation}
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
Use the binary-power construction of \cref{thm:variable}.  At stage $j$,
apply \cref{thm:batch} with update map $F$ and exponent $2^j$ on the $r$
current states, then use the $j$th bit of each $t_i$ to select whether to
retain or update that state.  The $O(mrn)$ selection gates are absorbed
by \cref{eq:batch-variable}.
\end{proof}

For polynomial $s$ and $m$, and $r=\floor{2^{\alpha n}}$ with fixed
$0\leq\alpha\leq 1$, \cref{eq:batch-variable} is at most
\[
  2^{(1+\alpha)n/2+O(\log n)}.
\]
This is an upper-bound sharing statement.  It is not a direct-sum
separation against optimal circuits for a single trajectory.

\subsection{Batched powers of the counting permutation}\label{sec:batch-Pn}

For the permutation $P_n$ of \cref{sec:lower}, sharing costs much less
than \cref{thm:batch} suggests.  Lean checks \cref{prop:batch-Pn} for
the modulus counter in its native basis, in the theorems
\texttt{batched\_counter\_power} and \texttt{batch\_lower\_bounds}
of the namespace \texttt{ModulusCounter}.  The upper bound is
$40000(k+1)^3(2^k+r)$ gates for $r\leq N$, with Batcher networks in the
shared lookup.  One block of a batched circuit gives a circuit for $P_n^T$
of no larger size, and a batched circuit has at least $r$ gates when
$P_n^T$ is not the identity.  \texttt{batched\_hard\_counter\_power}
combines both bounds for the hard exponents of \cref{thm:lower}.  The
AKS variant and the counting discussion below are not mechanized.

\begin{proposition}[Batched powers of $P_n$]\label[proposition]{prop:batch-Pn}
Let $n=2k$ be even, $1\leq r\leq N$, and $T\geq0$.  Then
\[
  \CC(B_{P_n,T,r})=O\!\left(n^3\bigl(2^{n/2}+r\bigr)\right).
\]
If $P_n^T$ is not the identity, then also
\[
  \CC(B_{P_n,T,r})\geq\max\{\CC(P_n^T),\,r\}.
\]
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
For the upper bound, use the dictionary with keys $a=1,\ldots,B$ and
payloads $r_T(a)=T\bmod a$, in the notation of \cref{thm:remainders}.
It has $B<2^{n/2}$ entries of $k$ bits.  The first coordinates
$a_1,\ldots,a_r$ of the $r$ inputs are the queries.  Since $r\leq N$,
\cref{lem:lookup} returns all $r_T(a_i)$ in $O(n^3(2^{n/2}+r))$ gates.
The query $a_i=0$ is unmatched and receives payload $0=r_T(0)$.  Each
input is then completed as in the proof of \cref{thm:remainders} with
$O(n)$ further gates.

For the lower bound, fixing all inputs but the first block to zero and
keeping the first output block gives a circuit for $P_n^T$.  For the
bound $r$, suppose that every output bit of the second coordinate of
$P_n^T$ is a constant or an input variable.  On the inputs with $a=0$
every state is fixed, so output bit $j$ agrees with $b_j$ there; it is
therefore not constant, and the only input variable that agrees with
$b_j$ on these inputs is $b_j$.  Hence $P_n^T$ is the identity.
Otherwise, some output bit of each of the $r$ blocks is computed by a
gate.  These $r$ output functions depend on disjoint sets of variables
and are nonconstant, so they are pairwise distinct and need $r$
distinct gates.
\end{proof}

With \cref{lem:lookup-aks} the factor $n^3$ becomes $n^2$.  For the
hard exponents of \cref{thm:lower}, the batched cost of $P_n$ is
therefore between $\Omega(2^{n/2}/n+r)$ and $O(n^2(2^{n/2}+r))$.  Each
additional input costs $\poly(n)$ gates.  The generic bound of
\cref{thm:batch} is $O(n^3\sqrt r\,2^{n/2})$ for $P_n$, since
$A=\Theta(n^3)$, so the factor $\sqrt r$ is not attained here.

\paragraph{Counting cannot show more.}
As $T$ varies, the number of distinct maps $B_{F,T,r}$ equals the number
$D(F)$ of distinct powers of $F$, since the first output block is
$F^T$.  By \cref{thm:main}, every power has a circuit of size
$S^*=O(A+\sqrt{n^3NA})$, so \cref{lem:count} gives
$\log D(F)=O((S^*+n)\log(n+S^*+2))$.  For polynomial $s$ this is
$2^{n/2}\poly(n)$.  A counting argument over exponents compares $D(F)$
with the number of circuits on $rn$ inputs, which only grows with $r$.
It therefore certifies at most $2^{n/2}\poly(n)$ gates for every $r$,
and counting over all maps of polynomial size as well adds only
$\poly(n)$ to the logarithm.  For $P_n$, counting gives
$\Omega(2^{n/2}/n)$ while $r\leq 2^{n/2}/n^2$, which the restriction to
one block already gives.  Once $r$ exceeds a suitable polynomial in $n$,
no counting argument over powers of polynomial-size maps reaches the
upper bound of \cref{thm:batch}.

\paragraph{Mass production.}
Uhlig showed that for every $f:\bits^n\to\bits$ and every
$r=2^{o(n/\log n)}$, evaluating $f$ on $r$ independent inputs has
circuits of size $(1+o(1))2^n/n$~\cite{Uhlig74,Uhlig92}.  Applied to each
output bit, this gives $\CC(B_{F,T,r})\leq(1+o(1))2^n$ for every map $F$
in that range of $r$, with no assumption on $\CC(F)$.  So Boolean circuits
have no direct-sum behaviour for repeated evaluation even without
structure.  For polynomial $s$, \cref{eq:batch-opt} is smaller,
$2^{n/2+o(n)}$, throughout this range of $r$, and it remains $O(2^n)$
for all $r\leq N/(n^3A)$.

\paragraph{An open question.}
Is there a family $F_n$ of polynomial size, a constant $0<\alpha<1$,
and exponents $T_n$ such that, for $r=\floor{2^{\alpha n}}$,
\[
  \CC(B_{F_n,T_n,r})\geq 2^{(1+\alpha)n/2-o(n)}?
\]
By \cref{eq:batch-opt} this exponent would be the largest possible.  For
$P_n$ the exponent is $\max\{1/2,\alpha\}$, which is smaller, and
counting cannot answer the question.  We know no polynomial-size family
for which any exponent strictly between $\max\{1/2,\alpha\}$ and
$(1+\alpha)/2$ is proved.

\subsection{A polynomial improvement with AKS sorting networks}
\label{sec:aks}

The factor $n^3$ in \cref{thm:main} has two sources: $O(n)$ gates per
comparator on $O(n)$-bit records, and the $O(\log^2 R)=O(n^2)$ comparators
per record of Batcher's networks.  Ajtai, Koml\'os, and Szemer\'edi
constructed comparator networks sorting $R$ keys with $O(R\log R)$
comparators~\cite{AKS83}.  Replacing Batcher's networks by theirs saves
one factor of $n$.  Their constants are very large, so this is an
asymptotic statement only.  The Lean development mechanizes the Batcher
version; nothing in this subsection is mechanized.

\begin{lemma}[Lookup and repetition with AKS networks]\label[lemma]{lem:lookup-aks}
The dictionary lookup of \cref{lem:lookup} and the repeated-state detection
of \cref{lem:repeat} have circuits of sizes
\[
  O\!\left((M+Q)\bigl(w+\log(M+Q+2)\bigr)\log(M+Q+2)\right)
  \quad\text{and}\quad
  O\!\left(Q\bigl(w+\log(Q+2)\bigr)\log(Q+2)\right),
\]
respectively.  In particular, for $w=O(n)$ and $\log(M+Q+2)=O(n)$ both
costs are $O(n^2(M+Q))$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Repeat the proofs of \cref{lem:lookup,lem:repeat} with every sorting
network replaced by an AKS network on the same records, padded with
dummy records as before.  The networks use $O(R\log(R+2))$ comparators,
each of cost $O(w+\log(R+2))$.  The scans and the selection of a
flagged record cost $O(R(w+\log(R+2)))$ gates and are unchanged.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[AKS-strengthened tradeoff]\label{thm:aks}
Under the assumptions of \cref{thm:main}, put $A_2=s+n^2$.  For every
integer $T\geq0$ and every integer $1\leq L\leq N$,
\[
  \CC(F^T)=O\!\left(Ls+n^2\left(L+\frac NL\right)\right),
  \qquad\text{hence}\qquad
  \CC(F^T)=O\!\left(A_2+n\sqrt{NA_2}\right).
\]
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Use the construction of \cref{sec:fixed} unchanged, and implement its
lookup and repeated-state detection by \cref{lem:lookup-aks}.  These now
cost $O(n^2(N/L+L))$ and $O(n^2L)$ gates.  Every other component already
fits this bound: the two simulations cost $O(Ls)$; the residue-table
multiplexer, the trajectory selections, and the final output selection
cost $O(nL)$; and the long division and modular correction of
\cref{eq:period} cost $O(n^2)$.  For the optimized bound, choose
$L=\max\{1,\ceil{\sqrt{n^2N/A_2}}\}$ and argue as for
\cref{eq:L-choice}, with $n^3$ replaced by $n^2$ and $A$ by $A_2$.
Then $L\leq\ceil{\sqrt N}\leq N$ since $A_2\geq n^2$, and
$\sqrt{n^2NA_2}=n\sqrt{NA_2}$.
\end{proof}

The proofs of the following results use the lookup and repetition
circuits only through the per-record cost of \cref{lem:lookup,lem:repeat},
together with $O(Ls)$ simulation, $O(nL)$ selection, and $O(n^2)$
arithmetic.  Each therefore holds with $n^3$ replaced by $n^2$ in its
parameterized bound and with $A$ replaced by $A_2$ in its optimized bound:
the long-trajectory, invariant-set and image-sensitive bounds
(\cref{thm:active-mass,thm:invariant,cor:image}), exact permutation
inversion (\cref{cor:inverse}), variable-time iteration
(\cref{thm:variable}, by $m$ applications of \cref{thm:aks}), and batched
fixed powers and independent input times
(\cref{thm:batch,cor:batch-variable}).  For the batched bound, the shared
lookup costs $O(n^2(N/L+rL))$ and the $r$ separate repetition tests cost
$O(rn^2L)$, giving
\[
  \CC(B_{F,T,r})=O\!\left(rA_2+n\sqrt{rNA_2}\right).
\]
For the permutation of \cref{sec:lower}, $\CC(P_n)=O(n)$ gives $A_2=O(n^2)$
and $\max_T\CC(P_n^T)=O(n^22^{n/2})$, within a factor $O(n^3)$ of the
lower bound in \cref{thm:lower}.  We do not claim the improvement for the
navigation and target results of \cref{sec:structure} or for
\cref{thm:long-time}, whose proofs contain further $n^3$ terms that we
have not re-examined.

\subsection{Depth}\label{sec:depth-size}

The constructions above were stated for size only; for instance, the
selection scan in the primitives is a sequential chain.  Every component
except the two short simulations of $F$ can be made shallow without
increasing its size by more than a constant factor, so the depth of a
fixed-power circuit is governed by the $O(L)$ sequential applications of
$F$.  Nothing in this subsection is mechanized.

\begin{theorem}[Depth--size tradeoff]\label{thm:depth-size}
Let $F$ have a circuit of size $s$ and depth $d$, and let
$S\subseteq\bits^n$ be forward invariant under $F$ with $|S|=M$; the
case $S=\bits^n$ gives $M=N$.  For every $T\geq0$ and $1\leq L\leq M$
there is a circuit agreeing with $F^T$ on $S$ of size
\[
  O\!\left(Ls+n^3\left(L+\frac ML\right)\right)
  \quad\text{and depth}\quad
  O\!\left(Ld+n^2\log n\right).
\]
With the comparator networks of \cref{sec:aks} the size is
$O(Ls+n^2(L+M/L))$ and the depth is $O(Ld+n\log n)$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Use the construction of \cref{sec:fixed}, with the invariant-set
checkpoints of \cref{thm:invariant}, and implement each component as
follows; all record widths are $w=O(n)$ and all record counts satisfy
$\log(R+2)=O(n)$, since $M\leq N$ and $L\leq N$.

\emph{Arithmetic.}  Addition, subtraction and comparison of $w$-bit
words have circuits of size $O(w)$ and depth $O(\log w)$ by parallel
prefix computation of the carries~\cite{LF80}.  The long division of
\cref{eq:period} is a chain of $O(w)$ conditional subtractions, of size
$O(w^2)$ and depth $O(w\log w)=O(n\log n)$.  The modular correction that
follows has depth $O(\log n)$.

\emph{Selection.}  A multiplexer tree over $q$ words has depth
$O(\log q)$.  To select the first flagged record of a list of $q$, compute
prefix ORs of the flags with a size-$O(q)$, depth-$O(\log q)$ prefix
circuit~\cite{LF80}, mark the unique record whose flag is one and whose
preceding prefix OR is zero, and combine the marked record by a balanced
OR tree; size $O(qw)$ and depth $O(\log q)$.  This replaces the
sequential scan described in the primitives.

\emph{Sorting.}  Batcher's networks~\cite{Batcher68} have
$O(\log^2R)$ comparator layers.  A comparator on $w$-bit records
compares keys in depth $O(\log w)$ and then swaps by multiplexers in depth
$O(1)$.  Each sorting network therefore has depth
$O(\log^2R\cdot\log w)=O(n^2\log n)$.  The networks of~\cite{AKS83} have
$O(\log R)$ layers, giving depth $O(n\log n)$.

\emph{Payload propagation.}  The scan in \cref{lem:lookup} assigns to each
query the payload of the dictionary record at the start of its equal-key
run, if there is one.  Compute the run boundaries by comparing adjacent
keys, in depth $O(\log w)$.  Each record then carries a triple
(boundary bit, dictionary bit, payload), and the required values are the
prefix products of these triples under the associative operation of a
segmented copy scan, which keeps the right operand when it starts a run
or carries a dictionary record and otherwise keeps the left payload.  This
operation has a constant-depth circuit of size $O(w)$, so a prefix
circuit~\cite{LF80} computes all values with size $O(Rw)$ and depth
$O(\log R+\log w)=O(n)$.  Repeated-state detection (\cref{lem:repeat}) is a
sorting network, adjacent comparisons and a first-flag selection, so it
has the same depth bounds as the lookup.

\emph{Simulations.}  If $T<K=2L$, the circuit consists of $T$ copies of $F$
in series, of depth at most $2Ld$.  Otherwise the computation has one
sequential spine: the trajectory \cref{eq:prefix}, of depth $Kd$; then the
lookup and the selection of the checkpoint index $d$; then the second
trajectory of length $K$, of depth $Kd$; then the selection of its entry
$K-d$ and the final branch selection.  The repetition branch runs in
parallel with the lookup and the second trajectory, and consists of
repeated-state detection, the residue multiplexer, the division and a
selection.  Summing along the spine gives depth
$4Ld+O(n^2\log n)$ with Batcher's networks and $4Ld+O(n\log n)$ with the
networks of~\cite{AKS83}.

Each replacement changes the size of its component by at most a constant
factor, so the sizes are those of \cref{thm:invariant} and of its
AKS version in \cref{sec:aks}.
\end{proof}

For example, with $S=\bits^n$ and $L=\ceil{N^{1/4}}$, every $T$-step map
of an $n$-bit iteration has a circuit of depth $O(N^{1/4}d+n^2\log n)$ and
size $O(N^{1/4}s+n^3N^{3/4})$, whereas unrolling has depth $Td$.  More
generally, for $d+n^2\log n\leq D\leq Nd$ the choice
$L=\Theta(D/d)$ gives depth $O(D)$ and size $O(Ds/d+n^3(D/d+Nd/D))$.

The same accounting applies to the batched construction of
\cref{thm:batch}.  Its single shared lookup has
$R=O(N/L+rL)$ records with $\log(R+2)=O(n)$, and the $r$ trajectories,
repetition tests and corrections run in parallel, so
$B_{F,T,r}$ has circuits of the size in \cref{eq:batch-tradeoff} and
depth $O(Ld+n^2\log n)$.  For \cref{thm:variable} the $m$ stages are
composed in series, which gives depth $m\cdot O(Ld+n^2\log n)$; we do not
claim anything better for input times.

\begin{corollary}[Cellular automata]\label[corollary]{cor:ca}
Let $G$ be a cellular automaton on $m$ cells, arranged on a ring or with
fixed boundary values, with finite alphabet $\Sigma$ and a fixed local
rule of constant radius.  Encode each cell by $\ceil{\log_2|\Sigma|}$ bits.
For every $T\geq0$ the $T$-step map of $G$ on configurations in
$\Sigma^m$ has circuits of size
$|\Sigma|^{m/2}\poly(m)$, and also circuits of depth
$|\Sigma|^{m/4}\poly(m)$ and size $|\Sigma|^{3m/4}\poly(m)$.  The
polynomials depend on $|\Sigma|$ and the radius, but not on $T$.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
Let $n=m\ceil{\log_2|\Sigma|}$ and let $F$ apply the local rule to every
cell, mapping invalid cell codes to a fixed symbol.  Each output cell is a
fixed function of a constant number of input bits, so $F$ has size $O(m)$
and depth $O(1)$, and the set $S$ of valid encodings, of size
$|\Sigma|^m$, is forward invariant.  Apply \cref{thm:depth-size} with
$M=|\Sigma|^m$, $s=O(m)$ and $n=O(m)$: choosing $L$ as in
\cref{eq:L-choice}, with $N$ replaced by $M$, gives size $O(m^3\sqrt M)$, and
$L=\ceil{M^{1/4}}$ gives depth $O(M^{1/4}+m^2\log m)$ and size
$O(mM^{1/4}+m^3M^{3/4})$.
\end{proof}

For instance, Wolfram's Rule~30~\cite{Wolfram86} on a ring of $m$ cells
has, for every $T$, a $T$-step circuit of size $2^{m/2}\poly(m)$.
Whether the exponent $1/2$ is sharp for cellular automata is not
established here.  We expect that a radius-one automaton simulating an
in-place implementation of $P_n$ inherits a lower bound of the form
$2^{m/2}/\poly(m)$ from \cref{thm:lower}, but we do not prove it.

\section{Functional-graph navigation}\label{sec:structure}

Write $B=A+\sqrt{n^3NA}$.  For a state $x$, let $\mu(x)$ be its distance
to its eventual cycle, let $\ell(x)$ be that cycle's length, and let $c(x)$
be the lexicographically least state on the cycle.  Define
$\phi(x)\in\{0,\ldots,\ell(x)-1\}$ by
\begin{equation}\label{eq:phase}
 F^t(x)=F^{(\phi(x)+t)\bmod\ell(x)}(c(x))\qquad(t\geq\mu(x)).
\end{equation}
The phase is an eventual-orbit label; it does not assert that a transient
state itself lies on the cycle.  These labels are unique.  Cycle lengths
are encoded with $n+1$ bits, since a cycle may have length $N$.

\begin{theorem}[Cycle labels]\label{thm:labels}
A circuit of size $O(B)$ computes $(\ell(x),c(x),\phi(x))$ for every $x$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Fix $L$ and use the checkpoint construction with $K=2L$.  Hardwire the
three labels for each checkpoint.  If $F^d(x)=h$ for $d\leq K$, output
\[
 \ell(x)=\ell(h),\qquad c(x)=c(h),\qquad
 \phi(x)=(\phi(h)-d)\bmod\ell(h).
\]
This follows by applying \cref{eq:phase} after both transients have ended.
All records still have $O(n)$ bits.

If there is no checkpoint hit, the prefix repeats.  Sort prefix records
by state and then by original index.  Among adjacent equal-state pairs,
choose the pair with smallest second index $j$, retaining its first
index $i$.  This is the first repetition in the original trajectory:
any earliest second occurrence is adjacent to its first occurrence in
its equal-state run.  The states before $j$ are distinct, so
$i=\mu(x)$ and $[i,j)$ consists of exactly one cycle traversal.
Set $\ell=j-i$, and select the minimum state $c=x_p$ over $i\leq p<j$.
Then $\phi(x)=(-p)\bmod\ell$.  Minimum selection and the interval mask
cost $O(nK)$ gates, and word arithmetic costs $O(n^2)$ gates.
Sorting and lookup cost $O(n^3(L+N/L))$, while the prefix costs $O(Ls)$.
Optimizing $L$ proves the claim.  Discarded branches use safe positive
dummy moduli, as in the fixed-power construction.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Transient depth]\label{thm:depth}
The exact transient depth $\mu(x)$ is computable by a circuit of size $O(B)$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Put $D=N!$ and $R=F^D$.  Every trajectory is cyclic after $N$ steps,
every cycle length divides $D$, and $D\geq N$.  Thus $R$ maps into the
cyclic states, fixes each such state, and satisfies $R^2=R$.  It commutes
with all iterates of $F$, so
\begin{equation}\label{eq:projection-depth}
 F^i(x)=F^i(R(x))\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad i\geq\mu(x).
\end{equation}
Indeed, equality is equivalent to $F^i(x)=R(F^i(x))$, which is exactly
cyclicity.  The projection costs $O(B)$ gates by \cref{thm:main}.

Fix a checkpoint parameter $L$ and set $K=2L$.  Retain the first $K+1$
states of both trajectories and compare corresponding states.  If the
states at position $K$ agree, select the first equality; its index is
$\mu(x)$.  Otherwise $\mu(x)>K$, so the original prefix has no repetition
and must meet a checkpoint $h=F^d(x)$ with $d<K$.  Every such hit is
transient and satisfies $\mu(x)=d+\mu(h)$.

Hardwire $\mu(h)$ with each checkpoint and resolve all prefix queries
using \cref{lem:lookup}.  Either branch uses $O(n)$-bit records; comparing,
selecting, and adding indices cost $O(nL)$ gates.  The two prefixes and
shared lookup cost $O(Ls+n^3(L+N/L))$.  Use the same $L$ for the projection
and these operations, then optimize as in \cref{eq:L-choice}.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Compressed checkpoint forest]\label[lemma]{lem:checkpoint-forest}
Let $H$ be the checkpoint set from \cref{lem:checkpoints}.  Cut the outgoing
edge of every cyclic vertex, and let $S$ be the union of the paths from
$H$ to their first cyclic vertices, including both endpoints.  There is
a set $J$ with $H\subseteq J\subseteq S$ and $|J|\leq2|H|$ such that:
\begin{enumerate}
\item Every root and every branching vertex of the forest on $S$ lies in $J$.
\item From each nonroot $a\in J$, the next vertex of $J$ is reached after
some $1\leq d_a\leq2L$ steps.  The segments
$a,F(a),\ldots,F^{d_a-1}(a)$ are pairwise disjoint.
\item Each $a\in J$ has labels $p(a),q(a)<N$ such that, for $a,h\in J$,
\[
 p(a)\leq p(h)\leq q(a)
 \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
 F^t(h)=a\text{ for some }0\leq t\leq\mu(h).
\]
\end{enumerate}
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Take $J$ to be $H$ together with the roots and branching vertices of $S$.
Every leaf belongs to $H$: any other vertex on a checkpoint path has a
predecessor on that path.  In a rooted forest, the number of roots plus
the number of vertices with at least two children is at most the number
of leaves.  Indeed, summing the numbers of children gives the number of
nonroot vertices; a branching vertex contributes at least two.  Therefore
$|J|\leq |H|+|\text{leaves}|\leq2|H|$.

Apply checkpoint coverage to $F(a)$.  A checkpoint hit gives a positive
hit from $a$ within $2L$ steps; a repetition gives cycle entry within $L$
steps, where a root is marked.  Hence the next marked vertex is at
distance at most $2L$.  Between marks, each vertex of $S$ has a unique
predecessor in $S$, so two such segments cannot merge.  Finally order
vertices by a preorder traversal of the cut forest.  Descendants of a
vertex occupy a contiguous interval; its first and last ranks give
$p,q$.  Only the labels of $J$ need to be stored.  The Lean proof obtains
the same interval property by lexicographically ordering root-to-vertex
paths, without formalizing a traversal algorithm.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Reachability and first hitting times]\label{thm:reachability}
A circuit of size $O(B)$, on input $(x,y)$, decides whether
$F^t(x)=y$ for some $t\geq0$ and returns the least such $t$ when it exists.
The output may use a separate existence flag and $n$ time bits.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Use a common parameter $L$ in the depth and cycle-label constructions.
Write $K=2L$ and construct the forest of \cref{lem:checkpoint-forest}.
Store a dictionary of the marked vertices $a\in J$, with their states,
depths, and interval labels.  A second dictionary has one key
$F^{d_a-1}(a)$ for each nonroot mark $a$, with the same payload for $a$.
These last-predecessor keys are distinct by disjointness of the segments.
Both dictionaries have $O(N/L)$ records of $O(n)$ bits.

Suppose first that $y$ is transient.  Generate the prefixes of $x$ and
$y$ through time $K$.  If $y$ occurs in the prefix of $x$, accept.
Otherwise select any marked vertex $h$ in that prefix; if none exists,
reject.  To select a candidate ancestor $a$, use $a=y$ if $y\in J$.
Otherwise query the second dictionary on the prefix of $y$, retain only
matches with $\mu(a)\geq\mu(y)$, and select any retained record.  Reject
if there is no such record.  Finally accept exactly when
\[
 y\in\{a,F(a),\ldots,F^K(a)\}
 \quad\hbox{and}\quad p(a)\leq p(h)\leq q(a).
\]
The first test uses a third short prefix.  It is essential for rejecting
side branches outside $S$.

Every acceptance is sound: $x$ reaches $h$, the interval test says that
$h$ reaches $a$, and the verification prefix reaches $y$.  Conversely,
suppose $x$ reaches a transient $y$ beyond its inspected prefix.
That prefix cannot repeat and must contain a checkpoint.  Every selected
mark $h$ precedes $y$, so $y\in S$.  If $y$ is unmarked, it lies in a
unique compressed segment, whose last-predecessor key appears within
$K$ steps of $y$.  Its lower endpoint passes the depth filter.
Moreover, every retained key has the same property: if
$F^j(y)=F^{d_a-1}(a)$ and $\mu(a)\geq\mu(y)$, decreasing depth gives
$j\leq d_a-1$.  Unique predecessors inside the segment then give
$y=F^{d_a-1-j}(a)$.  A marked $h$ can reach this segment only through
$a$.  Thus the selected record passes both final tests.  The marked-target
case takes $a=y$ directly.

Three prefixes cost $O(Ls)$ gates.  Shared lookup costs
$O(n^3(L+N/L))$, and depth filtering, record selection, prefix equality,
and interval comparisons add $O(nL+n^3)$ gates.  Depths and cycle labels
fit the same tradeoff.  Optimization therefore gives $O(B)$ gates.
For a positive transient query, the first hitting time is simply
$\mu(x)-\mu(y)$, since depth decreases by one on each transient step.

If $y$ is cyclic, it is reachable exactly when $c(x)=c(y)$.  On a positive
instance its first hitting time is
\[
 \mu(x)+\bigl((\phi(y)-\phi(x)-\mu(x))\bmod\ell(x)\bigr),
\]
where the parenthesized residue lies in $[0,\ell(x)-1]$.  This is the
first time on or after entry into the cycle with the required phase.
Finally $\mu(x)+\ell(x)\leq N$, so every first hitting time is at most
$N-1$.  Arithmetic may use $n+1$ intermediate bits.  The time field is unspecified on negative instances; an additional
$O(n)$-gate mask can set it to zero if desired.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Complete visit schedules]\label[corollary]{cor:schedules}
A circuit of size $O(B)$, on input $(x,y)$, returns a success flag and
two integers $d,p$ describing every visit time.  A zero flag denotes no
visits.  For a positive flag, $d<N$ is the first visit, $0\leq p\leq N$,
and
\[
 \{t\geq0:F^t(x)=y\}=\{d+kp:k\geq0\}.
\]
Here $p=0$ for transient $y$, giving the singleton $\{d\}$, and
$p=\ell(y)$ for cyclic $y$.  The period field uses $n+1$ bits.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
Use \cref{thm:reachability} for the flag and $d$, and \cref{thm:labels}
and an $O(B)$ cyclicity test for $p$.  After the first visit, a later
time $t\geq d$ is a visit exactly when $F^{t-d}(y)=y$.  Return times of a
cyclic point are precisely the multiples of its least positive period.
A transient point has no positive return.  This proves the descriptor.
\end{proof}

For example, a visit occurs by deadline $T$ exactly when the flag is
positive and $d\leq T$.  The number of visits at times $0,\ldots,T$ is
\[
 \begin{cases}
 0,&\text{flag zero or }T<d,\\
 1,&\text{flag positive, }T\geq d,\ p=0,\\
 1+\floor{(T-d)/p},&\text{flag positive, }T\geq d,\ p>0.
 \end{cases}
\]
These are arithmetic consequences of the returned descriptor; the
descriptor bound does not include a circuit for processing an additional
deadline input of unspecified length.

\paragraph{Relation to navigation data structures.}
Function powers and path queries have established data-structure
