formulations~\cite{MRRR12,GHN19}.  In particular, nearest labeled
successors include reachability of a fixed singleton target.
Those results charge representation space and query time in a RAM model.
Here the given object is a succinct transition circuit, and every gate
accessing a nonuniform table is charged.  These differences specify the
claim being compared; they do not establish historical novelty of the
present bounds.

\subsection{Cycle spectra and cycle-length transfer}\label{sec:spectrum}

The fixed-power construction stores one payload per checkpoint.  The
following decomposition shows where the information about $T$ actually
resides: in one residue per distinct cycle length.  Write $\ell_F$ for the
map $x\mapsto\ell(x)$, with $(n+1)$-bit outputs, let $c(F)$ be the number
of distinct cycle lengths of $F$, let $\lambda(F)$ be their least common
multiple, and let $\mu(F)=\max_x\mu(x)$.  Since distinct cycle lengths sum
to at most $N$, always $c(F)\leq\sqrt{2N}$.  The semantic identities of
this subsection are checked in Lean, in \texttt{Proofs/Spectrum.lean}:
the reduction $F^T(x)=F^{N+((T-N)\bmod\ell(x))}(x)$ for $T\geq N$
(\texttt{iterate\_eq\_card\_add\_mod}) and the bound $N+((T-N)\bmod\ell(x))<2N$
(\texttt{card\_add\_mod\_lt}); the spectrum test for maps and for permutations
(\texttt{cycle\_test\_iff}, \texttt{cycleSpectrum\_test},
\texttt{injective\_spectrum\_test}); and $P^{-1}(x)=P^{\ell(x)-1}(x)$
(\texttt{perm\_symm\_eq\_iterate}).  The squaring bound is the mechanized
binary-powering construction behind \texttt{variable\_time}.  The gate
counts below, the prime-power test, the order bound and the factoring
example remain prose results outside the Lean formalization.

\begin{theorem}[Spectrum decomposition]\label{thm:spectrum}
For every $T\geq0$,
\[
 \CC(F^T)\leq\CC(\ell_F)+\CC(\Iter_{F,n+1})+O(c(F)\,n).
\]
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
If $T<N$, hardwire $T$ as the $(n+1)$-bit time input of a circuit for
$\Iter_{F,n+1}$.  Otherwise let $\ell_1<\cdots<\ell_c$ be the distinct cycle
lengths and hardwire $r_i=(T-N)\bmod\ell_i$.  Compute $\ell=\ell(x)$, the
flags $e_i=[\ell=\ell_i]$, and $r=\bigvee_ie_i\wedge r_i$; exactly one
flag is set, so $r=(T-N)\bmod\ell(x)$, and this costs $O(cn)$ gates.
Since $r<\ell\leq N$, the integer $u=N+r<2N$ is obtained by setting bit
$n$ above the $n$ bits of $r$, with no gates.  Both $T$ and $u$ are at
least $N\geq\mu(x)$ and are congruent modulo $\ell(x)$, so
$F^T(x)=F^u(x)=\Iter_{F,n+1}(x,u)$.
\end{proof}

Combined with \cref{thm:labels,thm:variable} the right-hand side is
$O(nB)$, so the decomposition is not a better general bound.  Its content
is structural: all dependence on $T$ is confined to the $c(F)$-entry table
$\ell\mapsto T\bmod\ell$.  For $P_n$, $\ell_{P_n}(a,b)$ is $a$ on active
states and $1$ otherwise, which costs $O(n)$ gates; \cref{eq:easy-iterator}
gives $\CC(\Iter_{P_n,n+1})=O(n^2)$; and $c(P_n)=B$.  Hence
\[
 \max_{T\geq0}\CC(P_n^T)=O\!\left(n\,2^{n/2}\right),
\]
a factor $n^2$ below the bound from \cref{thm:main}, and within $O(n^2)$
of \cref{thm:lower}.  A finer treatment of the residue table determines
the leading constant; we do not pursue it here.

The next lemma relates the four quantities that the decomposition
involves.  Write $\Omega(\lambda)$ for the number of prime factors of
$\lambda$ counted with multiplicity, so $\Omega(\lambda)\leq\log_2\lambda$.

\begin{lemma}[Transfer lemmas]\label[lemma]{lem:transfer}
Let $\ell_1<\cdots<\ell_c$ be the distinct cycle lengths of $F$ and
$\lambda=\lambda(F)=\prod_pp^{e_p}$.
\begin{enumerate}
\item\label{it:squaring} For every $m\geq1$,
 $\CC(\Iter_{F,m})\leq\sum_{j<m}\CC(F^{2^j})+O(mn)$.
\item\label{it:spectrum-test}
 $\CC(\ell_F)\leq(c+1)\,\CC(\Iter_{F,n+1})+O(cn)$.  For a permutation
 the factor $c+1$ may be replaced by $c$.
\item\label{it:prime-power-test}
 $\CC(\ell_F)\leq\CC(F^N)+\Omega(\lambda)
 \bigl(\max_{d\mid\lambda}\CC(F^d)+O(n^2)\bigr)$.  For a permutation the
 term $\CC(F^N)$ may be omitted.
\item\label{it:order-count} If $\CC(F^T)\leq s$ for every $T\geq0$, with
 $s\geq n$, then $\lambda(F)\leq2^{O(s\log s)}$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Part~\ref{it:squaring} is the proof of \cref{thm:variable}, with the
circuits for $F^{2^j}$ taken optimal.

For the other parts, put $y=F^N(x)$.  It is cyclic and $\ell(y)=\ell(x)$,
and a cyclic state satisfies $F^d(y)=y$ exactly when $\ell(y)\mid d$.  For
a permutation every state is cyclic and we may take $y=x$.

Part~\ref{it:spectrum-test}.  Compute $y=\Iter_{F,n+1}(x,N)$ and the flags
$g_i=[\Iter_{F,n+1}(y,\ell_i)=y]$; all time inputs are at most $N$.  Then
$g_i=1$ exactly when $\ell(y)\mid\ell_i$.  Since $\ell(y)$ is itself one of
the $\ell_i$, and every multiple of it in the list is at least as large,
the least $\ell_i$ with $g_i=1$ is $\ell(x)$.  Comparisons and the
priority selection cost $O(cn)$ gates.

Part~\ref{it:prime-power-test}.  For a prime $p\mid\lambda$ and
$0\leq f\leq e_p$, put $d_{p,f}=\lambda/p^{e_p-f}$.  Its $q$-adic
valuation is $e_q$ for $q\neq p$ and $f$ for $q=p$.  Since
$\ell(y)\mid\lambda$, the divisibility $\ell(y)\mid d_{p,f}$ holds exactly
when $v_p(\ell(y))\leq f$.  Hence
\[
 v_p(\ell(x))=\min\bigl(\{f<e_p:F^{d_{p,f}}(y)=y\}\cup\{e_p\}\bigr).
\]
This uses $\sum_pe_p=\Omega(\lambda)$ fixed powers applied to $y$, each
followed by an $n$-bit equality test.  Select the hardwired constant
$p^{v_p(\ell(x))}$ among $e_p+1$ candidates and multiply it into a running
product.  Every partial product divides $\ell(x)\leq N$, so each
multiplication has $(n+1)$-bit operands and costs $O(n^2)$ gates.  There
are at most $\Omega(\lambda)$ primes.

Part~\ref{it:order-count}.  Put $\mu=\mu(F)$.  For $\mu\leq a<b<\mu+\lambda$
the difference $b-a$ is not divisible by $\lambda$, so some cycle length
$\ell$ does not divide it.  At a state $y$ on such a cycle,
$F^a(y)\neq F^b(y)$.  Thus $F^\mu,\ldots,F^{\mu+\lambda-1}$ are $\lambda$
distinct maps of complexity at most $s$, and \cref{lem:count} with $q=s\geq n$
gives $\lambda\leq(s+1)16^s(n+s+2)^{2s+n}=2^{O(s\log s)}$.
\end{proof}

Call a function of a family \emph{easy} if it has circuits of size
polynomial in $n$.

\begin{theorem}[Which maps have only easy powers]\label{thm:easy-powers}
Let $F_n$ be maps with $\CC(F_n)=n^{O(1)}$.
\begin{enumerate}
\item\label{it:char} If every $F_n$ is a permutation, then
 $\max_T\CC(F_n^T)=n^{O(1)}$ if and only if $\Iter_{F_n,n}$ and
 $\ell_{F_n}$ are easy and $\log_2\lambda(F_n)=n^{O(1)}$.  In particular,
 if all powers are easy then the cycle length is easy.
\item\label{it:few-lengths} If $c(F_n)=n^{O(1)}$, then
 $\max_T\CC(F_n^T)$ and $\CC(\Iter_{F_n,n+1})$ are polynomially related:
 \[
  \CC(\Iter_{F,n+1})\leq(n+1)\max_T\CC(F^T)+O(n^2),\qquad
  \max_T\CC(F^T)\leq(c+2)\,\CC(\Iter_{F,n+1})+O(cn).
 \]
\item\label{it:inverse} For a permutation $P$,
 $\CC(P^{-1})\leq\CC(\ell_P)+\CC(\Iter_{P,n})+O(n)$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Part~\ref{it:char}, forward direction: part~\ref{it:squaring} of \cref{lem:transfer} with $m=n$ bounds the iterator, part~\ref{it:order-count}
bounds $\log_2\lambda$ by $O(s\log s)$, and part~\ref{it:prime-power-test}
then bounds $\CC(\ell_F)$ by $\log_2\lambda$ times a polynomial.  Reverse
direction: hardwire $T'=T\bmod\lambda$, which has $n^{O(1)}$ bits.  Since
$\ell(x)\mid\lambda$, we have $T\bmod\ell(x)=T'\bmod\ell(x)$.  Compute this
remainder by streaming the bits of $T'$ as in \cref{thm:long-time}, at
$O(n)$ gates per bit.  The remainder is at most $N-1$, so it fits the
$n$-bit time input of $\Iter_{F,n}$, and every state of a permutation is
cyclic, so $F^T(x)=\Iter_{F,n}(x,T'\bmod\ell(x))$.

Part~\ref{it:few-lengths}: the first inequality is
part~\ref{it:squaring} of \cref{lem:transfer}, and the second combines
\cref{thm:spectrum} with part~\ref{it:spectrum-test} of \cref{lem:transfer}.

Part~\ref{it:inverse}: $P^{-1}(x)=P^{\ell(x)-1}(x)$, and $\ell(x)-1\leq N-1$
has $n$ bits.
\end{proof}

More generally, a permutation with easy $\ell$ and an easy iterator has
easy $T$-th power whenever the residues $\ell\mapsto T\bmod\ell$ on its
spectrum are computed by a small circuit.  Part~\ref{it:char} of \cref{thm:easy-powers} fails without the bound on $\lambda$: for $P_n$ the cycle
length and the iterator are easy, but some powers are hard.  Conversely,
the cycle length can be hard while the map, its inverse, and its iterator
are all easy.

\begin{proposition}[Hard cycle length, easy iterator and inverse]
\label[proposition]{prop:hard-cycle-length}
For $w\geq2$ define $P$ on triples $(M,a,x)$ of $w$-bit integers, so
$n=3w$, by
\[
 P(M,a,x)=\begin{cases}
 (M,a,ax\bmod M),&M\geq2,\ a,x<M,\ \gcd(a,M)=\gcd(x,M)=1,\\
 (M,a,x),&\text{otherwise}.
 \end{cases}
\]
Then $P$ is a permutation, and $P$, $P^{-1}$, and $\Iter_{P,m}$ have
circuits of size $\poly(w)$ and $\poly(w,m)$.  If $\ell_P$ has circuits of
size $q$, then a circuit of size $O(w)(q+\poly(w))$ outputs a nontrivial
factor of every composite $w$-bit integer.  Consequently, if no
polynomial-size circuits find a nontrivial factor of every composite
$w$-bit integer, then $\ell_P$ requires superpolynomial circuits.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
For fixed valid $(M,a)$, multiplication by the unit $a$ permutes the units
below $M$, and invalid triples are fixed; so $P$ is a permutation.
Validity tests use gcd circuits, and modular products cost $\poly(w)$
gates.  The inverse multiplies by $a^{-1}\bmod M$, computed by an
extended-Euclid circuit.  The iterator returns $(M,a,a^tx\bmod M)$ on valid
triples, with $a^t\bmod M$ computed by square-and-multiply over the $m$
bits of $t$.  On a valid triple, $a^jx\equiv x$ exactly when $a^j\equiv1$,
so $\ell_P(M,a,x)=\ord_M(a)$.

Now let $M$ be composite.  If $M$ is even, output $2$.  If $M=r^e$ with
$e\geq2$, output $r$; integer $e$-th roots for all $e\leq w$ cost
$\poly(w)$ gates.  Otherwise $M$ is odd with at least two distinct prime
factors.  Draw $a$ uniformly from $[1,M-1]$ by rejection sampling on the
bit length of $M$.  If $\gcd(a,M)>1$, output it.  Otherwise put
$r=\ell_P(M,a,1)=\ord_M(a)$.  If $r$ is even and
$a^{r/2}\not\equiv-1\pmod M$, then $\gcd(a^{r/2}-1,M)$ is a nontrivial
factor, and for such $M$ this happens with probability at least $1/2$
over a uniform unit $a$~\cite{Mil76,Sho97}.  One trial therefore succeeds
with probability at least $1/4$, and $O(w)$ independent trials fail with
probability below $2^{-w-1}$.  A union bound over all $w$-bit $M$ and
Adleman's argument~\cite{Adleman78} fix the random bits.  Each trial uses
one copy of the circuit for $\ell_P$ and $\poly(w)$ further gates.
\end{proof}

By \cref{thm:easy-powers}, the permutation of
\cref{prop:hard-cycle-length} then has superpolynomially many distinct
cycle lengths and some hard fixed powers, under the same hypothesis.  The
hardness obtained is only superpolynomial, since factoring has circuits of
size $2^{\tilde O(w^{1/3})}$.  Whether the cycle length of an explicit
polynomial-size permutation can be hard without number-theoretic
assumptions is a question about space-bounded computation; we do not
pursue it here.

\subsection{Recognizable targets and terminating computations}

\begin{corollary}[First recognizable target]\label[corollary]{cor:target}
Let $P:\bits^n\to\bits$ have circuit size $a$, and put
$A_P=s+a+n^3$.  Eventual target membership,
\[
 H(x)=1\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
 \exists t\geq0:\ P(F^t(x))=1,
\]
has a circuit of size $O(A_P+\sqrt{n^3NA_P})$.  Within the same bound,
a circuit returns the first visited target state and its exact first
visit time on positive instances.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
Set $G(x)=x$ when $P(x)=1$ and $G(x)=F(x)$ otherwise.  This costs
$O(s+a+n)$ gates.  A first target, if one exists, is visited before
step $N$, since an earlier repeated state would preclude a later first
visit.  Hence $G^N(x)$ is the first target state on positive instances,
and $H(x)=P(G^N(x))$.  Apply \cref{thm:main} to $G$.
For the time, apply \cref{thm:depth} to $G$.  On a positive instance,
the first target is an absorbing fixed point.  A cyclic state cannot
precede it on the trajectory: iteration is injective on cyclic states,
so any cyclic predecessor of that fixed point is the same point.
Consequently $\mu_G(x)$ is exactly the first target time.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Complete-configuration simulation]\label[corollary]{cor:halting}
Suppose a deterministic computation has its entire configuration encoded
in $b$ bits and a next-configuration circuit of size $s$.  If halting
configurations are absorbing and every valid initial configuration
halts, its final-configuration map on valid initial states has circuit
size
\[
 O\!\left(s+b^3+\sqrt{b^3 2^b(s+b^3)}\right).
\]
The exact first halting time can also be returned within this bound.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
Before halting, no configuration can repeat.  The iterate at time $2^b$
is therefore the final configuration.  Apply \cref{thm:main} with
state width $b$.  The first halt is the transient depth, by the same
absorbing-state argument as in \cref{cor:target}; use \cref{thm:depth}.
Initialization and output decoding circuits are
charged separately.
\end{proof}
In particular, when $s=\poly(b)$, both the final configuration and the
first halting time have circuit size
\[
  2^{b/2}\poly(b).
\]
For a nearly in-place computation on an $n$-bit input, with complete state
width $b=n+O(\log n)$ and a polynomial-size transition, this specializes to
$2^{n/2}\poly(n)$.  Polynomial-size initialization and output decoding
preserve that bound.  The parameter $b$ includes all persistent input
information, control, and workspace; these bits are counted even when an
abstract machine model calls part of its storage read-only.  A general
polynomial-space machine can have far more than $n+O(\log n)$ configuration
bits, so the corollary gives no such input-length bound for all of PSPACE.
For machines whose only storage is the input tape,
\cref{sec:inplace} replaces the width $b$ by the number of reachable
configurations.

\subsection{In-place Turing machines}\label{sec:inplace}

\Cref{cor:halting} charges the complete configuration width $b$.  For a
Turing machine whose only storage is its input tape, the configurations
actually visited can be far fewer than $2^b$: a ternary tape alphabet
already needs $b\geq2n$ bits, while only $3^n\poly(n)$ configurations
exist.  Combining a locality bound for the step map with
\cref{thm:invariant} on the reachable configurations gives bounds in
terms of the number of reachable configurations, also for machines that
need not halt, and the exponent is attained by an explicit two-head
binary machine.  The Lean module \texttt{Proofs/InPlace.lean} defines the
configurations and the step map $F_M$ of the model below and checks the
counts of configurations and of reachable configurations used in
\cref{thm:inplace} (\texttt{InPlace.card\_config},
\texttt{reach\_card\_le\_configs}, \texttt{reach\_card\_le\_time}), the
stabilization argument of \cref{cor:inplace-decide}
(\texttt{iterate\_card\_halting}), and the application of
\cref{thm:invariant} to the reachable set, precomposed with an
initialization circuit (\texttt{inplace\_fixed\_power\_optimized},
\texttt{inplace\_halting}).  These circuit theorems take as hypotheses a
Boolean encoding of configurations, injective on the reachable set, a
circuit for $F_M$ on that encoding, and a circuit for $x\mapsto\iota(x)$;
\cref{lem:inplace-step}, the width \cref{eq:config-bits} and the
asymptotic simplifications are not mechanized.  For
\cref{prop:inplace-hard}, Lean checks items~2 and~3 for every machine
that satisfies item~1 and whose encoding exposes the tape of an initial
configuration by wiring (\texttt{reach\_card\_le\_round},
\texttt{round\_bounds}); the machine $M^*$ and item~1 are prose.

\subsubsection{Model and step circuit}

An \emph{in-place machine} $M=(Q,\Gamma,h,\delta,q_0,Q_{\mathrm{halt}})$
has one tape whose cells are exactly the $n$ input cells, a finite tape
alphabet $\Gamma\supseteq\bits$, and $h\geq1$ heads on that tape.  In
one step the control reads the state $q$, the $h$ symbols under the
heads, and for each head whether it is on cell~$1$ and whether it is on
cell~$n$.  The transition function $\delta$ returns a new state, a
symbol to write under each head, and a move in $\{-1,0,+1\}$ for each
head; a move past either end leaves the head in place.  When several
heads are on the same cell, the head with the largest index determines
the written symbol.  Halting states are absorbing: on them $\delta$
writes the symbols read and does not move or change state.  Nothing is
assumed about running time.

A configuration is $(w,p_1,\ldots,p_h,q)$ with $w\in\Gamma^n$,
$p_j\in[n]$, and $q\in Q$.  Encode it cell by cell in
\begin{equation}\label{eq:config-bits}
  c=c_M(n)=n\ceil{\log|\Gamma|}+h\ceil{\log n}+\ceil{\log|Q|}
\end{equation}
bits, so that there are $|\Gamma|^n\,n^h\,|Q|$ valid encodings.  Let
$F_M:\bits^c\to\bits^c$ be the one-step map, extended to invalid
encodings by the circuit below; valid encodings are mapped to valid
encodings.  An input $x\in\bits^n$ is identified with the initial
configuration $\iota(x)=(x,1,\ldots,1,q_0)$; the map $\iota$ only
inserts constants.  Let
$\operatorname{Reach}_M(n)$ be the number of configurations
$F_M^t(\iota(x))$ with $t\geq0$ and $x\in\bits^n$.  Write $\CC(\delta)$
for the circuit size of $\delta$ on its
$O(\log|Q|+h\log|\Gamma|+h)$ input bits.

\begin{lemma}[Step circuit]\label[lemma]{lem:inplace-step}
$\CC(F_M)=O(hn+\CC(\delta))$, where the constant depends only on
$|\Gamma|$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Decode each head position into a one-hot vector $e^{(j)}_1,\ldots,
e^{(j)}_n$ with $O(n)$ gates.  The symbol under head $j$ is
$\bigvee_i(e^{(j)}_i\wedge w_i)$, computed bitwise in $O(n)$ gates, and
the two end flags are $e^{(j)}_1$ and $e^{(j)}_n$.  One copy of a circuit
for $\delta$ produces the new state, the written symbols
$\sigma'_1,\ldots,\sigma'_h$, and the moves.  The new content of cell $i$
is obtained from $w_i$ by $h$ successive multiplexers, the $j$th
selecting $\sigma'_j$ when $e^{(j)}_i=1$, so that the largest index
wins; this costs $O(h)$ gates per cell.  Each head update is a clamped
increment or decrement of a $\ceil{\log n}$-bit word, $O(\log n)$ gates.
\end{proof}

This is the only place where the structure of Turing machines is used.
A cell changes only if a head is on it, which is why the step map is
small even though it acts on $\Theta(n)$ bits.

\subsubsection{Circuit bounds}

\begin{theorem}[In-place machines]\label{thm:inplace}
Let $M$ be an in-place machine, let $c$ be as in
\cref{eq:config-bits}, and put $A_M=hn+\CC(\delta)+c^3$ and
$R=\operatorname{Reach}_M(n)$.  For every $T\geq0$ the map
$x\mapsto F_M^T(\iota(x))$ on $\bits^n$ has a circuit of size
\[
  O\!\left(A_M+c^{3/2}\sqrt{R\,A_M}\right).
\]
Always $R\leq|\Gamma|^n n^h|Q|$.  If $M$ halts within $t(n)$ steps on
every input in $\bits^n$, then also $R\leq2^n(t(n)+1)$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
The set $S$ of configurations reachable from $\iota(\bits^n)$ is
forward invariant under $F_M$ and has $|S|=R$.  Apply
\cref{thm:invariant} to $F_M$ on $c$ bits, with $s=O(hn+\CC(\delta))$ by
\cref{lem:inplace-step}, and choose
$L=\max\{1,\ceil{\sqrt{c^3R/A_M}}\}$ as in \cref{eq:L-choice}, with $N$
replaced by $R$; then $1\leq L\leq\ceil{\sqrt R}\leq 2^c$.  Precompose
with $\iota$.  Every valid configuration is counted in the first bound
on $R$.  Under the time bound, each trajectory from $\iota(x)$ visits
at most $t(n)+1$ configurations, because halting configurations are
absorbing.
\end{proof}

For constant $h$, $|Q|$ and $|\Gamma|$ we have $c=O(n)$ and $A_M=O(n^3)$,
so the two bounds on $R$ give
\begin{equation}\label{eq:inplace-bounds}
  O\!\left(n^{3+h/2}\,|\Gamma|^{n/2}\right)
  \qquad\text{and}\qquad
  O\!\left(n^3\,2^{n/2}\sqrt{t(n)+1}\right).
\end{equation}
The circuit depends on $M$, $n$ and $T$; the bound does not depend on $T$.

\begin{corollary}[Predicates decided in place]
\label[corollary]{cor:inplace-decide}
Let $M$ be an in-place machine with constant $h$, $|Q|$ and $|\Gamma|$,
not necessarily halting.  Whether $M$ halts on $x$, whether it accepts
$x$, and its final tape when it halts (arbitrary otherwise) all have
circuits of size $O(n^{3+h/2}|\Gamma|^{n/2})$.  In particular, over a
binary tape alphabet these functions have circuits of size
$2^{n/2+O(\log n)}$, and over a ternary alphabet of size
$2^{0.7925n+O(\log n)}$.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
Let $T^*=|\Gamma|^n n^h|Q|$, the number of valid configurations.  A run
that halts does so within $T^*-1$ steps, since the configurations
before halting are distinct.  A run that has not halted after $T^*$
steps has repeated a configuration; it then cycles forever, and the
cycle contains no halting configuration, because a halting configuration
on a cycle is a fixed point.  So $F_M^{T^*}(\iota(x))$ is a halting
configuration exactly when $M$ halts on $x$, and it is then the final
configuration.  Apply \cref{thm:inplace} with $T=T^*$ and read off the
state and the tape.  Finally $3^{1/2}=2^{0.7925\ldots}$.
\end{proof}

Unlike \cref{cor:halting}, the bound is governed by the reachable
configurations rather than by the encoding width: for a ternary
alphabet $c\geq2n$, so the width bound would give nothing below the
truth table.  For $|\Gamma|\geq4$ the alphabet bound is no better than
the $O(2^n/n)$ truth-table bound, and the reachable-set bound is the one
to use.  The time bound in \cref{eq:inplace-bounds} beats direct
unrolling, which costs $O(n\,t(n))$ gates, only when
$t(n)\geq2^n\poly(n)$.  A machine with a separate read-only input and
$S$ work cells has circuits of size $2^{O(S)}\poly(n)$ by repeated
squaring of its configuration graph; this is better than the present
bounds whenever $S=o(n)$, and the bounds here are specific to the regime
in which the input tape is the work space.

\paragraph{Several heads.}
The heads cost the factor $n^{h/2}$ in the alphabet bound.  For a binary
alphabet, $2^{n/2}n^{h/2}\poly(n)$ is below the truth-table bound
$2^n/n$ exactly when $h\log n\leq n(1-o(1))$, that is, for
$h=o(n/\log n)$ heads.  With $h=\Theta(n)$ heads the bound is vacuous,
as it should be: $n$ heads can hold $n$ bits of information outside
the tape.  The model is in-place in the tape, not in the heads.

\subsubsection{Sharpness}

The permutation $P_n$ of \cref{eq:permutation} can be run in place, and
the counting lower bound of \cref{thm:lower} then transfers to the
machine.

\begin{proposition}[A two-head binary machine with a hard power]
\label[proposition]{prop:inplace-hard}
There is an in-place machine $M^*$ with two heads, tape alphabet $\bits$
and a constant number of states, and for each even $n\geq2$ an integer
$\tau=\tau(n)=\Theta(n)$, such that:
\begin{enumerate}
\item for every $x\in\bits^n$, $F_{M^*}^{\tau}(\iota(x))=\iota(P_n(x))$;
\item $\operatorname{Reach}_{M^*}(n)\leq2^n\tau$;
\item for all sufficiently large even $n$,
\[
  \Omega\!\left(\frac{2^{n/2}}{n}\right)
  \leq\max_{T\geq0}\CC\bigl(x\mapsto F_{M^*}^T(\iota(x))\bigr)
  \leq O\!\left(n^{7/2}2^{n/2}\right).
\]
\end{enumerate}
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
Let $n=2k$.  The input is $x=(a,b)$ with $a$ in cells $1,\ldots,k$ and
$b$ in cells $k+1,\ldots,2k$, most significant bit first.  The state
consists of a phase, a parity bit, and three flags $\mathit{lt}$,
$\mathit{diff}$ and $\mathit{carry}$.  We design $\delta$ so that the
moves of both heads and the changes of phase and parity depend only on
the phase, the parity, and the end flags, never on symbols or flags;
symbols and flags influence only what is written and how the flags are
updated.  Consequently the sequence of head positions, phases and
parities is the same for every input, and a round has an
input-independent length $\tau$.  Head~2 never changes a symbol (it
always writes what it reads), and head~1 changes symbols only in phases
3 and~5, during which it is on cells $k+1,\ldots,2k$ and head~2 is on
cells $1,\ldots,k$; so the write-conflict rule never matters.  Each
phase below processes the cells under the heads in every step, and
moves on to the next phase in the step in which its stopping condition
is read.
\begin{enumerate}
\item \emph{Midpoint.}  Both heads start on cell $1$.  In each step
  head~2 moves right and head~1 moves right when the parity bit is $0$;
  the parity bit flips.  Stop when head~2 is on cell $2k$.  This takes
  $2k-1$ moves of head~2, during which head~1 moves $k$ times, so it
  stops on cell $k+1$.
\item \emph{Rewind head~2.}  Move head~2 left until it is on cell $1$,
  in $2k-1$ moves.  Set $\mathit{lt}=\mathit{diff}=0$.
\item \emph{Compare.}  Heads~2 and~1 start on cells $1$ and $k+1$ and
  move right in lockstep, stopping when head~1 is on cell $2k$, so that
  they read the pairs $(a_i,b_i)$ for $i=1,\ldots,k$.  If
  $\mathit{diff}=0$ and $a_i\neq b_i$, set $\mathit{diff}=1$ and
  $\mathit{lt}=a_i$.  At the end, $\mathit{lt}=1$ exactly when $b<a$,
  since the first differing bit decides the comparison.  Set
  $\mathit{carry}=1$ and $\mathit{diff}=0$.
\item \emph{Increment.}  Move both heads left in lockstep from cells
  $(k,2k)$, stopping when head~2 is on cell $1$, so that head~1 visits
  cells $2k,2k-1,\ldots,k+1$.  At each cell head~1 writes
  $b_i\oplus\mathit{carry}$ if $\mathit{lt}=1$ and $b_i$ otherwise, and
  $\mathit{carry}$ becomes $b_i\wedge\mathit{carry}$.  If $\mathit{lt}=1$
  the block now holds $b+1$; there is no overflow because
  $b+1\leq a\leq2^k-1$.
\item \emph{Compare again.}  As in phase~3, the heads move right from
  cells $1$ and $k+1$ to cells $k$ and $2k$; set $\mathit{diff}=1$ at the
  first $i$ with $a_i\neq b'_i$, where $b'$ is the current second block,
  and leave $\mathit{lt}$ unchanged.  At the end, $\mathit{diff}=0$
  exactly when $b'=a$.
\item \emph{Zero.}  As in phase~4, head~1 visits cells
  $2k,\ldots,k+1$ and writes $0$ if $\mathit{lt}=1$ and
  $\mathit{diff}=0$, and rewrites the symbol read otherwise.
\item \emph{Return.}  Move head~1 left until it is on cell $1$, in $k$
  moves; head~2 is already there.  Clear the flags and enter $q_0$.
\end{enumerate}
Counting the stopping step of each phase, the round takes
$\tau=9k+O(1)=\Theta(n)$ steps, the same for every input.  If $b\geq a$, which
includes every state with $a=0$, then $\mathit{lt}=0$ and nothing is
changed.  If $b<a$, the second block becomes $b+1$, and then $0$ exactly
when $b+1=a$; that is, it becomes $(b+1)\bmod a$.  The first block is
never changed.  Comparing with \cref{eq:permutation}, the tape after one
round is $P_n(x)$, both heads are on cell $1$, and the state is $q_0$,
which is item~1.

For item~2, item~1 shows that every configuration reachable from
$\iota(x)$ has the form $F_{M^*}^j(\iota(y))$ with $y=P_n^i(x)$ and
$0\leq j<\tau$.  There are at most $2^n\tau$ such configurations.

For item~3, item~1 gives $F_{M^*}^{\tau T}(\iota(x))=\iota(P_n^T(x))$.
The tape bits of this configuration are $P_n^T(x)$, so a circuit for
$x\mapsto F_{M^*}^{\tau T}(\iota(x))$ computes $P_n^T$ as a projection,
and \cref{thm:lower} supplies $T$ with
$\CC(P_n^T)=\Omega(2^{n/2}/n)$.  For the upper bound apply
\cref{thm:inplace} with $h=2$, $|\Gamma|=2$, $\CC(\delta)=O(1)$,
$c=n+O(\log n)$ and $R\leq2^n\tau=O(n2^n)$:
$O(c^{3/2}\sqrt{RA_M})=O(n^{3/2}\cdot n^{1/2}2^{n/2}\cdot n^{3/2})
=O(n^{7/2}2^{n/2})$.
\end{proof}

The alphabet bound of \cref{thm:inplace} gives $O(n^4 2^{n/2})$ here;
the reachable-set bound is slightly better.  Both match the lower bound
in the exponent.  With a single head the same permutation can be run in
place using a second marker track, that is, with $\Gamma=\bits^2$ and
$\tau=\Theta(n^2)$; we omit the details.  The alphabet bound is then
$2^n\poly(n)$, and only the reachable-set bound
$\sqrt{2^n\tau}\,\poly(n)=2^{n/2}\poly(n)$ matches the lower bound.

As for $P_n$ itself, the hard exponents of $M^*$ are long, and its
iterator with a short time input is easy.  For an $m$-bit $t$ write
$t=\tau T+\rho$ with $0\leq\rho<\tau$; dividing by the fixed integer
$\tau$ costs $O(m\log n)$ gates.  Then
$F_{M^*}^t(\iota(x))=F_{M^*}^{\rho}(\iota(P_n^T(x)))$: compute
$P_n^T(x)$ with the iterator of \cref{eq:easy-iterator}, generate the
$\tau$ configurations of the next round with $\tau$ copies of
$F_{M^*}$, and select entry $\rho$.  This costs $\poly(n,m)$ gates, so
every $T$ witnessing the lower bound in item~3 has
$2^{\Omega(n)}$ bits, exactly as in \cref{cor:exponent-length}.

Everything in this subsection is nonuniform.  The checkpoint payloads
$F_M^{T-K}(h)$ may take time proportional to $T$ to compute, and no
bound for a specific, efficiently described $T$ is obtained.

\subsection{Synchronous and recurring queries}\label{sec:eventual-queries}

The projection $R=F^{N!}$ from the proof of \cref{thm:depth} also supplies
queries about the eventual dynamics.

\begin{corollary}[Eventual synchronous coalescence]\label[corollary]{cor:coalescence}
A circuit of size $O(B)$ decides, on input $(x,y)$, whether there exists
$t\geq0$ such that $F^t(x)=F^t(y)$.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
The required equivalence is
\[
 \exists t\geq0:\ F^t(x)=F^t(y)
 \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad F^N(x)=F^N(y).
\]
Equality before time $N$ persists to time $N$.  Both states are cyclic
by that time, and $F$ restricts to a bijection on the cyclic states.
Therefore later equality can be canceled back to time $N$.  Compute
two copies of $F^N$ and compare their outputs.
\end{proof}

This query requires the same elapsed time on both trajectories.  Orbit
intersection permits different elapsed times and is a different predicate;
for example, distinct points of one cycle never coalesce synchronously.

\begin{corollary}[Recurring and permanent targets]\label[corollary]{cor:recurring}
For a fixed predicate $P:\bits^n\to\bits$ of circuit size $a$, each of
\[
 \forall u\ \exists t\geq u:\ P(F^t(x))=1,
 \qquad
 \exists u\ \forall t\geq u:\ P(F^t(x))=1
\]
is decidable by a circuit of size
$O(A_P+\sqrt{n^3NA_P})$, where $A_P=s+a+n^3$.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
A target recurs arbitrarily late exactly when the eventual cycle contains
an accepting state.  Start at $R(x)$ and use the absorbing-target
construction in \cref{cor:target}.  If $G$ makes accepting states
absorbing, the circuit outputs $P(G^N(R(x)))$.  A successful visit is
periodic and hence recurs arbitrarily late; every sufficiently late visit
from $x$ is a future visit from $R(x)$.  The projection and the target
circuit each obey the stated bound.  Permanent eventual acceptance is
the complement of recurrence of the predicate $1-P$, which changes the
bound only by a constant number of gates.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Short exponents and polynomial space}\label{sec:short-exponents}

The hard powers of \cref{sec:lower} have exponentially long exponents
(\cref{cor:exponent-length}).  For exponents with polynomially many bits,
the question changes character: \cref{cor:halting} has a vacuous bound
when the configuration width is polynomial rather than $n+O(\log n)$.
This subsection shows that hardness of short powers is exactly a circuit
lower bound for $\mathsf{PSPACE}$.  None of its results is covered by the
Lean formalization.

Families are indexed by the state width.  A family of functions $(G_n)$
is \emph{superpolynomially hard} if for every $k$ its complexity
$\CC(G_n)$ exceeds $n^k$ for infinitely many $n$.

\begin{theorem}[Short exponents and $\mathsf{PSPACE}$]\label{thm:pspace-short}
The following are equivalent.
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
\item $\mathsf{PSPACE}\not\subseteq\mathsf{P}/\mathrm{poly}$.
\item There are maps $F_n$ with $\CC(F_n)=O(n)$ and exponents
  $T_n\leq 2^n$ such that $(F_n^{T_n})$ is superpolynomially hard.
\item There are maps $F_n$ with $\CC(F_n)=n^{O(1)}$ and exponents
  $T_n\leq 2^{n^{O(1)}}$ such that $(F_n^{T_n})$ is superpolynomially hard.
\end{enumerate}
The same equivalence holds with $\mathsf{P}/\mathrm{poly}$ replaced by
subexponential size: some language in $\mathsf{PSPACE}$ has, for some
$\delta>0$, complexity at least $2^{n^\delta}$ infinitely often if and
only if some such family has $\CC(F_n^{T_n})\geq 2^{n^{\delta'}}$
infinitely often for some $\delta'>0$.  In (ii) the maps can be taken
to be permutations, with $T_n$ a power of two.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
(ii)$\Rightarrow$(iii) is immediate.

(iii)$\Rightarrow$(i).  Suppose $\mathsf{PSPACE}\subseteq\mathsf{P}/\mathrm{poly}$.
Let $\CC(F_n)\leq n^a$ and $T_n\leq 2^{n^c}$.  Consider the language $U$
of tuples $\langle 1^n,D,t,x,i\rangle$ such that $D$ encodes a circuit of
size at most $n^a$ on $n$ inputs, $t$ has $n^c+1$ bits, $x\in\bits^n$,
$i\leq n$, and bit $i$ of $D^{\operatorname{val}(t)}(x)$ is one.  Use a
fixed-length encoding of the fields for each $n$, so that the length
$m(n)$ of a tuple is a polynomial in $n$.  A machine decides $U$ in
polynomial space: it evaluates $D$ repeatedly, storing the current state
and a counter of $n^c+1$ bits.  By assumption $U$ has circuits of size
$m^e$ for some $e$.  Hardwiring the description of $F_n$, the exponent
$T_n$, and each index $i$ gives $\CC(F_n^{T_n})\leq n\,m(n)^e=n^{O(1)}$
for all large $n$, so (iii) fails.

(i)$\Rightarrow$(ii).  Let $L\in\mathsf{PSPACE}\setminus\mathsf{P}/\mathrm{poly}$,
decided by a deterministic one-tape machine $M$ that uses the cells
$1,\ldots,S(n)$ with $S(n)=n^c$, halts on every input, and returns its
head to cell $1$ before halting.  These normalizations cost only a
polynomial in space.  Encode a configuration cell by cell: each cell
holds a tape symbol and a control field, which is zero when the head is
absent and otherwise names the machine state.  With $r=O(1)$ bits per
cell, the state width is $b=rS(n)$.  Choose the symbol encoding so that
the initial configuration on $w$ is obtained from $w$ by inserting
constant bits; this map $\iota$ costs no gates.

The next-configuration map acts on each cell by a fixed function of that
cell and its two neighbours, so it has a circuit of size $O(b)$, and it
fixes halting configurations.  Use it on all $2^b$ strings; on strings
that are not valid configurations its values are irrelevant.  Valid
configurations do not repeat before halting, so, as in the proof of
\cref{cor:halting}, $F_b^{2^b}(\iota(w))$ is the halting configuration
of $M$ on $w$.  Acceptance is read from the control field of cell $1$
with $O(1)$ gates.  Hence
\[
 \CC(L_n)\leq\CC\bigl(F_{b(n)}^{2^{b(n)}}\bigr)+O(1).
\]
Since $b(n)$ is strictly increasing, define $F_b$ to be the identity on
widths outside its range.  If $\CC(F_b^{2^b})\leq b^k$ for all large $b$,
then $\CC(L_n)\leq O(n^{ck})$ for all large $n$, contradicting the choice
of $L$.  The permutation strengthening is \cref{prop:pspace-permutation}.

For the subexponential version, the argument for (iii)$\Rightarrow$(i)
gives $\CC(F_n^{T_n})\leq n\,2^{m(n)^\delta}$ whenever every language in
$\mathsf{PSPACE}$ has complexity at most $2^{m^\delta}$ for all large $m$;
since $m(n)\leq n^e$, taking $\delta$ small rules out
$\CC(F_n^{T_n})\geq 2^{n^{\delta'}}$ infinitely often.  Conversely, if
$\CC(L_n)\geq 2^{n^\delta}$ infinitely often, the construction above gives
$\CC(F_b^{2^b})\geq 2^{n^\delta}-O(1)\geq 2^{b^{\delta/(2c)}}$ for those
$n$ when $n$ is large.
\end{proof}

The next construction uses reversible machines.  Lange, McKenzie, and
Tapp~\cite{LMT00} show that every language decided in deterministic space
$S(n)\geq\log n$ is decided by a reversible machine in space $O(S(n))$
that halts on every input.  Each configuration of a reversible machine
has at most one successor and at most one predecessor.  Both, and whether
they exist, are determined by the constant number of cells around the
head~\cite{Bennett73,LMT00}.  We count configurations without a successor
in a nonhalting state as rejecting halts.

\begin{proposition}[Hard permutations and cycle lengths]\label[proposition]{prop:pspace-permutation}
Suppose $L\in\mathsf{DSPACE}(S(n))$ with $S(n)\geq n$ space-constructible.
There are permutations $P_b$ of $\bits^b$ with $b=O(S(n))$ and
$\CC(P_b)=O(b)$, an exponent $T=2^{b'}$ with $b'<b$, and a
constant-insertion map $\iota$ from $\bits^n$ to $\bits^b$, such that
for every $w\in\bits^n$:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\alph*)]
\item one fixed output bit of $P_b^{T}(\iota(w))$ equals $L(w)$;
\item $L(w)=1$ exactly when $\ell(\iota(w))\geq 2^{b'+1}$.
\end{enumerate}
Consequently, $\mathsf{PSPACE}\not\subseteq\mathsf{P}/\mathrm{poly}$ if and
only if there are polynomial-time constructible permutations $P_b$ with
$\CC(P_b)=O(b)$ whose cycle-length maps $x\mapsto\ell(x)$ are
superpolynomially hard.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Let $R$ be a reversible machine deciding $L$ in space $O(S)$, encoded cell
by cell as in \cref{thm:pspace-short}.  Let $V$ be the set of valid
configurations, those with exactly one head, and let $2^{v}$ be the number
of strings of the configuration field, so $|V|\leq 2^{v}$.  Put
$D=2^{v+2}$.  The state set consists of
\[
 (C,+),\quad (C,-)\qquad (C\in V),
 \qquad\qquad
 (C,j)\qquad(C\in V\ \text{accepting halting},\ 0\leq j<D).
\]
Define $P$ by
\begin{align*}
 (C,+)&\mapsto(R(C),+)&&\text{if $C$ has a successor},\\
 (C,+)&\mapsto(C,-)&&\text{if $C$ is a rejecting halt},\\
 (C,+)&\mapsto(C,0)&&\text{if $C$ is an accepting halt},\\
 (C,j)&\mapsto(C,j+1)&&\text{if $j<D-1$, and $(C,D-1)\mapsto(C,-)$},\\
 (C,-)&\mapsto(R^{-1}(C),-)&&\text{if $C$ has a predecessor},\\
 (C,-)&\mapsto(C,+)&&\text{otherwise}.
\end{align*}
Every state has exactly one preimage.  For $(C',+)$ it is $(R^{-1}(C'),+)$
if $C'$ has a predecessor and $(C',-)$ otherwise.  For $(C',-)$ it is
$(R(C'),-)$, $(C',+)$, or $(C',D-1)$, according as $C'$ has a successor,
is a rejecting halt, or is an accepting halt.  For $(C',j)$ with $j\geq1$
it is $(C',j-1)$, and for $(C',0)$ it is $(C',+)$.  Thus $P$ is a
permutation of the state set.

Encode a state by the configuration field, a direction bit, a detour bit,
and a $(v+2)$-bit counter, so $b=2v+4=O(S)$; strings encoding no state
are fixed points.  Validity is a linear-size test, and the other
conditions are local at the head, so $\CC(P_b)=O(b)$.  Fixing the
strings that encode no state preserves bijectivity.

Let $C_0,\ldots,C_k$ be the maximal configuration path through the initial
configuration $I_w$, with $C_0$ having no predecessor and $C_k$ halting.
It has no repetition, since the forward path from $I_w$ halts and $R$ is
injective, so $k+1\leq 2^{v}$.  The cycle of $(I_w,+)$ visits
$(C_0,+),\ldots,(C_k,+)$, then the $D$ detour states if $C_k$ accepts,
then $(C_k,-),\ldots,(C_0,-)$.  Its length is $2(k+1)+D\cdot L(w)$.  Since
$2(k+1)\leq 2^{v+1}<D$, part (b) holds with $b'=v+1$, because
$2^{b'+1}=D$.  For part (a), take $T=2^{b'}=D/2$.  From $(I_w,+)$ the
first detour state is reached after at most $k+1\leq 2^v<D/2$ steps and
the detour lasts $D$ steps, so $P^{T}(\iota(w))$ is a detour state when
$L(w)=1$.  When $L(w)=0$ the cycle contains no detour states.  The detour
bit is the required output.

For the equivalence, if $\mathsf{PSPACE}\not\subseteq\mathsf{P}/\mathrm{poly}$,
apply (b) to a hard $L$ with polynomial $S$; testing $\ell\geq 2^{b'+1}$
costs $O(b)$ gates, and the width bookkeeping is as in
\cref{thm:pspace-short}.  Conversely, $\ell(x)$ is computable in polynomial
space from a circuit for $P_b$ by walking the cycle with a $(b+1)$-bit
counter, and the advice argument of \cref{thm:pspace-short} gives
polynomial circuits for $\ell$ when $\mathsf{PSPACE}\subseteq\mathsf{P}/\mathrm{poly}$.
\end{proof}

Part (a) completes the permutation clause of \cref{thm:pspace-short}.
The exponent $T=2^{b'}$ is explicit and has $O(b)$ bits.  This contrasts
with \cref{sec:lower}, where an explicit permutation has hard powers
at exponents of exponential length, without identifying an explicit hard
exponent.  Here the permutation depends on a hard language, and its power
at a single short exponent inherits hardness from that language.

\begin{remark}[Linear space and exponential bounds]\label[remark]{rem:linear-space}
The polynomial loss in the state width prevents a $2^{\varepsilon n}$
version of \cref{thm:pspace-short}.  Linear space gives one up to advice.
If $\mathsf{DSPACE}(O(n))\not\subseteq\mathsf{SIZE}(2^{\varepsilon n})$
infinitely often, the construction of \cref{thm:pspace-short} with
$S(n)=O(n)$ gives maps with $\CC(F_b)=O(b)$, $T_b=2^b$, and
$\CC(F_b^{T_b})\geq 2^{\varepsilon' b}$ infinitely often, with
$\varepsilon'>0$ depending on $\varepsilon$ and the machine.  Conversely,
for such a family the power $F_b^{T_b}$ is computable in space $O(b)$
from $O(b\log b)$ bits of advice describing $F_b$ and $T_b$: store all
gate values and a counter.  Hence
$\mathsf{DSPACE}(O(n))/O(n\log n)\not\subseteq\mathsf{SIZE}(2^{\varepsilon''n})$
infinitely often for some $\varepsilon''>0$.  The two statements differ
only in the advice.
\end{remark}

\subsection{Machines with a separate work tape}\label{sec:space-bounded}

\Cref{cor:halting} charges every configuration bit, including a
read-only input.  For a space-bounded machine this gives a bound in
terms of input length plus work space.  The results of this subsection
are not mechanized in the Lean development.

Let $M$ be a deterministic machine with a read-only input tape holding
$x\in\bits^n$ between two endmarkers, one work tape over an alphabet
$\Gamma$ that contains the blank, $h_1$ input heads, $h_2$ work heads,
and a finite state set $Q$; put $h=h_1+h_2$.  Halting states are
absorbing, and on every input of length $n$ the machine halts and uses
only work cells $1,\ldots,S=S(n)$.  A configuration
$(x,w,p,q)$ consists of the input, the work contents $w\in\Gamma^S$, the
head positions $p$, and the state.  Encode it in
\[
  b=n+S\ceil{\log|\Gamma|}+h\ceil{\log(n+S+2)}+\ceil{\log|Q|}
\]
bits, and let $F_M:\bits^b\to\bits^b$ be the one-step map, with
$\iota(x)$ the initial configuration on input $x$.  Reading the symbols
under the heads, writing, and moving the heads cost
$s=O(h(n+S\log|\Gamma|)+h\log(n+S))$ gates for fixed $Q$ and $\Gamma$:
decode each head position to a one-hot vector, select the symbols by
multiplexers, apply one circuit for the transition function, and
update each work cell and head position locally.

\begin{theorem}[Space-bounded machines]\label{thm:space-bounded}
Let $M$ be as above, with $h$, $|Q|$ and $|\Gamma|$ fixed.  The language
decided by $M$ has circuits on $\bits^n$ of size
\[
  2^{(n+S\log|\Gamma|)/2}\,(n+S)^{O(1)},
\]
where the $O(1)$ depends on $h$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Let $R$ be the set of configurations $F_M^t(\iota(x))$ with $t\geq0$ and
$x\in\bits^n$.  It is forward invariant, and every configuration in it
has input part $x$, work contents in $\Gamma^S$ and heads within their
ranges, so
\[
  |R|\leq 2^n|\Gamma|^S(n+S+2)^h|Q|.
\]
Before halting, a run visits distinct configurations of $R$, so
$F_M^{|R|}(\iota(x))$ is the final configuration.  Apply
\cref{thm:invariant} with state width $b$, the set $R$, and $T=|R|$,
choosing $L$ as in \cref{eq:L-choice} with $N$ replaced by $|R|$.  With
$A=s+b^3$ the size is $O(A+\sqrt{b^3|R|A})$.  Since $b$ and $s$ are
$O(n+S)$, this is $(n+S)^{O(1)}\sqrt{|R|}$.  Precompose with $\iota$,
which inserts constants, and read the acceptance bit from the state.
\end{proof}

For a binary work alphabet the encoding has $b=n+S+O(\log(n+S))$ bits,
and the theorem already follows from \cref{cor:halting}.  For other
alphabets the reachable set matters, since $S\ceil{\log|\Gamma|}$ can
exceed $S\log|\Gamma|$ by a constant factor.

The bound is new only in a narrow range.  Every Boolean function on $n$
bits has a circuit of size $O(2^n/n)$.  The standard simulation squares
the configuration map for the given input about $S\log|\Gamma|$ times;
each squaring composes two tables with $|\Gamma|^S\poly(n,S)$ entries,
for $|\Gamma|^{2S}\poly(n,S)$ gates in total.  For a binary work tape and
$S=\sigma n$ with a constant $\sigma>0$, the three exponents are
$(1+\sigma)/2$, $1$ and $2\sigma$.  The first is the smallest exactly
when $1/3<\sigma<1$.  For instance, a language decided in space $n/2$
with a binary work tape has circuits of size $2^{3n/4}\poly(n)$.  We have
not found this bound in the literature.  It combines checkpointing with
locality and may be known.  At $S=n$ it gives only $2^n\poly(n)$; the
square-root bound $2^{n/2}\poly(n)$ of \cref{cor:halting} needs the input
to serve as the workspace.

\paragraph{Improving the square-root bound for in-place computations.}
Improving the exponent $1/2$ for computations that work in place would
improve the generic bound for linear space.  We make this precise with
the configuration-map setting of \cref{cor:halting}.  A \emph{nearly
in-place computation} is a family of maps
$G_n:\bits^{b(n)}\to\bits^{b(n)}$ with
$n<b(n)\leq n+c\ceil{\log(n+1)}$ and $\CC(G_n)\leq n^c+c$ for a constant
$c$.  Its acceptance predicate on $\bits^n$ is
\[
  \operatorname{acc}_G(x)=\text{last bit of }
  G_n^{2^{b(n)}}\bigl(x0^{b(n)-n}\bigr).
\]
The time $2^{b(n)}$ is at least the number of states, so for a halting
computation with absorbing halting states this is the last bit of the
final state.  \Cref{thm:main} gives
$\CC(\operatorname{acc}_G)\leq2^{n/2}\poly(n)$.  An in-place machine with
a binary tape alphabet and a constant number of heads is an example: its
configuration consists of the $n$ tape bits and $O(\log n)$ bits for
heads and state.

\begin{proposition}[Padding linear space]\label[proposition]{prop:space-padding}
Fix $\varepsilon>0$.  Suppose that every nearly in-place computation
satisfies $\CC(\operatorname{acc}_G)\leq2^{(1/2-\varepsilon)n}$ for all
sufficiently large $n$.  Let $L$ be decided by a machine $M$ as above
with a binary work alphabet, constant $h$, and a nondecreasing space
bound $S(n)$ with $1\leq S(n)\leq n^{O(1)}$.  Then
\[
  \CC(L\cap\bits^n)\leq 2^{(1/2-\varepsilon)(n+S(n))}
\]
for all sufficiently large $n$.  In particular, every language decided
in space $n$ with a binary work tape has circuits of size
$2^{(1-2\varepsilon)n}$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Put $m(n)=n+S(n)$, which is strictly increasing.  For $m=m(n)$, let
$G_m$ act on configurations of $M$ on inputs of length $n$, laid out as
the input $x$, then the $S$ work bits, then a field $z$ that holds the
head positions, the state, and an acceptance bit in its last position.
Choose the encoding of $z$ so that $z=0\cdots0$ is the initial head
positions and state, and encode the blank by $0$.  Let $G_m$ be the step
map of $M$ that sets the acceptance bit when it enters an accepting
halting state.  The field $z$ has $O(\log(n+S))=O(\log m)$ bits, and
$\CC(G_m)=O(m)$ by locality, so for some constant $c$ this is a nearly
in-place computation once $G_m$ is defined as the identity on $m+1$
bits at the lengths $m$ outside the range of $m(\cdot)$.

On $y=x0^S$, the state $y0\cdots0$ encodes the initial configuration of
$M$ on $x$.  The machine halts within the number of states, so
$\operatorname{acc}_G(x0^S)=1$ exactly when $M$ accepts $x$.  Fixing the
last $S$ inputs of a circuit for $\operatorname{acc}_G$ on $\bits^m$ to
zero gives a circuit for $L\cap\bits^n$ of size at most
$2^{(1/2-\varepsilon)m}$.  For $S(n)=n$ the size is
$2^{(1/2-\varepsilon)2n}$.
\end{proof}

A bound of $2^{(1-\delta)n}$ for linear space with a binary work tape
would beat the $\Theta(2^n/n)$ worst case for $n$-bit functions by an
exponential factor.  We are not aware of such a bound, and we do not
know whether the hypothesis of \cref{prop:space-padding} holds for any
$\varepsilon>0$.  We do not prove the proposition with the hypothesis
restricted to in-place Turing machines.  That would require the padded
machine to find the end of $x$ itself, with no symbols to spare.

The hypothesis concerns one predicate per input length, at the fixed time
$2^{b(n)}$.  For the configuration map, or for a predicate at a time chosen
for each $n$, the analogous improvement is false.  The map $P_n$, with one
idle extra bit, is a nearly in-place computation, and \cref{thm:lower,cor:one-output} give
exponents $T_n$ for which $P_n^{T_n}$, and some output bit of it, need
$2^{n/2}/\poly(n)$ gates.  Those exponents come from counting over $T$.
Counting says nothing about a single predicate per length.

\section{Structured transitions}\label{sec:structured}

The universal bound uses only the state width and the size of one
transition.  Additional structure can give compact circuits for whole
iterates even when an orbit visits exponentially many states.  Affine
payload updates stay affine along each autonomous controller trajectory;
sparse counter edits give orbit coordinates described by a few intervals.
In both cases, selecting and evaluating the resulting description has a
charged circuit cost that can improve on the universal bound.

\subsection{Small controllers with affine payloads}\label{sec:controller}

The next result isolates all exponential cost in the autonomous controller,
allowing a much larger affine payload.

\begin{theorem}[Affine-controller powering]\label{thm:affine-controller}
Let $q+p=n\geq1$, and write a state as $(u,v)\in\bits^q\times\mathbb F_2^p$.
Suppose
\[
 F(u,v)=\bigl(g(u),A(u)v+b(u)\bigr),
\]
where $g:\bits^q\to\bits^q$, $A(u)$ is a $p\times p$ binary matrix,
and $b(u)\in\mathbb F_2^p$.  For every fixed integer $T\geq0$,
\[
 \CC(F^T)=O\!\left(2^q(q+p^2)\right).
\]
For an $m$-bit input exponent, the size bound is
$O(m2^q(q+p^2))$.  In particular, $q=O(\log n)$ gives polynomial-size
circuits for every fixed power, uniformly in the magnitude of $T$.
The matrices may be singular and need not commute.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Once the initial controller $u$ is fixed, the controller trajectory and
all affine maps applied to $v$ are fixed.  Composition of affine maps is
affine, so for some hardwired matrix $M_T(u)$ and vector $d_T(u)$,
\[
 F^T(u,v)=\bigl(g^T(u),M_T(u)v+d_T(u)\bigr).
\]
For each of the $2^q$ controller values, evaluate its fixed affine map
using $O(p^2)$ gates and provide its fixed controller output.  Compute
each controller-equality flag once, and share it across the $q+p$ output
bits.  A scan selects the matching record using
$O(2^q(q+p))$ additional gates.  This proves the fixed-power bound,
including a charge for every table-selection gate.

For an input exponent, conditionally apply the circuits for $F^{2^j}$
to the current full state, as in \cref{sec:variable}.  Each stage updates
the controller as well as the payload and adds $O(q+p)$ selection gates.
Summing over $m$ stages gives the second bound.
\end{proof}

Autonomy is essential to this argument: if the controller update depends
on $v$, its future choices need not be determined by the initial $u$.
No efficient algorithm for obtaining $M_T(u)$ from a succinct transition
is asserted here.  The bound uses ordinary affine composition and finite
selection; it does not claim a new algebraic closure principle.

The same reasoning applies beyond affine maps.  Let $\mathcal M$ be a
family of maps on $p$-bit payloads containing the identity and closed under
composition, and suppose \emph{every} member has a circuit of size at
most $\kappa$.  If the autonomous controller selects maps from
$\mathcal M$, every fixed power costs
\[
 O\!\left(2^q(\kappa+q+p+1)\right),
\]
and an $m$-bit input time multiplies this by $m$.  Closure alone is insufficient: the uniform evaluation
bound on composite maps is a separate hypothesis.  For nonuniform circuit
existence, no efficient procedure for computing those composites is needed.

\subsection{Sparse modifications of a counter}\label{sec:sparse-counter}

The state-space parameter is not the only source of compression.  Identify
$\bits^n$ with $\mathbb Z/N\mathbb Z$, where $N=2^n$, and write
$R(x)=x+1\pmod N$.  A permutation obtained by changing a small number of
counter successors admits a bound linear in its sparse description, even
when it is a single cycle through all $N$ states.

\begin{theorem}[Sparse-counter powers]\label{thm:sparse-counter}
Let $n\geq1$, let $F$ be a permutation of $\mathbb Z/N\mathbb Z$, and let
$r=|\{x:F(x)\ne R(x)\}|$.  For every fixed $T\in\mathbb Z$,
\[
 \CC(F^T)=O\bigl(n(r+1)\bigr).
\]
For an unsigned $m$-bit time supplied as input,
\[
 \CC\bigl((x,t)\mapsto F^t(x)\bigr)=O\bigl(n(r+m+1)\bigr).
\]
Both constants are absolute.  The dependence on time-input length is additive.
\end{theorem}

\subsubsection{Commutation defects and displacement}

The fixed-power assertion follows from a more general statement.

\begin{proposition}[Sparse-counter centralizers]\label[proposition]{prop:centralizer}
Let $F$ be any map on $\mathbb Z/N\mathbb Z$ differing from $R$ at $r$
inputs.  Every permutation $H$ commuting with $F$ has a displacement
function $\delta_H(x)=H(x)-x\pmod N$ that changes on at most $2r$ counter
edges.  Consequently, $\CC(H)=O(n(r+1))$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Put $E=\{x:F(x)\ne R(x)\}$.  If $x\notin E$ and $H(x)\notin E$, then
\[
 H(R(x))=H(F(x))=F(H(x))=R(H(x)).
\]
Thus the set of commutation failures for $H$ and $R$ lies in
$E\cup H^{-1}(E)$ and has size at most $2r$.  Commutation at $x$ is
exactly $\delta_H(x+1)=\delta_H(x)$.  Cutting the ordinary linear order
$0,\ldots,N-1$ at these changes gives at most $2r+1$ constant intervals.
Compare the input with their endpoints, select a hardwired displacement,
and add it modulo $N$, at cost $O(n(r+1))$.

An alternative implementation, used in Lean, avoids ordering the changes.
Let $D=\{i:\delta_H(i+1)\ne\delta_H(i)\}$, with cyclic addition in the
arguments and ordinary integer comparisons below.  Telescoping gives
\[
 H(x)=x+\delta_H(0)+
       \sum_{\substack{i\in D\\i<x}}
         \bigl(\delta_H(i+1)-\delta_H(i)\bigr)\pmod N.
\]
Each summand uses one comparison, a conditional constant word, and a
linear-size adder.  The wraparound edge is never selected.  All gates,
including the shared carry chains, are charged.
\end{proof}

Every signed power of a permutation commutes with it, proving the first
part of \cref{thm:sparse-counter}.  The proposition only requires
injectivity of $H$ in its cardinality argument; it does not require $F$
to be a permutation.  Its set-containment argument works on arbitrary
universes, before finiteness or coordinates are imposed.

The geometry relating commutation failures to blocks in cycles is
established permutation theory.  Moreno and Rivera characterize these
blocks in their Theorem~3.8 and Corollary~3.10~\cite{MR14}.  Here we use
the elementary defect containment and account for the Boolean cost of the
resulting representation.  Historical priority of these precise circuit
bounds has not been determined.

\subsubsection{A chart constructed from the edited successors}

For the live-time bound, assume first $r>0$ and cut the counter cycle
immediately after each point of $E$.  There are $r$ nonempty circular
arcs.  Inside an arc $F$ increments; its only possible change is at the
last state.  Since $F$ and $R$ are permutations agreeing off $E$,
\[
 F(E)=R(E).
\]
Indeed, the images of the unedited inputs coincide, so their complements
coincide.  Therefore each edited edge leads to an arc start, and these
edges induce a permutation of the $r$ arcs.

Decompose that permutation into cycles and concatenate the arcs in each
cycle, retaining their individual lengths.  Assign to a state the triple
$(c,p,\ell)$ consisting of its cycle identifier, its position within that
cycle, and its total cycle length.  The coordinate map and its inverse
are piecewise translations on the original arcs and their concatenated
images.  At most one original arc crosses zero; splitting it there adds
one linear interval.  Comparisons, shared record selection, and addition
therefore implement each direction in $O(n(r+1))$ gates.  Identifiers and
positions use $n$ bits, while lengths use $n+1$ bits to allow $\ell=N$.
All intermediate arithmetic uses $O(n)$ bits.

In these coordinates one step preserves $c,\ell$ and increments $p$
modulo $\ell$.  For an $m$-bit input $t$, compute its remainder by scanning
bits from most significant to least significant.  With current remainder
$u<\ell$, the next remainder is $2u+b$ or $2u+b-\ell$, according as
$2u+b<\ell$ or not.  A comparison and subtraction cost $O(n)$ per bit;
one extra bit prevents overflow.  Add $p$, reduce once more, and decode.
The cost is $O(nr+nm+n)$.  If $r=0$, ordinary addition modulo $N$ gives
the same bound.  This proves the second part of \cref{thm:sparse-counter}.
For fixed signed $T$, hardwire $T\bmod\ell$ for each cycle instead.

\begin{corollary}[Labels and navigation for sparse counters]\label[corollary]{cor:sparse-labels}
For a permutation differing from the counter at $r$ states, the cycle
length, numerically least cycle vertex, and phase of an input state can be computed
with $O(n(r+1))$ gates.  The same bound suffices, on input $(x,y)$, for
reachability, the exact first hitting time, and a descriptor of all visits.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
Cut the ordinary linear order after each exception, obtaining at most
$r+1$ intervals.  Within each interval $[a,b)$, the trajectory follows
ordinary addition.  Hardwire its cycle representative $c$, cycle length
$\ell$, and phase $p_a$ at its first state.  An input $x$ in that interval
has label $(c,\ell,(p_a+x-a)\bmod\ell)$.  The interval length is at most
$\ell$, so one addition and conditional subtraction suffice for the phase.
Comparisons and record selection cost $O(n)$ per interval.

Compute the two labels.  Since every state is cyclic, $y$ is reachable
from $x$ exactly when $c(x)=c(y)$.  On a positive instance, the first hit is
\[
 d=(\phi(y)-\phi(x))\bmod\ell(x),
\]
and all visits occur at times $d+k\ell(x)$, $k\geq0$.  Both phases are
already below the common cycle length, so the difference needs only
comparison and subtraction: use $\phi(y)-\phi(x)$ when nonnegative, and
$\ell(x)-(\phi(x)-\phi(y))$ otherwise.  This costs $O(n)$ additional gates.
The descriptor also determines the exact bounded visit count; this is an
arithmetic interpretation of its output, with no deadline input charged.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[Polynomial-time construction from an edit list]
\label[proposition]{prop:sparse-compiler}
Given $n$ and an explicit list of the $r$ edited successors of the
$n$-bit counter, one can check permutation preservation and construct an
orbit chart of $O(r+1)$ records, each of $O(n)$ bits, in time polynomial
in $n$ and $r+1$.  For a valid list, the chart supplies the encoding and
decoding used above.  Given also a signed binary integer $T$, one can
construct a piecewise-translation table for $F^T$ with at most $2r+1$
linear intervals in time polynomial in $n$, $r+1$, and $\log(|T|+2)$.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
Compare the edited outputs with the freed counter outputs to check
$F(E)=R(E)$, after checking distinct input keys.  Sort the cuts, compute
arc lengths, follow the induced permutation of the arcs, and form prefix
sums on each of its cycles.  These operations construct the chart using
arithmetic on $O(n)$-bit integers.  The case $r=0$ is the ordinary counter
chart.  For a signed power, reduce $T$ modulo each chart cycle length.
Use the chart to evaluate $F^{-T}$ on $E$, and test the $O(r)$ candidate
change edges $E\cup F^{-T}(E)$ from \cref{prop:centralizer}.  Sorting their
successors and evaluating the displacement at the interval starts gives
the table; discard boundaries without a displacement change.
\end{proof}

The input here is the explicit edit list, whose entries specify where
counter edges have changed and their new successors.  Given only a circuit
for $F$, identifying that list is a separate task: the proposition asserts
no polynomial-time extraction of exceptions from a transition circuit.
The chart construction avoids enumeration of the $N=2^n$ states.
The explicit arithmetic and selection constructions above then compile
an $m$-bit iterator in time polynomial in $n,r+1,m$ with the gate bound
of \cref{thm:sparse-counter}.

The executable \texttt{sparse\_counter.py} implements this chart and interval
compiler.  Independent finite checks and their scope are recorded in
\texttt{REVIEW.md}; they do not prove the gate bound or compiler correctness.

\subsubsection{Large transitions and charged coordinate changes}

The improvement can apply to transitions with genuinely exponential
minimum circuit size.  Fix $r$ nonempty counter arcs and reconnect them
in any single cyclic order.  This gives $(r-1)!$ distinct permutations,
each differing from the counter at at most $r$ states and having one
orbit of length $N$.  For $r=2^{\lfloor\alpha n\rfloor}$ with fixed
$0<\alpha<1$, the logarithm of this family size is $\Theta(r\log r)$.
The circuit-description bound used in \cref{sec:lower} implies that some
members need $\Omega(r)$ gates, while all have $O(n(r+1))$-gate circuits.
Consequently there exist families with
\[
 \CC(F_n)=2^{\alpha n+O(\log n)},\qquad
 \sup_{T\in\mathbb Z}\CC(F_n^T)=2^{\alpha n+O(\log n)}.
\]
The lower bound is existential counting, not an explicit hard transition.
Using only their transition sizes in \cref{thm:main} would instead give
an upper bound with exponential scale $2^{(1+\alpha)n/2}$.

\begin{corollary}[Charged orbit coordinates]\label[corollary]{cor:coordinates}
Suppose a bijection $\phi:\bits^n\to\mathbb Z/N\mathbb Z$ satisfies
$\phi(G(x))=\phi(x)+1\pmod N$.  If a permutation $F$ differs from $G$
at $r$ states, then
\[
 \CC(F^T)\leq \CC(\phi)+\CC(\phi^{-1})+O(n(r+1))
 \quad(T\in\mathbb Z).
\]
For an $m$-bit input time, the last term is $O(n(r+m+1))$.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
Conjugate $F$ by $\phi$.  The exceptional-set cardinality is unchanged,
and the reference permutation becomes the counter.  Apply
\cref{thm:sparse-counter} and compose with the two coordinate circuits.
\end{proof}

Both coordinate costs are essential.  A long orbit does not itself
supply cheaply computable coordinates.  Conversely, these examples can
visit the entire state space: the compressed object is the arrangement
of arcs, without a reduction in the number of reachable states.

For the pair queries of \cref{cor:sparse-labels}, coordinate transport
needs only the forward map: encode both $x$ and $y$, then query the
conjugated permutation.  Reachability and visit times are preserved by
conjugacy, so the bound is $2\CC(\phi)+O(n(r+1))$.  No state is returned,
and no inverse-coordinate circuit is required.

A finite-field application, including the cost of discrete-logarithm
coordinates, is given in \cref{app:binary-field}.

\section{Distinguished points: small width and reversible circuits}
\label{sec:distinguished}

The batched lookup of \cref{lem:lookup} is needed because membership in the
packed checkpoint set of \cref{lem:checkpoints} has no cheap test: every
state of the initial trajectory must be looked up.  If membership in $H$
can be decided by a small circuit, only the \emph{first} checkpoint on the
trajectory needs a payload, and one linear scan over the dictionary
suffices.  Distinguished points, an idea attributed to Rivest in the
time--memory tradeoff literature~\cite{vOW99}, give such a set when they
are selected by a hash function of limited independence.  This section derives three
consequences: a polynomial-factor improvement of \cref{eq:optimized},
circuits of width $O(n+s)$, and reversible circuits with few ancillas.
The Lean development checks only the semantic correctness of the six-step
procedure in the proof of \cref{thm:distinguished}, for an arbitrary set
$H$ with the hitting property of \cref{lem:distinguished}
(\texttt{distinguishedPower\_correct}; see \cref{sec:lean}).  The hash
lemma, the gate counts and widths, and the reversible and pebbling results
of this section are not mechanized.

\subsection{Hashed checkpoints}

Identify $\bits^n$ with the field $\mathbb F_{2^n}$ through a fixed
irreducible polynomial of degree $n$ over $\mathbb F_2$.  Put $t=2n$.  For
coefficients $a=(a_0,\ldots,a_{t-1})\in\mathbb F_{2^n}^t$ and
$0\leq l\leq n$, let
\[
  g_a(x)=\sum_{j<t}a_jx^j,\qquad
  H_{a,l}=\{x:\text{the first $l$ coordinates of $g_a(x)$ vanish}\}.
\]

\begin{lemma}[Hashed checkpoint or repetition]\label[lemma]{lem:distinguished}
Let $n\geq3$, let $F$ be any function on $\bits^n$, and let $L=2^l$ with
$0\leq l\leq n-1$.  Put $K=16nL-1$.  There are coefficients $a$ such that
$H=H_{a,l}$ satisfies $|H|\leq 2N/L$ and, for every $x$, either two of
$x,F(x),\ldots,F^K(x)$ are equal or $F^i(x)\in H$ for some $i\leq K$.
Membership in $H_{a,l}$ has a circuit with $O(n^3)$ gates and width $O(n)$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Choose $a$ uniformly.  For distinct $x_1,\ldots,x_t$ the vector
$(g_a(x_1),\ldots,g_a(x_t))$ is uniform on $\mathbb F_{2^n}^t$, since the
Vandermonde matrix of distinct points is invertible; here $t\leq N$.
Hence the indicators $Y_x=[x\in H_{a,l}]$ are $t$-wise independent
Bernoulli variables with mean $1/L$.

\emph{Size.}  The indicators are in particular pairwise independent, so
$|H|=\sum_xY_x$ has mean $N/L$ and variance at most $N/L$.  By Chebyshev's
inequality, $\Pr[|H|>2N/L]\leq L/N\leq1/2$.

\emph{Hitting.}  Fix $x$ and suppose that $x_i=F^i(x)$, $0\leq i\leq K$,
are distinct.  The trajectory is determined by $F$ alone, before $a$ is
drawn.  Then $X=\sum_{i\leq K}Y_{x_i}$ is a sum of $K+1=16nL$
$t$-wise independent indicators with mean $\mu=16n$.  Bellare and Rompel's
tail bound for even $t\geq4$~\cite{BR94},
\[
  \Pr[|X-\mu|\geq\Delta]\leq
  8\left(\frac{t\mu+t^2}{\Delta^2}\right)^{t/2},
\]
applies with $\Delta=15n$, because $X=0$ forces $|X-\mu|=16n\geq\Delta$.
Here $(t\mu+t^2)/\Delta^2=(32n^2+4n^2)/(225n^2)=0.16$, so
$\Pr[X=0]\leq8\cdot0.16^{\,n}$.  If the first $K+1$ states are not
distinct there is nothing to prove.  A union bound over the $N$ starting
states therefore bounds the failure probability of the hitting condition
by $8\cdot0.32^{\,n}\leq0.27$ for $n\geq3$.  Since $0.27+1/2<1$, some
$a$ satisfies both conditions.

\emph{Membership.}  Evaluate $g_a(x)$ by Horner's rule: $t-1$ products of
two variable field elements and $t$ additions of hardwired constants.  A
product is a schoolbook polynomial product followed by reduction modulo the
fixed irreducible polynomial.  Both are $\mathbb F_2$-bilinear or linear
maps with $O(n^2)$ gates.  Producing one product coefficient at a time and
adding its fixed reduction vector into an $n$-bit accumulator keeps only
$O(n)$ wires live.  Finally test $l$ bits for zero.  The total is
$O(tn^2)=O(n^3)$ gates and width $O(n)$.
\end{proof}

The coefficients are found nonuniformly, as are all tables in this paper.
No attempt is made to reduce the $O(n^3)$ membership cost, for instance by
fast field multiplication.

\subsection{One lookup per input}

The \emph{width} of a circuit is the minimum, over topological orders of
its gates, of the largest number of inputs and gate values that have
already been produced and are still needed by a later gate or as an
output.  It is the number of registers of a straight-line program
evaluating the circuit.  Every circuit of size $s$ for $F$ has width at
most $s+n$.

\begin{theorem}[Distinguished-point fixed powers]\label{thm:distinguished}
Let $n\geq3$, let $F$ have a circuit with $s$ gates and width $w$, and put
$A=s+n^3$.  For every $T\geq0$ and every power of two $1\leq L\leq N/2$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:dp-tradeoff}
  \CC(F^T)=O\!\left(nLA+\frac{nN}{L}\right),
\end{equation}
by a circuit of width $O(n+w)$.  Consequently, every fixed power satisfies
\begin{equation}\label{eq:dp-optimized}
  \CC(F^T)=O\!\left(n\sqrt{NA}\right),
\end{equation}
again by circuits of width $O(n+w)\leq O(n+s)$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Take $H$, $K=16nL-1$, and $x_i=F^i(x)$ as in \cref{lem:distinguished}.  If
$T<K$, unroll $T$ copies of $F$, using $Ts\leq16nLs$ gates and width
$O(n+w)$.  Assume $T\geq K$ and hardwire the payloads $z_h=F^{T-K}(h)$ for
$h\in H$ and the residues $R(\lambda)=(T-K)\bmod\lambda$ for
$1\leq\lambda\leq K$.  Each phase below is a straight-line program on an
$O(n)$-bit configuration; stage indices are hardwired, so no counter is
needed.  All index fields have $O(n)$ bits, since $K<8nN$.
\begin{enumerate}
\item \emph{Scan.}  The configuration is $(u,\phi,d,c)$, initially
  $(x,0,0,0)$.  At stage $i=0,1,\ldots,K$: if $\phi=0$ and $u\in H$, set
  $\phi=1$, $d=i$ and $c=u$; then, if $i<K$, replace $u$ by $F(u)$.
  Finally $u=x_K$, and if $\phi=1$, then $d$ is the first index with
  $x_d\in H$ and $c=x_d$.  Each stage costs $s+O(n^3)=O(A)$ gates.
\item \emph{Lookup.}  Put $z=\bigvee_{h\in H}[c=h]\cdot z_h$, bitwise.
  For each key this is an $n$-bit equality test and $O(n)$ further
  gates, $O(n|H|)=O(nN/L)$ in total, in one pass over the keys.
\item \emph{Correction.}  Starting from $v=z$, at stage $i=0,\ldots,K-1$
  replace $v$ by $F(v)$ exactly when $i\geq d$.  This applies $F$ exactly
  $K-d$ times, so if $\phi=1$, then as in \cref{eq:identity},
  $v=F^{K-d}(F^{T-K}(x_d))=F^T(x)$.
\item \emph{Period.}  In a separate register, starting from $w=x_K$, at
  stage $j=1,\ldots,K$ replace $w$ by $F(w)$, and record $\lambda=j$ the
  first time $w=x_K$; use the dummy value $\lambda=1$ if this never
  happens.  The register $v$ from the correction step is left unchanged.
\item \emph{Residue and cycle walk.}  Read $r=R(\lambda)$ by a linear scan
  of the $K$ table entries, $O(nK)$ gates.  Starting from $v'=x_K$, at
  stage $i=0,\ldots,K-1$ replace $v'$ by $F(v')$ exactly when $i<r$.
\item \emph{Output.}  Return $v$ if $\phi=1$ and $v'$ otherwise.
\end{enumerate}
If $\phi=1$ the output is correct by step~3.  If $\phi=0$, then by
\cref{lem:distinguished} two of $x_0,\ldots,x_K$ coincide.  The orbit of
$x$ then has tail length $\mu$ and cycle length $\lambda^*$ with
$\mu+\lambda^*\leq K$.  Hence $x_K$ lies on the cycle, step~4 returns
$\lambda=\lambda^*$, and step~5 gives $v'=F^{K+r}(x)$ with
$K+r\equiv T\pmod{\lambda^*}$ and $K+r,T\geq\mu$, so $v'=F^T(x)$.  As in
\cref{sec:fixed}, both branches are evaluated on every input, with dummy
values where needed.  No division is performed.

The four walks cost $O(KA)=O(nLA)$ gates, the lookup $O(nN/L)$, and the
residue scan and selections $O(nK)=O(n^2L)$, which is absorbed.  This
proves \cref{eq:dp-tradeoff}.  Only $O(1)$ configurations, the
$O(n)$-bit Horner state of \cref{lem:distinguished}, and one evaluation of
$F$ are live at any time, which gives width $O(n+w)$.

For \cref{eq:dp-optimized}, if $A\leq N$ let $L$ be the largest power of
two with $L\leq\sqrt{N/A}$.  Then $L\leq\sqrt{N/n^3}\leq N/2$, so
$nLA\leq n\sqrt{NA}$ and $nN/L<2n\sqrt{NA}$.  If $A>N$, scan a complete
truth table of $F^T$: for each state $x'$ an $n$-bit equality test with
the input and $O(n)$ gates to OR in the stored value.  This uses
$O(nN)\leq O(n\sqrt{NA})$ gates and width $O(n)$.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Comparison with sorting-based lookup]\label[remark]{rem:dp-compare}
For $A\leq N$, \cref{eq:dp-optimized} improves \cref{eq:optimized} by a
factor of order $\sqrt n$, with the same $A=s+n^3$.  The saving comes from
replacing a sort of $\Theta(N/L+L)$ records, at $O(n^3)$ gates per record,
by one comparison per key at $O(n)$ gates; the price is a walk of length
$16nL$ in place of $2L$.  This is not the best known gate count.  An
$O(R\log R)$-comparator network~\cite{AKS83} in \cref{lem:lookup,lem:repeat}
reduces the sorting cost to $O(n^2R)$ and gives
$O(A_2+n\sqrt{NA_2})$ with $A_2=s+n^2$ (\cref{thm:aks}), which is smaller than
\cref{eq:dp-optimized} by up to a factor $\sqrt n$ when $s<n^3$.  The
$n^3$ term here is the cost of the hash, not of lookup.  The advantage of
distinguished points is structural: no sorting network is used, and the
width is $O(n+s)$.  The sorting network on $\Theta(N/L+L)$ records used
in \cref{sec:fixed} manipulates that many records together; we do not know how
to evaluate it in width much below $\sqrt N$ at the balanced choice of $L$.
Equivalently, \cref{thm:distinguished} describes a machine with $O(n+s)$
bits of read/write memory and a read-only data stream that it reads in one
left-to-right pass, performing $O(n\sqrt{NA})$ bit operations.  The
stream must repeat the data that the machine cannot retain: each of the
$O(K)$ membership tests needs the $2n$ hash coefficients, which occupy
$\Theta(n^2)$ bits, and each of the $O(K)$ evaluations of $F$ needs a gate
list of $O(s\log s)$ bits.  The stream therefore consists of one block of
coefficients and one copy of the gate list of $F$ in front of every stage
of the walks, followed at the appropriate point by the key--payload pairs
and the residue table.  Its length is
$O(nN/L+nL(n^2+s\log s))$, which is $O(n\sqrt{NA}\log s)$ at the balanced
choice of $L$.  If the machine may instead reread a fixed region of its
input, one copy of the coefficients and of the gate list suffices, and the
stream has length $O(nN/L+n^2+s\log s)$.  The circuit-width statement
needs neither variant, since its constants are hardwired.
\end{remark}

\subsection{Reversible circuits with few ancillas}

A reversible circuit uses NOT, CNOT and Toffoli gates on a fixed set of
wires.  A circuit \emph{computes} $G:\bits^n\to\bits^q$ with $a$ ancillas
if it maps $(x,y,0^a)$ to $(x,y\oplus G(x),0^a)$ for all $x$ and $y$.
Any Boolean circuit with $g$ gates gives such a circuit with $O(g+q)$
gates and $g$ ancillas: compute every gate into a fresh zero wire, copy
the outputs with CNOTs, and run the first part backwards.
A fan-in-two gate into a fresh wire needs $O(1)$ reversible gates, since
every Boolean function of two bits is a polynomial of degree at most two
over $\mathbb F_2$.  Applied to \cref{thm:distinguished} directly, this
gives as many ancillas as gates.  Bennett's pebbling of the four walks
removes almost all of them.

A walk with $K$ stages is a sequence of configurations $c_0,\ldots,c_K$
with $c_{i+1}=\mathrm{step}_i(c_i)$.  A \emph{move} XORs
$\mathrm{step}_i(c_i)$ into a register, which either creates $c_{i+1}$
from zeros or clears it.  Let $k\geq2$ and $m=\ceil{\log_kK}$, and pad the
walk to $k^m$ stages with identity steps.  To advance $k^j$ stages from a
held configuration, advance $k$ times by $k^{j-1}$ stages, and then undo
the first $k-1$ of these advances in reverse order.  If $M(j)$ moves
suffice for level $j$, then $M(j)=(2k-1)M(j-1)$ and $M(0)=1$, so
$M(m)=(2k-1)^m$.  At most $(k-1)m+1$ configurations besides $c_0$ are
held at once~\cite{Bennett89,LS90}.  Put
\[
  \varepsilon_k=\log_k(2k-1)-1=\log_k\!\left(2-\tfrac1k\right)
  \in\left(0,\tfrac1{\log k}\right].
\]
Since $k^{m-1}<K$,
\[
  (2k-1)^m=(k^m)^{1+\varepsilon_k}<(kK)^{1+\varepsilon_k}\leq k^2K^{1+\varepsilon_k}.
\]
For example, $\varepsilon_2=\log3-1\approx0.585$, $\varepsilon_4\approx0.404$
and $\varepsilon_{16}\approx0.238$.

\begin{theorem}[Reversible fixed powers]\label{thm:reversible}
Let $n\geq3$, $\CC(F)\leq s$, $A=s+n^3$, and let $k\geq2$ be an integer.
For every $T\geq0$ and every power of two $1\leq L\leq N/2$, a reversible
circuit computes $F^T$ with
\[
  O\!\left(\frac{nN}{L}+k^2(nL)^{1+\varepsilon_k}A\right)
  \text{ gates and }
  O\!\left(kn\ceil{\frac{n}{\log k}}+s\right)\text{ ancillas.}
\]
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Use the phases of \cref{thm:distinguished}.  Each of the four walks has
at most $K+1=16nL$ stages on configurations of $b=O(n)$ bits.

\emph{One move.}  For the scan, a move XORs $\mathrm{step}_i(c)$ into a
register $c'$.  Compute the membership bit of $u$ by Horner's rule,
keeping all $t=2n$ intermediate field elements; each product is formed
by $n^2$ Toffoli gates into a $(2n-1)$-bit coefficient register, reduced
by $O(n^2)$ CNOTs, after which the coefficient register is cleared by the
same Toffoli gates.  This costs $O(n^3)$ gates and $O(n^2)$ ancillas, all
cleared after the bit is copied.  Then XOR $F(u)$ into the $u$-field of
$c'$, with $O(s)$ gates and $s$ ancillas, and XOR the $O(n)$-gate updates
of $\phi$, $d$ and $c$ into $c'$.  Finally clear the membership bit.  A
move therefore costs $O(A)$ gates with $O(s+n^2)$ reusable ancillas.
Moves of the other walks cost $O(s+n)$ gates and $O(s+n)$ ancillas; a
conditional application of $F$ is computed into a temporary register,
selected bitwise into $c'$, and then uncomputed.

\emph{Walks.}  By the pebbling count, each walk uses
$(2k-1)^m\leq k^2(16nL)^{1+\varepsilon_k}$ moves, hence
$O(k^2(nL)^{1+\varepsilon_k}A)$ gates, and at most $(k-1)m+2$
configuration registers.  Since $K<8nN$,
$m=\ceil{\log_kK}=O(\ceil{n/\log k})$, so the registers use
$O(kn\ceil{n/\log k})$ wires.  Afterwards only $c_0$ and $c_K$ are held.

\emph{Assembly.}  Run the scan, keeping its final configuration.  For the
lookup, for each key $h$ compute $[c=h]$ into one ancilla with $O(n)$
gates and $O(n)$ ancillas, CNOT it into the positions of the one bits of
$z_h$ in a fresh register, and clear it.  Copy the needed fields with
CNOTs and run the correction and period walks.  Read the residue by the
same comparison scan over the $K$ table entries, using $O(nK)$ gates, and
run the cycle walk.  For each output bit, two Toffoli gates XOR the bit
selected by $\phi$ into $y$.  Finally run everything except the output
step backwards, which clears every ancilla.  If $T<K$, pebble the walk
of $T$ applications of $F$ instead.

The lookups cost $O(nN/L)$ gates.  The residue scans and copies cost
$O(nK)=O(n^2L)$, which is absorbed by the walks because $A\geq n^3$.
Besides the pebbling registers, $O(1)$ configurations and $O(s+n^2)$
workspace ancillas are used.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Ancilla--gate tradeoffs]\label[corollary]{cor:reversible}
Under the assumptions of \cref{thm:reversible}, write $\varepsilon=\varepsilon_k$
and assume $A\leq N/(k^2n)$.  A reversible circuit computes every fixed
power $F^T$ with
\[
  O\!\left(k\,n^{1+\varepsilon/2}A^{1/2}N^{(1+\varepsilon)/(2+\varepsilon)}\right)
\]
gates and $O(kn\ceil{n/\log k}+s)$ ancillas.  In particular:
\begin{enumerate}
\item for $k=2$, with $O(n^2+s)$ ancillas, the gate count is
  $N^{\log3/(1+\log3)}\poly(n)A^{1/2}$, where
  $\log3/(1+\log3)\approx0.6131$;
\item for fixed $k$, the exponent of $N$ is
  $\tfrac12+\varepsilon_k/(2(2+\varepsilon_k))<\tfrac12+1/(4\log k)$, with
  $O_k(n^2+s)$ ancillas;
\item for $k=n$, with $O(n^3/\log n+s)$ ancillas, the gate count is
  $N^{1/2}\,2^{n/(4\log n)}\poly(n)A^{1/2}=N^{1/2+O(1/\log n)}A^{1/2}$.
\end{enumerate}
A reversible circuit with $O(nN)$ gates and $O(n)$ ancillas computes
$F^T$ for every $F$, which covers larger $A$.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
Let $L$ be the largest power of two with
$L\leq\Lambda:=(N/(k^2n^{\varepsilon}A))^{1/(2+\varepsilon)}$.  The
assumption on $A$ gives $\Lambda\geq1$, and $\Lambda\leq\sqrt N\leq N/2$.
At $L=\Lambda$ the two terms of \cref{thm:reversible} agree up to a
constant, and halving $L$ changes them by constant factors.  So the gate
count is
\[
  O\!\left(\frac{nN}{\Lambda}\right)
  =O\!\left(nN^{(1+\varepsilon)/(2+\varepsilon)}
     (k^2n^\varepsilon A)^{1/(2+\varepsilon)}\right),
\]
which is at most the displayed bound because $k^2n^{\varepsilon}A\geq1$
and $1/(2+\varepsilon)\leq1/2$.  The exponent of $N$ equals
$\tfrac12+\varepsilon/(2(2+\varepsilon))$, and
$\varepsilon_k\leq1/\log k$.  For $k=2$,
$(1+\varepsilon_2)/(2+\varepsilon_2)=\log3/(1+\log3)$.  For $k=n$,
$N^{\varepsilon/(2(2+\varepsilon))}\leq N^{\varepsilon/4}\leq2^{n/(4\log n)}$
and $kn\ceil{n/\log k}=O(n^3/\log n)$.  For the last claim, scan the
truth table of $F^T$: for each state $x'$, compute $[x=x']$ into an
ancilla with $O(n)$ gates and $O(n)$ ancillas, CNOT it into the one bits
of $F^T(x')$ in $y$, and clear it.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[In-place permutations]\label[corollary]{cor:in-place}
Let $P$ be a permutation of $\bits^n$ with $\CC(P)\leq s$.  For each
integer $k\geq2$ satisfying the hypothesis of \cref{cor:reversible},
there is a reversible circuit mapping $(x,0^{n+a})$ to $(P(x),0^{n+a})$,
after a fixed relabelling of wires, with $a=O(kn\ceil{n/\log k}+s)$ and
