the gate count of \cref{cor:reversible} plus $O(s+n)$.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
First XOR $P(x)$ into a zero register with $O(s+n)$ gates and $s$
temporary ancillas.  Then apply the circuit of \cref{cor:reversible} for
$P^{-1}=P^{\ord(P)-1}$ from the new register into the input register.  It
XORs $P^{-1}(P(x))=x$ into $x$ and leaves zero there.  Relabel the two
$n$-wire registers.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Lower bounds and the pebbling overhead]\label[remark]{rem:pebbling}
A reversible circuit with $g$ gates yields a Boolean circuit with $O(g)$
gates, whatever the number of ancillas: each reversible gate changes one
wire by a fan-in-at-most-three function.  So \cref{thm:lower} applies
to reversible circuits with any number of ancillas, and the exponent
$1/2$ cannot be lowered for them either.  Whether $N^{1/2}\poly(n,s)$
gates are possible with $\poly(n,s)$ ancillas is open.  The overhead
$N^{\Theta(\varepsilon_k)}$ comes only from pebbling the four walks.
Bennett's pebble game on a path, which models any construction storing
only whole configurations of a walk, was analyzed exactly by
Knill~\cite{Knill95}, and Li, Tromp, and Vit\'anyi~\cite{LTV98} show that
Bennett's strategy reaches the greatest distance for a given number of
pebbles.  Such results limit this strategy class only.  They give no
circuit lower bound, since a reversible circuit need not simulate a walk
configuration by configuration.
\end{remark}


\section{Applications}\label{sec:applications}

This section connects the circuit bounds to exact program summaries,
bounded choices, preprocessing, universal maps, and asymmetric iterators.
The target and navigation circuits used below are mechanized, as are the
simulation and hardwiring arguments of \cref{sec:universal-map,sec:asymmetric}.
The local certificate characterization, SAT-oracle analyses, SETH reduction,
and preprocessing runtime are prose results.

\subsection{Exact program summaries and local certificates}
\label{sec:program-analysis}

Let $P:\bits^n\to\bits$ be a target predicate of size $a$, put
$N=2^n$, $A_P=s+a+n^3$, and $B_P=A_P+\sqrt{n^3NA_P}$.  Define the
\emph{target distance}
\[
 \tau_P(x)=
 \begin{cases}
  \min\{t\geq0:P(F^t(x))=1\},&\text{if the target is reached},\\
  N,&\text{otherwise}.
 \end{cases}
\]
Every finite first-hit time is less than $N$, so the sentinel $N$ means
\emph{never} without ambiguity; the output uses $n+1$ bits.

\begin{corollary}[Exact target-distance summaries]
\label[corollary]{cor:distance-summary}
The function $\tau_P$ has a circuit of size $O(B_P)$.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
Use the existence flag and first-hit-time circuit of \cref{cor:target},
and select $N$ on negative instances with $O(n)$ additional gates.
The time on positive instances is the transient depth of the absorbing
map $G(x)=x$ when $P(x)=1$ and $G(x)=F(x)$ otherwise.
No clock is added to the dynamical state.
\end{proof}

There is also a proof using only \cref{thm:main}, at the cost of a factor
$n$.  Compile $G^{2^i}$ for $0\leq i<n$.  For a nontarget input, start
with $z=x$, $r=0$, and consider these powers in decreasing order.
Accept a jump when its endpoint is still outside $P$, updating $z$ and
adding $2^i$ to $r$.  Absorption makes target membership monotone along
the orbit.  The accepted jumps find the largest $r<N$ still outside
the target, so $r+1$ is the first-hit time or $N$ if no target is reached.
Target inputs return zero.  The time accumulator belongs to the query
circuit, and does not enlarge the state width to which powering is applied.

\begin{proposition}[Local characterization]
\label[proposition]{prop:distance-certificate}
For a function $R:\bits^n\to\{0,\ldots,N\}$, one has $R=\tau_P$ if and
only if, for every $x$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:distance-certificate}
 R(x)=
 \begin{cases}
  0,&P(x)=1,\\
  \operatorname{inc}_N(R(F(x))),&P(x)=0,
 \end{cases}
 \qquad \operatorname{inc}_N(u)=\min(N,u+1).
\end{equation}
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
The target distance satisfies the equation.  Conversely, if the first
hit from $x$ is at time $d<N$, the equation forces zero at that target
and then $1,\ldots,d$ backwards along the path.  If the target is never
reached, the orbit enters a target-free cycle.  Going around a cycle of
length $k>0$ forces $u=\min(N,u+k)$, hence $u=N$.  The same value
propagates backwards to $x$.  These cases determine $R$ everywhere.
\end{proof}

For a proposed circuit $R$ with $q$ gates and $n+1$ output bits, a violation circuit
checks the range $R(x)\leq N$ and \cref{eq:distance-certificate} using
$O(q+s+a+n)$ gates.  Its unsatisfiability certifies the entire
summary.  This verification problem is coNP-complete in the length of
the supplied circuit descriptions: a violating state is a witness for
failure, and with $F$ the identity and $R\equiv0$, correctness is
equivalent to $P$ being a tautology.  Thus this is a small semantic
certificate with a coNP correctness check.  It does not bound the length
of a proof of the UNSAT obligation in a conventional proof system.
Guessing a summary, checking it with a SAT oracle, and testing its value
at the queried starting state gives certificates for either reachability
answer, with description length
$O(B_P\log(B_P+2))$ and a verification circuit of size $O(B_P)$.

The rank is necessary for this exact characterization.  The Boolean
equation $E(x)=P(x)\lor E(F(x))$ also admits the spurious value one on a
target-free cycle; a finite decreasing distance cannot do so.
In particular,
\[
 I_{\mathrm{safe}}(x)=[\tau_P(x)=N]
\]
is the largest inductive invariant avoiding $P$.  Every safe initial
set lies inside it.  On states with $0<\tau_P(x)<N$, the equation gives
$\tau_P(F(x))=\tau_P(x)-1$, an exact termination rank when $P$ denotes
halting.

Once $R=\tau_P$ is certified, evaluating it gives the answer and exact
time for any single starting state.  For an initial-state predicate $A$:
\begin{itemize}
\item safety is the unsatisfiability of $A(x)\land[R(x)<N]$;
\item with $P$ a halting predicate, nontermination from some allowed
  state is the satisfiability of $A(x)\land[R(x)=N]$;
\item if $A$ is nonempty and all its states terminate, binary search
  with queries $\exists x:\ A(x)\land[R(x)\geq k]$ finds the exact
  worst-case first-halting time in $O(n)$ SAT calls.
\end{itemize}
An empty initial set is detected by a separate satisfiability query.
The use of compact state predicates follows symbolic model checking
\cite{BCMDH90}; here the representation-size guarantee is for general
Boolean circuits.  Existence, discovery, and validation remain different
costs.  Feldman, Immerman, Sagiv, and Shoham \cite{FISS20} give exponential
query lower bounds for invariant inference even with short invariants;
their Hoare-query model is a specific restriction on access to the system.

\paragraph{One certificate for all state pairs.}
Let $D_F(x,y)$ be the least $t$ with $F^t(x)=y$, or $N$ if no such $t$
exists.  By \cref{thm:reachability}, a flag-to-sentinel conversion gives
$\CC(D_F)=O(B)$.  For each fixed $y$, apply
\cref{prop:distance-certificate} with the target predicate $[x=y]$.
Consequently $D_F$ is the unique function into $\{0,\ldots,N\}$ satisfying
\begin{equation}\label{eq:pair-distance-certificate}
 D_F(x,y)=
 \begin{cases}
  0,&x=y,\\
  \operatorname{inc}_N(D_F(F(x),y)),&x\ne y.
 \end{cases}
\end{equation}
For a candidate of $q$ gates, one UNSAT check over $x,y$ certifies all
pairwise first-hit times using $O(q+s+n)$ gates.  The circuit can then be reused with arbitrary
initial and target predicates through
$\exists x,y:\ A(x)\land[D_F(x,y)<N]\land P(y)$.
The two query inputs do not double the dynamical state width.
The existing lower bounds for arbitrary fixed powers do not establish
matching lower bounds for these distance or invariant circuits.

\subsection{A bounded number of choices}\label{sec:bounded-choice}

Let a deterministic computation have its configurations encoded in $b$
bits, as in \cref{cor:halting}, and suppose that some configurations are
\emph{choice configurations} with two successors $\sigma_0(c)$ and
$\sigma_1(c)$, while every other configuration $c$ has the single successor
$\sigma(c)$; halting configurations are absorbing.  Suppose that a circuit
of size $s$ decides whether $c$ is a choice configuration and computes
$\sigma_0(c)$, $\sigma_1(c)$ and $\sigma(c)$, and that along every path from
an initial configuration $\iota(x)$, $x\in\bits^n$, at most $g$ choice
configurations are visited.  Let $\mathrm{Conf}\subseteq\bits^b$ be a set
containing every configuration on such a path.  In an alternating
computation each choice configuration is labelled existential or
universal by the same circuit.  Acceptance is the least fixed point:
a halting configuration accepts according to its state, an existential
(universal) choice configuration accepts when some (every) successor
accepts, a nonchoice configuration accepts when its successor does, and
a configuration accepts only if this is forced after finitely many
steps.  A nondeterministic computation is the special case in which
every choice is existential.

\begin{proposition}[Bounded alternation]\label[proposition]{prop:bounded-choice}
In this setting put $b'=b+g+\ceil{\log_2(g+2)}$, $M=(g+1)|\mathrm{Conf}|$
and $A'=s+O(g\log(g+2))+b'^3$.  Acceptance on $\bits^n$ has a circuit of
size
\[
  2^{g+1}\cdot O\!\left(A'+\sqrt{b'^3MA'}\right)+O(2^g).
\]
For an in-place machine with $h$ heads, tape alphabet $\Gamma$, state set
$Q$ and $|\mathrm{Conf}|\leq|\Gamma|^n n^h|Q|$, and with $h$, $Q$ and
$\Gamma$ fixed, this is $2^g|\Gamma|^{n/2}\poly(n,g)$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
For a choice word $y\in\bits^g$ let $F_y$ act on pairs $(c,i)$, where
$0\leq i\leq g$ counts the choices made so far:
\[
  F_y(c,i)=
  \begin{cases}
    (\sigma_{y_{i+1}}(c),\,i+1), & c\text{ a choice configuration and }i<g,\\
    (\sigma(c),\,i), & c\text{ a nonchoice configuration},\\
    (c,i), & \text{otherwise}.
  \end{cases}
\]
With $y$ supplied as $g$ further state bits that never change, these maps
form one map on $b'$ bits whose circuit has $s+O(g\log(g+2))$ gates: the
bit $y_{i+1}$ is selected by a multiplexer and the counter is incremented
conditionally.  The deterministic trajectory of $(\iota(x),0)$ under
$F_y$ is the computation path that resolves its $j$th choice by $y_j$.
Fix $y$.  The set of pairs reachable from $\{(\iota(x),0):x\in\bits^n\}$ is
forward invariant under $F_y$ and has at most $M$ elements.  A trajectory
in a set of $M$ states that has not reached an absorbing state after $M$
steps never does, so $F_y^M(\iota(x),0)$ is a halting configuration if and
only if the path determined by $y$ halts, and it is then the final
configuration.  By \cref{thm:invariant}, with the bits of $y$ hardwired and
$L$ optimized as in \cref{eq:L-choice} with $N$ replaced by $M$, the bit
\[
  v_y(x)=[\,F_y^M(\iota(x),0)\text{ is an accepting halting configuration}\,]
\]
has a circuit of size $O(A'+\sqrt{b'^3MA'})$.

For a prefix $u\in\bits^j$ with $j<g$, let $F^{(u)}$ be $F_{u0^{g-j}}$
modified to fix every pair $(c,j)$ in which $c$ is a choice configuration.
The trajectory of $(\iota(x),0)$ under $F^{(u)}$ follows the path with
choices $u$ until it reaches its $(j+1)$st choice configuration, and stops
there.  The same argument gives a circuit of the same size for the bit
$e_u(x)$ that says whether $F^{(u)\,M}(\iota(x),0)$ is a universal choice
configuration with counter $j$.

Now evaluate the choice tree.  Put $\mathrm{val}(y)=v_y(x)$ for
$y\in\bits^g$, and for $|u|<g$ let $\mathrm{val}(u)$ be the AND of
$\mathrm{val}(u0)$ and $\mathrm{val}(u1)$ if $e_u(x)=1$ and their OR
otherwise.  This uses $O(2^g)$ further gates.  We claim that
$\mathrm{val}(\varepsilon)$ is the acceptance bit.  By induction on $g-|u|$,
$\mathrm{val}(u)$ is the value of the configuration at which the path with
choices $u$ makes its $(|u|+1)$st choice, if it makes one.  If it makes
none, every $y$ extending $u$ determines the same path, so all leaves below
$u$ equal its acceptance bit and so does $\mathrm{val}(u)$, whatever $e_u$
is.  A path that never halts determines the value $0$, as the least fixed
point requires, because only finitely many choices occur on it.  The
circuits $v_y$ and $e_u$ number $2^g+2^g-1$, which gives the bound.  For
the in-place case, $b=n\ceil{\log_2|\Gamma|}+h\ceil{\log_2n}+O(1)$ and
a step circuit decodes each head position and updates the cells under
the heads with $O(hn)$ gates, so $s=O(hn)$, $b'$ and $A'$ are $\poly(n,g)$ and
$\sqrt M\leq\sqrt{g+1}\,|\Gamma|^{n/2}n^{h/2}|Q|^{1/2}$.
\end{proof}

The factor $2^g$ is the price of enumerating the choice words; the
dictionaries of different words $y$ cannot be shared, because their
invariant sets are disjoint.  For a binary alphabet the bound
$2^{n/2+g}\poly(n)$ is below the truth-table bound whenever
$g\leq(1/2-\varepsilon)n$ for a fixed $\varepsilon>0$ and $n$ is large.  Unbounded nondeterminism lies outside the method.
When $g$ is not bounded, the configuration graph has out-degree two and a
computation is a tree rather than a trajectory.  The single-trajectory
arguments in \cref{lem:checkpoints} and the correction identity
\cref{eq:identity} do not directly apply.  Whether nondeterministic in-place machines admit circuits of
size $|\Gamma|^{n/2}\poly(n)$ is open.

\subsection{The cost of writing down the circuit}\label{sec:preprocessing}

The circuits of \cref{thm:main} are nonuniform: the checkpoint payloads
depend on all of $F$ and on $T$.  When $F$ is given by a circuit, they can
nevertheless be computed in time close to linear in $N$.  We use a
checkpoint set that is easier to compute than the path packing of
\cref{lem:checkpoints}.

\begin{lemma}[Depth-residue checkpoints]\label[lemma]{lem:residue-checkpoints}
Let $F$ be a map on $N$ states and $1\leq L\leq N$.  There is a set $H$ of
at most $2N/L$ states such that, for every $x$, the list
$x,F(x),\ldots,F^{2L}(x)$ contains an element of $H$ or a repeated state.
Given the table of $F$, such a set is computed in $O(N)$ operations.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Among the $L$ residue classes of the depths of noncycle vertices modulo
$L$, choose one of minimum size and put its vertices into $H$; this adds at
most $N_{\mathrm{tree}}/L$ vertices.  On every cycle of length
$\ell\geq L$, number the vertices in the forward direction and put those
with indices $0,L,2L,\ldots$ below $\ell$ into $H$, at most
$\ceil{\ell/L}\leq2\ell/L$ per cycle.  Leave cycles shorter than $L$
unmarked.  Then $|H|\leq2N/L$.  A trajectory starting at depth at least
$L$ meets every depth residue among its first $L$ states, hence meets $H$.
Otherwise it reaches its cycle in fewer than $L$ steps, then either meets
a mark within $L$ further steps or, on a short cycle, repeats a state
within $L$ further steps.  The cycles, the depths and the class sizes are
found by one traversal of the functional graph and one breadth-first
search from the cycles along reversed edges.
\end{proof}

Replacing \cref{lem:checkpoints} by this lemma in \cref{sec:fixed} changes
only the constant in the size of the dictionary, so it gives the bound of
\cref{thm:main} with the same $K=2L$.

\begin{proposition}[Preprocessing time]\label[proposition]{prop:preprocessing}
On a random-access machine with words of $\Theta(n)$ bits, given a circuit of
size $s$ for $F$ and the binary expansion of $T$, a circuit for $F^T$ of the
size in \cref{eq:optimized} is computed with
\[
  O\!\left(Ns+N+G+\sqrt N\ceil{\log_2(T+2)/n}\right)
\]
word operations, where $G=O(A+\sqrt{n^3NA})$ is the size of the output.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
If $T<K$ the circuit is $T$ copies of $F$.  Otherwise:
\begin{enumerate}
\item Evaluate $F$ on all $N$ states, $O(Ns)$ operations, and compute $H$ by
  \cref{lem:residue-checkpoints}, $O(N)$.
\item The payloads are $z_h=F^j(h)$ with the common exponent $j=T-K$.  Run a
  depth-first search on the reversed forest from each cycle vertex,
  keeping the current root-to-vertex path on a stack.  At a vertex $h$ of
  depth $d_h>j$ the payload is the stack entry $j$ positions below the
  top.  At a vertex of depth $d_h\leq j$ attached to the cycle vertex $c$
  of a cycle of length $\ell$, stored as an array, the payload is the cycle
  vertex at position
  $\mathrm{pos}(c)+\bigl((T\bmod\ell)-K-d_h\bigr)\bmod\ell$.  Cycle
  vertices are handled by the same formula with $d_h=0$.  This takes
  $O(N)$ operations once $T\bmod\ell$ is known for every cycle length.
\item The distinct cycle lengths sum to at most $N$, so there are at most
  $\sqrt{2N}$ of them.  The residue table \cref{eq:residue-table} needs
  $T\bmod p$ for $p\leq K\leq2\ceil{\sqrt N}$.  Each reduction of $T$
  modulo an $O(n)$-bit integer takes $O(\ceil{\log_2(T+2)/n})$ word
  operations by schoolbook division, so all residues take
  $O(\sqrt N\ceil{\log_2(T+2)/n})$ operations.
\item Emit the trajectory circuits, the Batcher network of
  \cref{lem:lookup} with the hardwired dictionary, the repetition and
  arithmetic circuits and the residue table, in time linear in their
  size.  Batcher's network is generated recursively.\qedhere
\end{enumerate}
\end{proof}

In bit operations the bound is multiplied by $\poly(n)$.  This refines the
polynomial bound in $N$, $s$ and $\log T$ recorded in the review notes
accompanying the repository.  In particular, if a uniform family $F_n$ has
circuits of size $2^{O(n)}$ computable in time $2^{O(n)}$, and $T_n$ is
computable in time $2^{O(n)}$, then the circuits for $F_n^{T_n}$ of size
$2^{n/2}\poly(n,s)$ are computable in time $2^{O(n)}$, that is, they form an
$\mathsf{E}$-uniform family.  The same holds for the final-configuration
circuits of \cref{cor:halting}, where $T=2^b$.  Only the circuit for the
first halting time, which uses \cref{thm:depth}, has not been analysed
here.

\begin{proposition}[A SETH obstruction to uniform analysis]
\label[proposition]{prop:seth-analysis}
Fix $0<\varepsilon<1$.  Unless the Strong Exponential Time Hypothesis
(SETH) fails, no deterministic uniform algorithm solves general
$n$-bit iteration in time
\[
  2^{(1-\varepsilon)n}\poly(s,n,\log(T+2)),
\]
or general target reachability in time
$2^{(1-\varepsilon)n}\poly(s,n,a)$.
The same obstruction applies to an algorithm that constructs an exact
Boolean circuit for the requested power, reachability summary, or exact
target-distance summary within those respective time bounds.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
Given an $m$-variable CNF formula $\varphi$, form the $(m+1)$-bit map
\[
 F_\varphi(u,b)=
 \bigl(u+1\bmod2^m,\ b\lor\varphi(u)\bigr).
\]
Its circuit has size $O(|\varphi|+m)$.  Starting at $(0^m,0)$, the
assignment register visits every assignment during the next $2^m$ steps,
and the last bit is then $\operatorname{SAT}(\varphi)$.
Equivalently, the target $b=1$ is reachable exactly when $\varphi$ is
satisfiable.  The hypothesized algorithm, with $n=m+1$ and
$T=2^m$, therefore decides CNF-SAT in
$2^{(1-\varepsilon)m}\poly(|\varphi|,m)$ time, contrary to SETH
\cite{CDLMPSSW16}.  A compiler has the same consequence: its explicit
circuit output can be evaluated with only polynomial overhead in $n$
per gate, which preserves the stated exponent on these instances.
For a target-distance summary, compare its value with the never sentinel.
\end{proof}

For this example the entire powered map is already tiny:
\[
 F_\varphi^{2^m}(u,b)=
 \bigl(u,\ b\lor\operatorname{SAT}(\varphi)\bigr).
\]
It is either the identity or the map that sets the last bit to one.
Determining which summary is correct decides SAT.  In particular, a
general $2^{n/2}\poly(s,n)$-time compiler would give a
$2^{m/2}\poly(|\varphi|,m)$-time SAT algorithm.  This is an obstruction
for algorithms inspecting the transition circuit, distinct from the
oracle-model restrictions in \cref{sec:blackbox}.  It already uses an
exponent with $O(n)$ bits and is compatible with the near-full-state-space
preprocessing bound of \cref{prop:preprocessing}.

\subsection{A universal linear-size map}\label{sec:universal-map}

Hardness of a fixed power of some linear-size map transfers to a single
explicit map with a logarithmic loss in the state width.

Fix $n$ and a constant $c\geq1$, and let $G=(c+1)n$.  Normalize every
circuit of size at most $cn$ for a map on $n$ bits to exactly $G$ gates in
topological order whose last $n$ gates are the outputs, by inserting dummy gates and then appending $n$ gates that copy the
outputs.  Such a circuit is described by
a list $D$ of $G$ entries, each an operation and two operand indices in
$[0,n+G)$, so $|D|=O(n\log n)$.  Let $U=U_{n,c}$ act on states
$(D,x,w,\mathit{pc})$ with $x\in\bits^n$, $w\in\bits^G$ and a counter
$0\leq\mathit{pc}\leq G$, of total width $m=O(n\log n)$:
\begin{itemize}
\item if $\mathit{pc}<G$, set $w_{\mathit{pc}}$ to the operation of entry
  $\mathit{pc}$ of $D$ applied to its operands, where index $i$ refers to
  $x_i$ for $i<n$ and to $w_{i-n}$ otherwise, and increment $\mathit{pc}$;
\item if $\mathit{pc}=G$, copy the last $n$ bits of $w$ into $x$, clear
  $w$ and reset $\mathit{pc}$ to $0$.
\end{itemize}
Other values of $\mathit{pc}$ are treated as $G$, and $D$ never changes.

\begin{proposition}[Universal map]\label[proposition]{prop:universal}
$U_{n,c}$ is computable in time $\poly(n)$ from $(n,c)$ and has
$\CC(U_{n,c})=O(n\log n)=O(m)$.  Put $\tau=G+1$.  For every
$F:\bits^n\to\bits^n$ with $\CC(F)\leq cn$ and every $T\geq0$,
\[
  \CC(F^T)\leq\CC(U_{n,c}^{\tau T}).
\]
Consequently, if some maps $F_n$ with $\CC(F_n)\leq cn$ have
$\CC(F_n^{T_n})\geq2^{\varepsilon n}$, then
$\CC(U_{n,c}^{\tau T_n})\geq2^{\Omega(m/\log m)}$, where the exponent
$\tau T_n$ has $O(\log T_n+\log n)$ bits.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
One step selects entry $\mathit{pc}$ of $D$ with a multiplexer over $G$
entries of $O(\log n)$ bits, $O(n\log n)$ gates; selects the two operands
from the $n+G$ bits of $x$ and $w$, $O(n)$ gates; and writes the result
through a decoder of $\mathit{pc}$, $O(n)$ gates.  The reset step and the
selection between the two cases cost $O(n)$ gates.  For the description
$D_F$ of $F$, gate $\mathit{pc}$ reads only $x$ and gates with smaller
indices, which have been written since the last reset, so
$U^{\tau}(D_F,x,0,0)=(D_F,F(x),0,0)$ and by induction
$U^{\tau T}(D_F,x,0,0)=(D_F,F^T(x),0,0)$.  Hardwiring $D_F$, $w=0$ and
$\mathit{pc}=0$ in a circuit for $U^{\tau T}$ and projecting onto $x$
gives a circuit for $F^T$ of no larger size, since constants and
projections are free.  The final claim follows from
$n=\Omega(m/\log m)$.
\end{proof}

Lean checks the simulation and hardwiring steps of this proof in
\texttt{UniversalMap.lean}.  The theorem \texttt{iterate\_simulation} derives
\[
 U^{\tau T}\circ\mathrm{enc}=\mathrm{enc}\circ F^T
\]
from the one-step identity.  The theorem \texttt{simulation\_circuit} turns a circuit for $U^{\tau T}$,
correct on encoded states, into one for $F^T$ when the encoding places the
input bits among constant bits; in the native basis this costs two gates
for the constants.  \texttt{Universal.step\_round} defines the step map on
abstract states $(D,x,w,\mathit{pc})$ over any CSLib signature and proves
$U^{G+1}(D_c,x,0,0)=(D_c,c(x),0,0)$ for every CSLib circuit $c$ with at most
$G$ gates, where $D_c$ lists the gates of $c$ followed by padding.  In this
version the reset step reads the output wires through indices stored in
$D$, which is equivalent to the appended copy gates above.
\texttt{Universal.universal\_power\_circuit} combines these steps:
under any bit encoding of the states that writes $x$ directly and fixes
the remaining bits of a start state to constants determined by $D_c$, a
circuit for $U^{(G+1)T}$ gives one for $c^T$ with at most two more gates.
The gate count $\CC(U_{n,c})=O(n\log n)$, the bit encoding of $D$, $w$ and
$\mathit{pc}$, the uniformity claim and the asymptotic consequence are not
mechanized.

\begin{remark}[One explicit candidate]\label[remark]{rem:universal-product}
Let $W=U_{n,c}\times P_{2k}$ act on $m+2k$ bits by
$(u,z)\mapsto(U_{n,c}(u),P_{2k}(z))$.  Every power satisfies
$\CC(W^T)\geq\max\{\CC(U_{n,c}^T),\CC(P_{2k}^T)\}$, since each factor is a
projection of $W^T$ with the other input fixed.  So this single explicit
linear-size map has a power of complexity $\Omega(2^k/k)$ by
\cref{thm:lower}, unconditionally but at an exponent of exponential length,
and at each exponent $\tau T$ it is at least as hard as every map on $n$
bits with at most $cn$ gates is at $T$, whether or not that map is
explicit.  So if any such map has a hard power at an explicit exponent
$T$ of polynomial length, the explicit map $W$ has one at the explicit
exponent $\tau T$, with only a logarithmic loss in the state width.
Lean checks the projection inequality, up to the two native constant
gates, as \texttt{prodMap\_power\_circuit} and
\texttt{prodMap\_power\_circuit\_left}.
\end{remark}

\subsection{Asymmetric iterators}\label{sec:asymmetric}

A permutation family can make all polynomially long iterations easy while
its inverse stays hard only under strong conditions.  For a permutation
$P$ let $\ell(x)$ be the length of the cycle of $x$, $c(P)$ the number of
distinct cycle lengths, and $\lambda(P)=\ord(P)$ the least common multiple
of the cycle lengths.

\begin{definition}
A family of permutations $P_n$ of $\bits^n$ with $\CC(P_n)=n^{O(1)}$ has an
\emph{asymmetric iterator} if $\CC(\Iter_{P_n,p(n)})=n^{O(1)}$ for every
polynomial $p$, while $\CC(P_n^{-1})$ is not bounded by any polynomial.
\end{definition}

\begin{proposition}[Necessary conditions]\label[proposition]{prop:asym-necessary}
If $P_n$ has an asymmetric iterator, then none of the following is bounded
by a polynomial in $n$: the circuit complexity of $x\mapsto\ell(x)$; the
number $c(P_n)$ of distinct cycle lengths; and $\log_2\lambda(P_n)$.
Every power $P_n^{T_n}$ whose complexity is not polynomially bounded,
for instance $P_n^{-1}=P_n^{\lambda-1}$, has an exponent whose bit length
is not polynomially bounded.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Since $\ell(x)-1<2^n$,
$P^{-1}(x)=\Iter_{P,n}(x,\ell(x)-1)$, so
$\CC(P^{-1})\leq\CC(\ell)+\CC(\Iter_{P,n})+O(n)$.  Let
$\ell_1<\cdots<\ell_c$ be the distinct cycle lengths.  A point $x$ satisfies
$P^{\ell_i}(x)=x$ exactly when $\ell(x)\mid\ell_i$, and $\ell(x)$ is itself
in the list, so
$\ell(x)=\min\{\ell_i:\Iter_{P,n+1}(x,\ell_i)=x\}$.  With the list
hardwired this gives
$\CC(\ell)\leq c\,(\CC(\Iter_{P,n+1})+O(n))+O(cn)$.  If
$\log_2\lambda\leq p(n)$, then
$P^{-1}(x)=\Iter_{P,p(n)}(x,\lambda-1)$ with $\lambda-1$ hardwired.
Finally, $P^T=\Iter_{P,m}(\cdot,T)$ with $T$ hardwired whenever $T$ has $m$
bits.  Each implication contradicts the assumption on $P^{-1}$ when the
quantity in question is polynomially bounded.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[Quantum inversion]\label[proposition]{prop:asym-quantum}
If $\Iter_{P,2n+1}$ has a circuit of size $q$, then $P^{-1}$ has a quantum
circuit of size $\poly(n,q)$ that is correct with probability at least
$2/3$ on every input.  If $P_n$ and $\Iter_{P_n,2n+1}$ are computable in
polynomial time, then $P_n^{-1}\in\mathsf{FBQP}$.  Hence a uniform family
with an asymmetric iterator would give $\mathsf{FBQP}\not\subseteq
\mathsf{FP/poly}$, and also $\mathsf{PSPACE}\not\subseteq\mathsf{P/poly}$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
The function $t\mapsto\Iter_{P,2n+1}(x,t)$ on $[0,2^{2n+1})$ is periodic with
least period $\ell(x)\leq2^n$ and injective on each period, so Shor's period
finding~\cite{Sho97}, or Kitaev's phase estimation~\cite{Kit95}, returns
$\ell(x)$ with probability $\Omega(1/\log n)$ using $\poly(n)$ queries.  Any
candidate $r$ with $1\leq r\leq2^n$ and $\Iter(x,r)=x$ is a multiple of
$\ell(x)$, so $y=\Iter(x,r-1)$ satisfies $P(y)=x$; the circuit checks
$P(y)=x$ and repeats $O(\log n)$ times.  For the last claim, $P_n^{-1}$ is
computable in polynomial space by iterating $P_n$, so its bit language lies
in $\mathsf{PSPACE}$ but, by assumption, not in $\mathsf{P/poly}$.
\end{proof}

So asymmetric iterators cannot be shown to exist unconditionally with
current techniques.  The natural candidates fail for a simple reason.

\begin{remark}[Fixed group elements]\label[remark]{rem:fixed-element}
Suppose $P(x)=g\cdot x$ for a group action given by a polynomial-size circuit
on encodings of group elements and points, with a fixed element $g$.  Then
$P^T(x)=g^T\cdot x$, and hardwiring an encoding of $g^T$ gives a
polynomial-size circuit for every power, including $P^{-1}$.  This covers
$x\mapsto ax\bmod M$, the RSA permutation $x\mapsto x^e\bmod N$ on
$(\mathbb Z/N)^\times$ with fixed $N$, where $g^T$ is the exponent
$e^T\bmod\lambda(N)$, Rabin squaring on the quadratic residues of a fixed
Blum integer, and the action of a fixed ideal class: all are symmetric
nonuniformly.  Making the modulus part of the state, as in the time-lock
puzzle of Rivest, Shamir and Wagner~\cite{RSW96}, removes the hardwired
group order, but then the iterator $x\mapsto x^{2^t}\bmod N$ and the inverse
are both believed hard without the factorization of $N$ and both easy with
it.  Lean checks the hardwiring step: given a circuit for an encoded group
action, \texttt{smul\_zpow\_circuit} gives a circuit for
$x\mapsto g^T\cdot x$ for every integer $T$ with at most two more gates, and
\texttt{smul\_iterate\_circuit} and \texttt{smul\_inverse\_circuit}
specialize it to $P^T$ and $P^{-1}$.  The examples are not mechanized.
\end{remark}

The generator must therefore be part of the input, in a group in which
multiplication is easy and inversion is not.

\begin{proposition}[Groups with infeasible inversion]\label[proposition]{prop:gii}
Let $\mathcal G_n$ be groups with injective encodings into $\bits^n$, such
that the set of encodings is recognized, and multiplication is computed,
by circuits of size $n^{O(1)}$, while the map $g\mapsto g^{-1}$ has no
polynomial-size circuits.  Then $P_n(g,x)=(g,gx)$ on pairs of encodings,
and the identity on all other strings, is a permutation family with an
asymmetric iterator, and $\ell(g,x)=\ord(g)$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Left multiplication by $g$ is a bijection of $\mathcal G_n$, so $P_n$ is a
permutation of $\bits^{2n}$.  For an $m$-bit $t$, square-and-multiply
computes $g^t$ with $O(m)$ multiplications, so
$\Iter_{P_n,m}((g,x),t)=(g,g^tx)$ has size $\poly(n,m)$.  With the encoding
of the identity $e$ hardwired, $P_n^{-1}(g,e)=(g,g^{-1})$, so a
polynomial-size circuit for $P_n^{-1}$ would invert group elements.
\end{proof}

Lean checks only the identity $P^t(g,x)=(g,g^tx)$ for any monoid action,
as \texttt{pairAction\_iterate}; the circuit bounds in this proof are prose.

Groups with infeasible inversion were introduced in the theses of
Hohenberger and of Molnar and used for directed transitive signatures and
broadcast encryption; Yamakawa, Yamada, Hanaoka and Kunihiro construct one
from indistinguishability obfuscation and factoring~\cite{YYHK14}, and
Altu\u{g} and Chen propose a candidate from isogenies over RSA
moduli~\cite{AC19}, which also surveys this history.  Whether either
candidate admits the injective encodings and recognizable encoding set
that \cref{prop:gii} needs is not settled here.  Any such group is broken
by a quantum computer, by order finding on $g$ followed by $g^{\ord(g)-1}$,
in accordance with \cref{prop:asym-quantum}.

\begin{remark}[No separation from a random power oracle]\label[remark]{rem:random-power-oracle}
Oracle separations need care with advice.  A uniformly random permutation
of $N$ points has $\ln N+O(1)$ cycles in expectation, hence $O(n)$
distinct cycle lengths with high probability.  Given the list of these
lengths as $O(n^2)$ bits of advice and the power oracle
$(t,y)\mapsto P^t(y)$, the spectrum test in the proof of
\cref{prop:asym-necessary} finds $\ell(x)$ with $O(n)$ queries, and one more
query returns $P^{\ell(x)-1}(x)=P^{-1}(x)$.  So a random permutation with
its power oracle separates iterating from inverting only for algorithms
without advice.  By \cref{prop:asym-necessary}, an oracle separation
against circuits needs a permutation with superpolynomially many distinct
cycle lengths.
\end{remark}

\section{Black-box lower bounds for fast-forwarding}\label{sec:blackbox}

The constructions of \cref{sec:fixed} use the transition only through
evaluations.  This section asks how far that use is optimal for oracle
algorithms that may store advice about the map.  If an algorithm must
handle every exponent, or the single exponent $\ord(P)-1$, then it inverts
the permutation $P$, and the inversion bounds of Yao and of De, Trevisan,
and Tulsiani~\cite{Yao90,DTT10} give $S\,t\geq\widetilde\Omega(\varepsilon N)$
in the notation below.  Here we treat one exponent fixed in advance, for
random maps and for random permutations.  The results are prose results.
Apart from the phase identities of \cref{lem:bb-phase}, which are checked
in Lean as \texttt{canonicalTime\_apply} and
\texttt{iterate\_eq\_canonical\_iff}, nothing in this section is mechanized.  Two lemmas about random
maps are used as explicit hypotheses; they are supported by heuristics and
by simulation but are not proved here.

Throughout this section $\log$ is to base $2$ and $\ln$ is natural.  The
letter $t$ denotes a number of oracle queries, not an input time, and $S$
denotes a number of advice bits, not an invariant set.

\subsection{Preprocessing algorithms and the upper bounds}

Let $V$ be a set of $N$ states and let $f$ be a random map on $V$, either
uniform among all $N^N$ maps or uniform among all $N!$ permutations.  An
\emph{$(S,t)$-algorithm} is a pair $(A_0,A_1)$.  The preprocessing stage
$A_0$ sees all of $f$ and outputs an advice string $z\in\bits^S$.  The
online stage $A_1$ receives $z$ and an input $x\in V$, makes at most $t$
adaptive oracle queries to $f$, and outputs a state.  It computes $f^T$
with success $\varepsilon$ if
\[
  \Pr_{f,x}\bigl[A_1^f(A_0(f),x)=f^T(x)\bigr]\geq\varepsilon,
\]
where $x$ is uniform and independent of $f$.  Preprocessing time is not
charged.  Randomness may be fixed by averaging, so we assume that both
stages are deterministic.  A query to $q$ returns $f(q)$; only forward
queries are allowed.

The construction of \cref{sec:fixed} is such an algorithm.  With $K=2L$,
the advice consists of the at most $N/L$ checkpoint records
$(h,z_h)$ and the residue table \cref{eq:residue-table}, so
$S=O((N/L+L)\log N)$.  The online stage walks $K$ steps, looks up the
first checkpoint, and walks at most $K$ more steps, so $t\leq 4L$.  Hence,
for every map and every exponent,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:bb-upper-perm}
  S\,t=O(N\log N)\qquad(t\leq\sqrt N).
\end{equation}
For maps whose images shrink, \cref{cor:image} first walks $d$ steps and
then uses checkpoints inside $\operatorname{im}(f^d)$.  This gives
$S=O((|\operatorname{im}(f^d)|/L+L)\log N)$ and $t\leq d+4L$.  For a
uniformly random map and each fixed $d$, Flajolet and
Odlyzko~\cite{FO90} show
$\mathbb E|\operatorname{im}(f^d)|/N\to\tau_d$ as $N\to\infty$, where
$\tau_0=1$, $\tau_{d+1}=1-e^{-\tau_d}$, and $\tau_d\sim 2/d$.  If the
profile $|\operatorname{im}(f^d)|=O(N/d)$ persists for $d$ growing with
$N$, which is a heuristic and not proved here, then $d=L=\floor{t/5}$
gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:bb-upper-map}
  S\,t^2=O(N\log N)\qquad(t\leq N^{1/3}),
\end{equation}
with balancing point $S=t=N^{1/3}$.  The question is whether
\cref{eq:bb-upper-perm,eq:bb-upper-map} are optimal for a fixed exponent.

\subsection{Phases and random-mapping lemmas}

We use the notation of \cref{sec:structure}: $\mu(x)$ is the transient
depth of $x$, $\ell(x)$ the length of its eventual cycle, and $c(x)$ the
least state on that cycle.  Let $\kappa(x)$ be the least $k\geq0$ with
$f^k(x)=c(x)$.  Then $\kappa(x)\leq\mu(x)+\ell(x)-1$, and
$\kappa(x)\equiv-\phi(x)\pmod{\ell(x)}$.

\begin{lemma}[Phase identities]\label[lemma]{lem:bb-phase}
Let $f$ be any map on a finite set.
\begin{enumerate}[label=\textup{(P\arabic*)}]
\item If $x$ is transient, then $\kappa(f(x))=\kappa(x)-1$.
\item If $T\geq\mu(x)$ and $a\geq0$, then $f^T(x)=f^a(c(x))$ if and only if
  $a+\kappa(x)\equiv T\pmod{\ell(x)}$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
(P1) The states $x$ and $f(x)$ have the same cycle.  Since $x$ is
transient, $x\neq c(x)$, so $\kappa(x)\geq1$ and $f^{k}(f(x))=c(x)$ holds
exactly when $f^{k+1}(x)=c(x)$.

(P2) Write $\ell=\ell(x)$ and $\kappa=\kappa(x)$, and choose $r$ with
$T+r\ell\geq\kappa$.  Since $T\geq\mu(x)$, the state $f^T(x)$ is cyclic,
so $f^T(x)=f^{T+r\ell}(x)=f^{T+r\ell-\kappa}(c(x))$.  The state $c(x)$ has
least period $\ell$, so $f^i(c(x))=f^j(c(x))$ if and only if
$i\equiv j\pmod\ell$.
\end{proof}

Thus a correct value $f^T(x)$ with $T\geq\mu(x)$ determines
$\kappa(x)\bmod\ell(x)$, and it carries no other information about $x$.
By (P1), $\kappa$ strictly decreases along every path to a cycle.

Now let $f$ be a uniformly random map on $V$.  Write
$\rho(x)=\mu(x)+\ell(x)$ for the number of distinct states on the
trajectory of $x$, and put $D_0=2\sqrt{N\ln N}$.

\begin{lemma}[Rho lengths]\label[lemma]{lem:bb-L0}
$\Pr[\max_x\rho(x)>D_0]\leq 1/N$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Let $k=\ceil{D_0}$.  For a fixed $x$, the event $\rho(x)>k$ says that
$x,f(x),\ldots,f^k(x)$ are distinct.  Each new value is uniform and
independent of the earlier ones, so this has probability
$\prod_{i=1}^k(1-i/N)\leq e^{-k(k+1)/(2N)}\leq N^{-2}$.  Take a union
bound over $x$.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Cycle length and depth of one state]\label[lemma]{lem:bb-L3}
For every state $x$ and every $m\geq0$,
\[
  \Pr[\ell(x)\leq m]\leq\frac{2m}{\sqrt N}
  \qquad\text{and}\qquad
  \Pr[\mu(x)<m]\leq\frac{2m}{\sqrt N}.
\]
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Let $K=\rho(x)$.  Conditioned on $K=k$, the first $k$ trajectory states
are distinct and $f^k(x)$ is uniform among them.  So $\mu(x)$ is uniform
on $\{0,\ldots,k-1\}$ and $\ell(x)=k-\mu(x)$ is uniform on
$\{1,\ldots,k\}$.  Both probabilities are therefore at most
$m\,\mathbb E[1/K]$.  Now $\Pr[K=k]=\Pr[K\geq k]\,k/N$, so
\[
  \mathbb E[1/K]=\frac1N\sum_{k\geq1}\Pr[K\geq k]=\frac{\mathbb E[K]}{N}.
\]
Finally $\mathbb E[K]=\sum_{k\geq1}\prod_{i<k}(1-i/N)\leq
1+\int_0^\infty e^{-u^2/(2N)}\,du=1+\sqrt{\pi N/2}\leq2\sqrt N$.
\end{proof}

The proof of \cref{thm:bb-map} also uses two statements that we do not
prove.

\begin{definition}[Random-mapping hypotheses]\label{def:bb-hyp}
Let $f$ be a uniformly random map on $N$ states.
\begin{enumerate}[label=\textup{(L\arabic*)},start=1]
\item There is an absolute constant $C_1$ such that, with probability at
  least $1-1/N$, every state $w$ and every $0\leq j\leq\sqrt N$ satisfy
  $|f^{-j}(w)|\leq C_1(j+1)\ln N$.
\item There is an absolute constant $C_2$ such that, with probability at
  least $1-1/N$, for every cycle and every $k\geq0$ at most
  $C_2\sqrt N\ln N$ states with that eventual cycle satisfy $\kappa(x)=k$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{definition}

Heuristically, the in-tree above a state is close to a critical Poisson
Galton--Watson tree.  Its generation $j$ survives with probability about
$2/j$ and then has size about $j$ times an exponential variable, which
suggests (L1).  A class of equal $\kappa$ takes one level from each tree
hanging on the cycle, which suggests (L2).  The script
\texttt{verify\_random\_mapping\_lemmas.py} checks (P1), (P2), and
\cref{lem:bb-L0} on random maps with $N=2^{12},2^{14},2^{16}$.  In these
runs, $\max_{w,\,j\leq64}|f^{-j}(w)|/((j+1)\ln N)$ is at most $0.93$ and
the largest class in (L2) has at most $1.5\sqrt N$ states.  Such
simulations test typical behaviour only, not the tail probability
$1/N$.

\subsection{Random maps}

\begin{theorem}[Random maps, assuming (L1) and (L2)]\label{thm:bb-map}
Assume (L1) and (L2).  There is an absolute constant $C$ with the
following property.  Let $f$ be a uniformly random map on $N$ states, let
$T\geq D_0$, $0<\varepsilon\leq1$, and $1\leq t\leq\varepsilon^2\sqrt N/1024$.
Suppose an $(S,t)$-algorithm computes $f^T$ with success $\varepsilon$.
Put
\[
  m_1=\floor{\frac{\varepsilon N}{C\,t^2\ln N}},\qquad
  m_2=\floor{\frac{\varepsilon N}{C\,t^3\ln N}},
\]
and
\begin{align*}
  \beta_1&=m_1\Bigl(\tfrac12\log N-2\log t-3\log\tfrac1\varepsilon
     -3\log\log N-C\Bigr),\\
  \beta_2&=m_2\Bigl(\log N-5\log t-\log\tfrac1\varepsilon
     -\log\log N-C\Bigr).
\end{align*}
Then $S\geq\min\{\beta_1,\beta_2\}-\log(8/\varepsilon)-3$.  If, on every
input $x$ with $\mu(x)>t$, the online stage does not query $f^t(x)$, then
$S\geq\beta_1-\log(8/\varepsilon)-3$.
\end{theorem}

The proof is an encoding argument in the style of Gennaro and Trevisan
and of De, Trevisan, and Tulsiani~\cite{GT00,DTT10}: a typical map is
described in fewer than $\log N^N$ bits by omitting some of its values,
which the decoder recovers with the help of the advice.

\begin{proof}
Let $C_1,C_2$ be the constants of (L1) and (L2), and put
$\ell_0=\varepsilon^2\sqrt N/512$.  The hypothesis on $t$ gives
$\varepsilon\geq32N^{-1/4}$ and $t+1\leq2t\leq\ell_0$.

\emph{Step 0: typical maps.}  Let $\mathcal F$ be the set of maps $f$ with
the following four properties.
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\alph*)]
\item The algorithm is correct on a set $G_f$ of at least $\varepsilon N/2$
  inputs.
\item The events of \cref{lem:bb-L0}, (L1), and (L2) hold.
\item At most $\varepsilon N/16$ states $x$ satisfy $\ell(x)<\ell_0$.
\item At most $\varepsilon N/16$ states $x$ satisfy $\mu(x)\leq t$.
\end{enumerate}
By averaging, (a) holds with probability at least $\varepsilon/2$.  Event
(b) fails with probability at most $3/N\leq\varepsilon/16$.  By
\cref{lem:bb-L3}, the expected numbers of states violating (c) and (d)
are at most $2\ell_0\sqrt N=\varepsilon^2N/256$, so by Markov's inequality
each of (c) and (d) fails with probability at most $\varepsilon/16$.
Hence $|\mathcal F|\geq(\varepsilon/4)N^N$.

Fix $f\in\mathcal F$ and $z=A_0(f)$.  Let $Q(x)$ be the set of states
queried by $A_1(z,x)$, so $|Q(x)|\leq t$.  Let
\[
  G'=\{x\in G_f:\ \ell(x)\geq\ell_0,\ \mu(x)>t\},
\]
so $|G'|\geq3\varepsilon N/8$.  For $x\in G'$ the states
$x,f(x),\ldots,f^t(x)$ are distinct and transient.  Split $G'$ into
$G_1=\{x:f^t(x)\notin Q(x)\}$ and $G_2=G'\setminus G_1$.  The map $f$ has
\emph{type~1} if $|G_1|\geq3\varepsilon N/16$, and \emph{type~2}
otherwise, in which case $|G_2|\geq3\varepsilon N/16$.  One type contains
a set $\mathcal F_i\subseteq\mathcal F$ of at least $(\varepsilon/8)N^N$
maps.  For each type we give an injective encoding of $\mathcal F_i$.  Its
length is then at least $N\log N-\log(8/\varepsilon)$.  Under the last
hypothesis of the theorem every map in $\mathcal F$ has type~1.

\emph{Step 1: selection for type~1.}  For $x\in G_1$ put $w(x)=f^t(x)$
and $\Pi(x)=\{x,f(x),\ldots,f^{t-1}(x)\}$.  By (L1) with $j=t$, at most
$C_1(t+1)\ln N\leq2C_1t\ln N$ inputs share a value $w(x)$.  So the set
$\mathcal C$ of these values has at least $3\varepsilon N/(32C_1t\ln N)$
elements.  For each $w\in\mathcal C$ fix one $x_w\in G_1$ with
$w(x_w)=w$.  Join $w\neq w'$ in $\mathcal C$ by an edge if
$w'\in Q(x_w)\cup\Pi(x_w)$ or $w\in Q(x_{w'})\cup\Pi(x_{w'})$.  Each $w$
names at most $2t$ others, so the average degree is at most $4t$.  By
Tur\'an's theorem in the form $\alpha\geq n/(\bar d+1)$, there is an
independent set of size at least
$|\mathcal C|/(4t+1)\geq3\varepsilon N/(160C_1t^2\ln N)$.  Taking
$C\geq54C_1$, it contains a set $W$ with exactly $m_1$ elements.  Put
$X=\{x_w:w\in W\}$.  For $w\in W$, neither $Q(x_w)$ nor $\Pi(x_w)$ meets
$W$: the state $w$ is excluded by type~1 and by distinctness of the
trajectory, and other members of $W$ by independence.

\emph{Step 2: encoding for type~1.}  The code consists of the following
parts.
\begin{enumerate}[label=(E\arabic*)]
\item The advice $z$, in $S$ bits.
\item The set $X$, as one of $\binom N{m_1}$ subsets.
\item The $N-m_1$ values of $f$ outside $W$, as one integer below
  $N^{N-m_1}$, in the following order.  First, for $x\in X$ in increasing
  order, simulate $A_1(z,x)$ and list $f(q)$ for each query $q$ whose value
  is not yet listed.  Second, for $x\in X$ in increasing order, list the
  values on $\Pi(x)$ not yet listed.  Third, list the remaining values
  outside $W$ in increasing order of the argument.
\item For each $w\in W$, a lap number $a_w\leq D_0/\ell_0$ and an index
  below $C_2\sqrt N\ln N$, all as one integer.
\end{enumerate}
The decoder reads (E1) and (E2) and replays the simulations.  Every query
lies outside $W$, so each needed value is either known or next in (E3).
The walks along $\Pi(x)$ also avoid $W$ and end at $w=f^t(x)$.  So the
decoder learns $W$ and the matching $x\leftrightarrow w$, and then the
third part of (E3).  It now knows $f$ outside $W$.

All states of $W$ are transient, so every cycle consists of known values;
the decoder knows all cycles, their lengths, and their least states.  Fix
$w\in W$ and $x=x_w$, and write $\ell=\ell(x)\geq\ell_0$.  The simulated
output is $f^T(x)$, because $x\in G_f$.  By \cref{lem:bb-L0},
$T\geq D_0\geq\mu(x)$, so $f^T(x)=f^a(c(x))$ for a known $a<\ell$, and
(P2) gives $\kappa(x)\equiv T-a\pmod\ell$.  The states
$x,\ldots,f^{t-1}(x)$ are transient, so (P1) gives $\kappa(w)=\kappa(x)-t$.
Since $\kappa(w)\leq\mu(w)+\ell-1<D_0+\ell$, the lap number
$a_w=\floor{\kappa(w)/\ell}$ is at most $D_0/\ell_0$, and with it the
decoder knows $\kappa(w)$ exactly.

The decoder now recovers $f$ on $W$ in increasing order of $\kappa$, with
ties broken by label.  Let $u=f(w)$.  By (P1), every transient state on the
path from $u$ to its cycle has $\kappa<\kappa(w)$, so every state of $W$ on
that path has already been recovered.  Let $U_w$ be the set of states $v$
whose path to a cycle is currently known, whose cycle is the cycle of $x$,
and which satisfy $\kappa(v)=\kappa(w)-1$.  Then $u\in U_w$ by (P1), and
$|U_w|\leq C_2\sqrt N\ln N$ by (L2).  The index in (E4) identifies $u$.
After the last step the decoder knows $f$, so the encoding is injective.

\emph{Step 3: counting for type~1.}  Using
$\log\binom N{m}\leq m\log(eN/m)$ and allowing three bits for rounding,
\[
  S+m_1\log\frac{eN}{m_1}+(N-m_1)\log N
  +m_1\log\Bigl(\bigl(\tfrac{D_0}{\ell_0}+1\bigr)C_2\sqrt N\ln N\Bigr)+3
  \geq N\log N-\log\frac8\varepsilon.
\]
Here $D_0/\ell_0+1\leq1025\sqrt{\ln N}/\varepsilon^2$ and, since
$m_1\geq\varepsilon N/(2Ct^2\ln N)$ whenever $m_1\geq1$,
$\log(eN/m_1)\leq2\log t+\log(1/\varepsilon)+\log\ln N+\log(2eC)$.
Rearranging gives $S\geq\beta_1-\log(8/\varepsilon)-3$, once $C$ also
satisfies $C\geq11+\log C_2+\log(2eC)$.  If $m_1=0$ the claim is trivial.

\emph{Step 4: type~2.}  For $x\in G_2$ the run queries $f^t(x)$ and at
most $t-1$ of the states $x,\ldots,f^{t-1}(x)$.  Let $j(x)$ be the largest
$j<t$ with $f^j(x)\notin Q(x)$, put $w(x)=f^{j(x)}(x)$, and let
$\Pi(x)=\{x,\ldots,f^{j(x)-1}(x)\}$.  By maximality, $f(w(x))\in Q(x)$:
the run queries $f(w)$ without querying $w$.  By (L1), at most
$\sum_{j<t}C_1(j+1)\ln N\leq C_1t^2\ln N$ inputs share a value $w(x)$.
The selection of Step~1 then yields an independent set of size at least
$3\varepsilon N/(80C_1t^3\ln N)\geq m_2$, and we fix $W$ and $X$ as before.
The code consists of (E1), (E2), (E3) with the new sets $\Pi(x)$, and,
for each $w\in W$, the number $j(x_w)<t$ and the position, among at most
$t$ queries of $A_1(z,x_w)$, of the query $f(w)$.  The decoder reads
$j(x)$ before walking along $\Pi(x)$; it then knows $w$, and the stated
position gives $f(w)$.  This part does not use correctness of the
outputs.  The counting gives
\[
  S\geq m_2\Bigl(\log N-\log\frac{eN}{m_2}-2\log t\Bigr)
  -\log\frac8\varepsilon-3\geq\beta_2-\log\frac8\varepsilon-3.\qedhere
\]
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}\label[corollary]{cor:bb-map}
Assume (L1) and (L2), and let $T\geq D_0$.  Fix $0<\delta\leq1/20$.  There
are $c>0$ and $N_0(\delta)$ such that, for $N\geq N_0(\delta)$ and
$\varepsilon\geq N^{-\delta/4}$, the following hold.
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\alph*)]
\item If $t\leq N^{1/5-\delta}$, then $S\,t^3\geq c\,\delta\,\varepsilon N$.
\item If $t\leq N^{1/4-\delta}$ and the online stage never queries
  $f^t(x)$ on an input $x$ with $\mu(x)>t$, then $S\,t^2\geq c\,\delta\,\varepsilon N$.
\item For constant $\varepsilon$ and $N\geq N_0(\varepsilon)$,
  $\max\{S,t\}\geq N^{1/5}/\log N$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
In each case $t\leq\varepsilon^2\sqrt N/1024$ for large $N$, and the
relevant bracket in \cref{thm:bb-map} is at least $\delta\log N$.  For
(a), the bracket of $\beta_2$ is at least
$(19\delta/4)\log N-O(\log\log N)$, and that of $\beta_1$ is at least
$(1/10+5\delta/4)\log N-O(\log\log N)$; also $m_2\leq m_1$ and
$m_2\geq\varepsilon N/(2Ct^3\ln N)$.  For (b), the bracket of $\beta_1$ is
at least $(5\delta/4)\log N-O(\log\log N)$.  For (c), if
$t\leq N^{1/5}/\log N$ then the bracket of $\beta_2$ is at least
$4\log\log N-O_\varepsilon(1)$ and $m_2\geq N^{2/5}$, while the bracket
of $\beta_1$ is $\Omega(\log N)$; so $S\geq N^{1/5}$.
\end{proof}

Part (b) covers the checkpoint algorithms of \cref{eq:bb-upper-map}: their
online stage walks to a checkpoint and then walks from a stored payload
far along the trajectory.  It queries $f^i(x)$ only for $i<t$ or
$i>T-t>t$.  For an input of depth greater than $t$, the state $f^t(x)$ is
transient and occurs only at time $t$, so it is not queried.  In the range
$t\leq N^{1/4-\delta}$, (b) matches
\cref{eq:bb-upper-map} up to a factor $O(\log N)$.  For general
algorithms, (a) loses a factor $t$.  The loss has two sources.  In
type~2 the omitted state $w(x)$ may sit at any of $t$ distances from $x$,
so up to $\Theta(t^2\log N)$ inputs can share it; and every selection loses
a factor $t$ to the conflict graph.  Distinct inputs genuinely share
omitted states: in the checkpoint algorithm all inputs below a checkpoint
share it, and there are only $O(N/t^2)$ checkpoints.

\begin{remark}[A barrier at $t=N^{1/4}$]\label[remark]{rem:bb-barrier}
The type-1 encoding recovers each omitted value from a correct value of
$f^T$.  By (P2), such a value carries only $\log\ell(x)\leq\frac12\log
N+O(\log\log N)$ bits about $f$ beyond what the decoder knows, while naming
$m$ omitted states costs about $m\log(N/m)$ bits.  An encoding of this
type therefore proves a positive bound only when $m\geq N^{1/2-o(1)}$.
For $t\geq N^{1/4+\delta}$, the advice of \cref{eq:bb-upper-map} is
$O(N^{1/2-2\delta}\log N)$, so no such encoding can be valid there, and
this method cannot reach the balancing point $N^{1/3}$.  This is an
observation about the method, not a lower bound.  Corrigan-Gibbs and
Kogan~\cite{CGK19} show that improving Yao's bound for function
inversion would give new lower bounds for depth-two circuits, and that
strong bounds for nonadaptive inversion would give lower bounds for
linear-size logarithmic-depth circuits.  We know of no analogous reduction
for fast-forwarding at $S\leq\sqrt N$.  The barrier above concerns the
proof technique, and it is not evidence that \cref{eq:bb-upper-map} is
optimal or not.
\end{remark}

The assumption $T\geq D_0$ makes every answer a cyclic state.  For
$t<T<\sqrt N$, most answers lie in trees, and we have no bound.

\subsection{Random permutations and a fixed exponent}

Now let $P$ be a uniformly random permutation of $V$ and fix
$T\geq0$, independent of $P$.  On a cycle of length $\ell$,
$P^T=P^{T\bmod\ell}$, so the hardness of $P^T$ depends on the divisors of
numbers near $T$.  For an integer $h\geq0$ put
\[
  \Lambda(T,h)=\{\ell\leq N:\ \ell\mid T-a\text{ for some integer }a
     \text{ with }|a|\leq h\}.
\]
If $T\leq h$ then $\Lambda(T,h)=\{1,\ldots,N\}$.  If $h<T\leq N^{O(1)}$, each
$T-a$ has $N^{o(1)}$ divisors, so $|\Lambda(T,h)|\leq(2h+1)N^{o(1)}$.  If
$T$ is uniform modulo $\lcm(1,\ldots,N)$, then
$\mathbb E|\Lambda(T,h)|\leq(2h+1)\sum_{\ell\leq N}1/\ell$.  On the other
hand $T=\lcm(1,\ldots,N)$ gives $P^T=\mathrm{id}$ and
$\Lambda=\{1,\ldots,N\}$.

The following class contains the algorithm of \cref{eq:bb-upper-perm}.
A \emph{path-following algorithm with one correction walk} with walk
length $t$ queries $x,P(x),\ldots,P^{t-1}(x)$, then chooses a state $y$ as
a function of $z$ and the values seen, queries $y,\ldots,P^{t'-1}(y)$ for
some $t'\leq t$, and outputs any function of everything seen.  Write
$R(x)=\{y,\ldots,P^{t'-1}(y)\}$ for the correction walk.  We require
$P^t(x)\notin R(x)$ for every $x$ whose cycle length is not in
$\Lambda(T,2t)$.  The checkpoint algorithm satisfies this: its correction
walk is $P^{[T-t+d,\,T-1]}(x)$ for a checkpoint at distance $d$, and it
contains $P^t(x)$ only if $\ell(x)\mid T-a$ with $t<a\leq2t$.

\begin{theorem}[Fixed powers of a random permutation]\label{thm:bb-perm}
Let $0<\varepsilon\leq1$ and $t\geq1$, and put
\[
  I=\ceil{16(t+1)/\varepsilon},\qquad h=2t+I+1,\qquad\sigma=2(t+I+1).
\]
Assume $|\Lambda(T,h)|+4\sigma\leq\varepsilon^2N/64$.  Suppose a
path-following algorithm with one correction walk of length $t$ and $S$
advice bits computes $P^T$ with success $\varepsilon$.  Then one of the
following holds.
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
\item With the same advice and $2t+I+1$ forward queries, some algorithm
  inverts $P$ at a uniform point with probability at least
  $\varepsilon^2/128$.  Consequently
  $S\,(2t+I+1)\geq\widetilde\Omega(\varepsilon^2N)$~\cite{Yao90,DTT10}.
\item $S\geq m\bigl(\log N-4\log t-7\log(1/\varepsilon)-44\bigr)
  -\log(8/\varepsilon)-3$, where
  $m=\floor{\varepsilon^2N/(3948\,t)}$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{theorem}

In particular, if $t\leq N^{1/4-\delta}$ and $\varepsilon\geq N^{-\delta/8}$,
then $S\,t\geq\widetilde\Omega(\delta\varepsilon^3N)$.  For constant
$\varepsilon$ this is the shape of \cref{eq:bb-upper-perm}.

\begin{proof}
Call a cycle \emph{bad} if its length lies in $\Lambda(T,h)$ or is at most
$4\sigma$.  A uniform permutation has, for each $\ell\leq N$, exactly one
state on cycles of length $\ell$ in expectation.  So the expected number
of states on bad cycles is at most $\varepsilon^2N/64$, and by Markov's
inequality it exceeds $\varepsilon N/16$ with probability at most
$\varepsilon/4$.  Let $\mathcal F$ be the set of permutations on which the
algorithm is correct on a set $G$ of at least $\varepsilon N/2$ inputs and
at most $\varepsilon N/16$ states lie on bad cycles.  Then
$|\mathcal F|\geq(\varepsilon/4)N!$.  Fix $P\in\mathcal F$, its advice $z$,
and let $G'\subseteq G$ be the inputs on good cycles, so
$|G'|\geq7\varepsilon N/16$.  Distances below are measured along cycles,
in either direction.

\emph{Step 1: inversion.}  Let $B$ be the set of $y\in G'$ whose
correction walk $R(y)$ contains $P^{-i}(y)$ for some $1\leq i\leq I$.
Suppose $|B|\geq\varepsilon N/16$ for at least half of $\mathcal F$.  On
challenge $y$, run $A_1(z,y)$ and then walk at most $I+1$ further steps
from the last state of $R(y)$.  If $y\in B$, the walk reaches $y$, and the
preceding state is $P^{-1}(y)$.  This inverter makes at most $2t+I+1$
queries and succeeds with probability at least
$(\varepsilon/8)(\varepsilon/16)$, which is case~(i).  Otherwise, let
$\mathcal F'$ be the set of at least $(\varepsilon/8)N!$ permutations in
$\mathcal F$ with $|B|<\varepsilon N/16$, and put $G_2=G'\setminus B$, so
$|G_2|\geq3\varepsilon N/8$.

\emph{Step 2: a correct state below.}  For $x\in G_2$ let $w(x)=P^t(x)$.
This map is injective, and $w(x)$ is not queried on input $x$: it is not
on the first walk because the cycle is longer than $t$, and it is not on
$R(x)$ by the class restriction.  Let $i(x)$ be the least $i\in[1,I]$ with
$P^{t+i}(x)\in G_2$.  If there is none, the window
$P^{[t+1,t+I]}(x)$ lies in a maximal run of at least $I$ consecutive
states outside $G_2$, and the window starts within $t+1$ steps after the
start of that run, because $x\in G_2$.  There are at most $N/I$ such runs,
so at most $(t+1)N/I\leq\varepsilon N/16$ inputs fail.  For the others put
$u'(x)=P^{t+i(x)}(x)\in G_2$ and
\[
  v(x)=P^T(u'(x))=P^{t+i(x)}\bigl(P^T(x)\bigr).
\]

\emph{Step 3: few wrong collisions.}  Let $\mathrm{wr}(v)$ be the number of
inputs $y$ with $A_1(z,y)=v\neq P^T(y)$.  At most $N/K$ states $v$ have
$\mathrm{wr}(v)>K$, and each $v$ equals $v(x)$ for at most $I$ inputs $x$.
With $K=16I/\varepsilon$, discarding the inputs with
$\mathrm{wr}(v(x))>K$ loses at most $\varepsilon N/16$ of them.  The
remaining values $w(x)$ form a set $\mathcal C$ with
$|\mathcal C|\geq\varepsilon N/4$; for $w\in\mathcal C$ write $x_w$,
$i_w$, $u'_w$, and $v_w$.

\emph{Step 4: selection.}  Choose greedily $W_1\subseteq\mathcal C$ with
pairwise distances greater than $\sigma$; then
$|W_1|\geq|\mathcal C|/(2\sigma+1)$.  For $w\in W_1$ the sets
$\Pi(x_w)=P^{[-t,-1]}(w)$, $P^{[1,i_w-1]}(w)$, and
$\Pi(u'_w)=P^{[i_w,i_w+t-1]}(w)$ lie within distance $\sigma$ of $w$ and
do not contain $w$, so they avoid $W_1$.  Join $w\neq w'$ if $w'$ lies in
$R(x_w)$, in $R(u'_w)$, or on the target walk
$P^{[0,t+i_w-1]}(P^T(x_w))$, or symmetrically.  Each of these sets is a
cycle interval of fewer than $\sigma$ states, so it contains at most one
state of $W_1$; the average degree is at most $6$, and Tur\'an's theorem
gives an independent set of size at least
$|W_1|/7\geq\varepsilon N/(28(2\sigma+1))$.  Since
$2\sigma+1\leq141t/\varepsilon$, this is at least
$\varepsilon^2N/(3948t)$, and we fix a subset $W$ with exactly $m$
elements.  The three conflict sets of $w$ do not contain $w$ itself:
$w\notin R(x_w)$ by the class restriction; $w\notin R(u'_w)$ because
$u'_w\notin B$; and $w$ on the target walk would give
$\ell(w)\mid T-a$ with $-I\leq a\leq t$, so $\ell(w)\in\Lambda(T,h)$.

\emph{Step 5: encoding.}  The code consists of $z$; the set
$X=\{x_w:w\in W\}$, as one of $\binom Nm$ subsets; the restriction of $P$
to $V\setminus W$, as an injection listed first in simulation order for
$X$ and then by argument, in $\log(N!/m!)$ bits; and, for each $w$, the
number $i_w$ and an index into
\[
  U_w=\bigl\{u'':\ P^{[0,i_w-2]}(u'')\text{ is known and }
     A_1(z,P^{i_w-1}(u''))\text{ can be simulated and equals }v_w\bigr\}.
\]
The simulations on $X$ avoid $W$ and reveal $W=\{P^t(x)\}$, so the decoder
can read the injection.  It obtains $v_w$ by walking $t+i_w$ steps from
the simulated output $P^T(x_w)$.  The true value $u=P(w)$ lies in $U_w$ by
Steps 2--4.  Distinct $u''\in U_w$ give distinct states $P^{i_w-1}(u'')$,
each with output $v_w$; at most one of them is correct, because $P^T$ is
injective.  So $|U_w|\leq1+\mathrm{wr}(v_w)\leq K+1$.

\emph{Step 6: counting.}  Injectivity on $\mathcal F'$ gives
\[
  S\geq\log m!-m\log\frac{eN}m-m\log\bigl(I(K+1)\bigr)
  -\log\frac8\varepsilon-3
  \geq m\Bigl(2\log m-\log N-3-\log\bigl(I(K+1)\bigr)\Bigr)
  -\log\frac8\varepsilon-3.
\]
Here $I\leq33t/\varepsilon$, so $I(K+1)\leq17I^2/\varepsilon\leq2^{15}t^2/\varepsilon^3$,
and $m\geq\varepsilon^2N/(7896t)$ whenever $m\geq1$.  Substituting gives
case~(ii).
\end{proof}

If the algorithm is correct on every input on a good cycle, the same
proof works with $I=1$ and $K=0$.  Inputs $x$ with $P^{t+1}(x)\notin G_2$
are then at most $N-|G_2|$, because $x\mapsto P^{t+1}(x)$ is a bijection,
and each $U_w$ has one element.  Under $|\Lambda(T,2t+2)|+8(t+2)\leq N/64$,
this gives either $S(2t+2)\geq\widetilde\Omega(N)$ or
\[
  S\geq m\bigl(\log N-2\log t-20\bigr)-5,\qquad m=\floor{N/(182t)}.
\]
So $S\,t\geq\widetilde\Omega(N)$ for $t\leq N^{1/2-\delta}$, the full range of
\cref{eq:bb-upper-perm}, and the second case gives
$S\,t\geq\Omega(\delta N\log N)$.  The checkpoint algorithm is of this
kind.

For general adaptive algorithms, the same scheme takes the first
unqueried state $P^{j(x)}(x)$ with $j(x)\leq t$ as the omitted state.  All
requirements become conflict edges, of out-degree $O(t+I)$, and the
inversion branch walks $I$ steps from each query.  A sketch along these
lines gives either $S\,t^2\geq\widetilde\Omega(\varepsilon^2N)$ by
inversion, or
$S\geq(\varepsilon^2N/(Ct^2))(\log N-8\log t-7\log(1/\varepsilon)-C)$,
hence $S\,t^2\geq\widetilde\Omega(\delta\varepsilon^2N)$ for
$t\leq N^{1/8-\delta}$.  We have not checked every inequality of this
sketch.

The divisor condition also controls a simpler model.  For integers $T$
and $N$ put
\[
  D_T=\max_{|a|<N}\bigl|\{\ell:\ |a|<\ell\leq N,\ \ell\mid T-a\}\bigr|.
\]

\begin{lemma}[Bit-fixing model]\label[lemma]{lem:bb-bitfix}
Suppose an adversary fixes $P$ on $p$ states, after which $P$ is uniform
among the permutations that extend these values.  Let $x$ be uniform and
independent of $P$.  If an algorithm makes $t$ forward queries, with
$p+t\leq N/2$, then it outputs $P^T(x)$ with probability at most
$(p+2t+2D_T)/N$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Let $Q$ be the partial injection formed by the fixed values and the query
answers, with $|Q|\leq p+t$.  Given $Q$ and $x$, the permutation $P$ is
uniform among extensions of $Q$.  The cycle of $x$ can be closed within
$Q$ in two ways.  Either $x$ lies on a cycle of the fixed values, which
has probability at most $p/N$; or some query $q$ returns the first state
of the path of $Q$ through $q$.  That answer is uniform over at least
$N/2$ unused states, so each query closes a cycle with probability at most
$2/N$.

Otherwise, contract each maximal path of $Q$, including single states,
to a node.  There are $M=N-|Q|\geq N/2$ nodes, and the extensions of $Q$
correspond to uniform permutations of the nodes.  The node cycle through
the node $C_0$ of $x$ can be sampled as a uniform ordering
$C_1,\ldots,C_{M-1}$ of the other nodes together with an independent
uniform $r\in\{1,\ldots,M\}$; the cycle is $C_0,C_1,\ldots,C_{r-1}$.  The
cycle length $\ell(r)=|C_0|+\cdots+|C_{r-1}|$ is strictly increasing in
$r$.  Fix the output $y$ and the ordering.  If $y$ lies in $C_0$ at
offset $a$ after $x$ or $b$ before $x$, then $P^T(x)=y$ means
$\ell(r)\mid T-a$ or $\ell(r)\mid T+b$, with $\ell(r)>a$ or $\ell(r)>b$.
If $y$ lies in $C_s$ with $s\geq1$, it is on the cycle only when $r>s$,
and then at a distance $a<\ell(r)$ from $x$ that does not depend on $r$;
again $\ell(r)\mid T-a$.  In each case at most $D_T$ values of $r$
qualify, so the probability is at most $D_T/M\leq2D_T/N$.
\end{proof}

With $p=0$ it bounds algorithms without advice.  The heuristic of
Coretti, Dodis, Guo, and Steinberger~\cite{CDGS18} replaces $S$ advice
bits and $t$ queries by about $p=S\,t$ fixed values.  Here it predicts
$S\,t\geq\varepsilon N-2D_T-2t$, matching \cref{eq:bb-upper-perm}.  Their
presampling theorem and its permutation version~\cite{CDG18} do not apply
directly, because they also charge the queries a challenger needs to
verify an answer, and verifying $y=P^T(x)$ may require walking the whole
cycle of $x$.

\subsection{Open problems}

The bounds above leave four questions.
\begin{enumerate}
\item For a fixed exponent with $D_T=N^{o(1)}$, for example a prime
  $T>N$, does every $(S,t)$-algorithm on a random permutation satisfy
  $S\,t\geq\widetilde\Omega(\varepsilon N)$, as for inversion?
  \Cref{thm:bb-perm} proves this for path-following algorithms with
  $t\leq N^{1/4-\delta}$ and constant $\varepsilon$, and \cref{lem:bb-bitfix} proves the analogue in
  the bit-fixing model.
\item For random maps and $T\geq D_0$, does every algorithm satisfy
  $S\,t^2\geq\widetilde\Omega(\varepsilon N)$ for all $t\leq N^{1/3}$?
  This would make the balancing point $N^{1/3}$ of \cref{eq:bb-upper-map}
  optimal.  \Cref{cor:bb-map} gives $S\,t^3$ in general, and $S\,t^2$ for
  algorithms that do not query $f^t(x)$, only for $t$ below $N^{1/5}$ and
  $N^{1/4}$.  Proving (L1) and (L2) is a smaller open task.
\item The bit-fixing heuristic with $p=S\,t$ predicts both conjectures.
  A presampling theorem that charges verification by the information it
  needs, about $\frac12\log N$ bits for a phase, rather than by its query
  cost would settle them in that form.  We know of none.
\item Multi-instance arguments, as used by Akshima, Guo, and
  Liu~\cite{AGL22} for Merkle--Damg\aa{}rd collisions, may avoid
  \cref{rem:bb-barrier}: bound the probability that $k$ independent
  challenges all succeed, then convert with $k$ of order $S$.
\end{enumerate}


\section{Lean formalization}\label{sec:lean}

The accompanying \texttt{lean/} project proves existence of actual Boolean
circuits with evaluation semantics and gate bounds.  It covers
\cref{tab:results}, the invariant-set, image-sensitive and active-mass
refinements, permutation inversion, complete-configuration simulation,
and the core matching-exponent lower bound.  Further checked components
include the exact number of distinct powers of a finite map
(\cref{lem:num-powers}), the counting step of \cref{prop:count-general},
and the semantic identities of
\cref{sec:spectrum} (but not their gate counts).  It also covers the reachable-set bounds for in-place machines given a step
circuit (\cref{sec:inplace}).  It also checks the in-forest duality
\cref{lem:forest-duality}, a combinatorial statement without circuits.
The simulation and hardwiring arguments of
\cref{sec:universal-map,sec:asymmetric} are checked as well.
The semantic lemmas also support arbitrary
state types with finite invariant subsets and supplied Boolean encodings;
initialization and decoding costs remain additional.

The development uses CSLib's circuits and simultaneous synthesis relation,
Mathlib's dynamics and arithmetic, and the author's Circuit library through
\texttt{complexitylib}, including its sorting and counting results
\cite{CSLib,Mathlib,Complexitylib}.  Its native basis consists of Boolean
constants, NOT, AND, and OR.  Every gate is charged, including constants;
wiring and fan-out are free.  This differs from the paper's basis by an
absolute factor and an additive constant.
\Cref{app:lean-details} states the native tradeoff and explains proof
differences.  The public theorem map, detailed constants, module guide,
pinned revisions, and build commands are maintained in \texttt{lean/README.md}.

The exported theorems have guarded axiom lists containing only
\texttt{propext}, \texttt{Classical.choice}, and \texttt{Quot.sound}, with no
\texttt{sorry} placeholders or additional axioms.  Classical choices supply
checkpoints, enumerations, and circuit witnesses; the result is nonuniform
existence.  The sparse chart's existence and circuit cost are checked, but
the edit-list compiler's algorithm and runtime, sparse-transition counting
application, and finite-field corollary remain prose results.  Only
the combinatorial core of \cref{sec:near-landau} is checked
(\cref{rem:near-landau-lean}).  In
\cref{sec:crypto}, Lean checks the exponent reduction for sequential
squaring modulo a fixed squarefree modulus, with $\lambda(M)$ taken as
the exponent of the unit group
(\texttt{SequentialSquaring.iterate\_square\_reduced} and
\texttt{pow\_eq\_pow\_of\_modEq}), and the exact powers of two in
\cref{tab:crypto} (\texttt{crypto\_table\_entries}).  The other
cryptographic statements and the literature discussions are outside the
mechanized boundary.  For \cref{sec:distinguished}, Lean checks the scan, lookup, correction,
period walk, cycle walk and output semantics under the checkpoint-hitting
hypothesis.  The period walk uses its own register.  The hash construction,
gate counts, width and reversible circuits remain prose results.
Kernel checking establishes neither historical priority nor
independent fidelity to every intended model convention.

\section{Conclusion and open questions}\label{sec:scope}

Sparse checkpoints and charged lookup give square-root state-space bounds
for arbitrary fixed iterates and functional-graph navigation.  The counting
example makes the exponent sharp for unrestricted fixed powers; it does not
establish sharpness for polynomial-width input times or exact program summaries.
The distance circuits have local coNP correctness checks, while
\cref{prop:seth-analysis} obstructs a general compiler with a fixed saving
in the state-width exponent under SETH.

In the black-box model, \cref{sec:blackbox} gives partial lower bounds
for a fixed exponent: matching ones for permutations within restricted
algorithm classes, and, under two random-mapping hypotheses, polynomial
ones for random maps.

Two directions remain: the additive bound
$s+\widetilde O(2^{n/2})$, and broader classes of transitions with cheaply
constructible orbit coordinates.  The sparse-counter compiler obtains these
from an explicit edit list; extracting that list from a transition circuit
is a separate task.

formulation, proof development, exposition, and literature searches.
The Lean development and the current revision also received assistance
from Codex, including circuit synthesis proofs, axiom auditing, and review.
Christian Reitwiessner contributed the manuscript extensions, Lean
formalizations, and validation programs integrated from repository pull
requests on October 17, 2026; those contributions disclose assistance
from Claude Code.  Codex assisted with their integration, the program-summary
and certificate discussion, bibliography reconciliation, and combined
validation.  The author field is provisional.  Historical priority and final
attribution require author review; the draft does not represent
independent peer verification.

\appendix
\section{Correspondence with the formalization}\label{app:lean-details}

The native circuit model charges constants as well as Boolean operations.
Its foundational statement is the following.

\begin{theorem}[Mechanized tradeoff]\label{thm:lean}
Let $c$ be a circuit over the Boolean constant, NOT, AND, and OR basis,
with $n$ inputs and $n$ outputs, and let $S\subseteq\bits^n$ be finite
and invariant under its evaluation $F$.  For all natural numbers $T$ and
$1\leq L\leq2^n$, Lean proves the existence of a circuit $d$ agreeing
with $F^T$ on $S$ such that
\[
 |d|\leq40000\left[L|c|+(n+1)^3
       \left(L+\floor{|S|/L}+1\right)\right].
\]
Here circuit size counts every gate, including constant gates.
\end{theorem}

The harmless $+1$s cover zero-width and empty-set cases.  Define
\[
 \widehat A=s+(n+1)^3,\qquad
 \widehat B=\widehat A+\sqrt{(n+1)^3 2^n\widehat A}.
\]
The checked real optimization gives the fixed-power bound
$120000\widehat B$, and a separate theorem derives the
$2^{\lceil n/2\rceil}\poly(n)$ consequence for polynomial-size updates.
The constants are deliberately loose accounting bounds.  Free wiring
and arbitrary fan-out permit sharing; constants are charged.
Translating the paper's fixed fan-in-two basis to this basis changes
size by at most an absolute factor and an additive constant.

The public theorem map in \texttt{lean/README.md} gives the remaining exact
constants and exported names.  Three implementation differences explain how
the formalized constructions realize the paper's asymptotic bounds.

\begin{enumerate}
\item For a single fixed power, one useful collision suffices.  The circuit
sorts checkpoint and prefix records with tagged padding, then selects an
adjacent equal-key pair witnessing either a checkpoint hit or a repeated
state.  Batches use the full shared dictionary lookup of \cref{lem:lookup}.
The repeated-state decoder adds the period to a hardwired residue until the
index lies in the repeated interval; its $O(Kn^2)$ cost, with $K=2L$, fits
the main budget.
\item Cycle labels are computed after projecting by $F^{N!}$, which reaches
the cycle and preserves its labels.  A short cyclic prefix either meets a
checkpoint or contains the whole cycle, allowing a scan for its minimum and
period.  This replaces the direct first-repetition argument in
\cref{thm:labels}.  The forest proof obtains ancestry intervals by ordering
root-to-vertex paths, without implementing a traversal algorithm.
\item Sparse-counter charts use at most $r+1$ ordinary linear intervals,
with hardwired canonical cycle labels, rather than compiling the weighted
arc permutation.  Encoding and decoding handle phase wraparound within
those intervals.  Both coordinate circuits and one streaming remainder
computation are charged.  This proves the claimed circuit existence without
verifying the edit-list compiler.
\end{enumerate}

Internal arithmetic uses spare bits to represent full-length cycles and
prevent overflow.  The checked first-hitting-time circuit returns $n+1$
time bits; its bound below $2^n$ on positive instances permits discarding
the top bit.  Visit descriptors use a success flag and two $(n+1)$-bit
fields, with period zero denoting a singleton.  Their all-time semantics
and bounded-count interpretation do not include an additional
deadline-processing circuit.

\section{Sparse edits of a primitive binary-field cycle}\label{app:binary-field}

\begin{corollary}\label[corollary]{cor:binary-field}
Represent $K=\mathbb F_{2^n}$ in a fixed polynomial basis and fix a
primitive element $\omega$.  If a permutation $F$ differs from
$L(x)=\omega x$ at $r$ states, then
\[
 \CC(F^T)\leq n^{O(\log n)}+O(n(r+1))
 \quad(T\in\mathbb Z).
\]
With an $m$-bit input time the bound is
$n^{O(\log n)}+O(n(r+m+1))$.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
Insert zero into the nonzero multiplicative cycle by setting
$G(0)=1$, $G(\omega^{-1})=0$, and $G(x)=\omega x$ elsewhere.
Then $G$ is one cycle, and $F$ differs from it at at most $r+2$ states.
Coordinates are
$\phi(0)=0$ and $\phi(\omega^j)=j+1$ for $0\leq j<2^n-1$.
Decoding uses finite-field exponentiation and costs $\poly(n)$ gates.
Encoding uses discrete logarithms.

Kleinjung and Wesolowski's Theorem~1.1~\cite{KW22} gives expected time
$n^{O(\log n)}$ for discrete logarithms in characteristic two.
To obtain exact nonuniform circuits, let $\tau(n)$ bound this expectation
for every input.  Truncate each trial at $2\tau(n)$ steps and verify
candidate logarithms by exponentiation.  A trial succeeds with probability
at least $1/2$.  Among $n+1$ independent random tapes, the probability that
some one of the fewer than $2^n$ nonzero inputs has no successful trial is
less than one.  Fix tapes that work for every input and select the first
verified answer.  Circuit simulation and verification have polynomial
overhead in $\tau(n)$ and $n$, preserving quasipolynomial size.
Apply \cref{cor:coordinates}.  The case of bounded $n$ is absorbed by
the constants.
\end{proof}

This corollary relies on the cited discrete-logarithm theorem and the
nonuniform simulation argument; those ingredients are not formalized in
our Lean development.  It concerns sparse permutation-preserving edits
to a primitive multiplicative cycle.  It does not imply the unrestricted
conjectural bound $s+\widetilde O(2^{n/2})$.
